Below I've assumed it landed on the standard route: **VCMI**, the open-source Heroes 3 engine, running the original **Shadow of Death / Complete** game files. That's the one setup that runs natively on your Apple Silicon Mac and also lets you build your own AI later.

I checked this Mac: it's arm64 on macOS 27 with Homebrew, and there's no Heroes, VCMI or GOG files on it yet. So you're starting from zero.

## Get the game running

1. **Buy the game files.** Get *Heroes of Might and Magic 3: Complete* on GOG (gog.com). Download the **offline backup installer for Windows**, which is a `setup_heroes_of_might_and_magic_3_complete_*.exe` plus a `.bin` file. VCMI needs the original data files. The Ubisoft "HD Edition" is a worse fit, and HotA isn't fully supported, so don't use either as your base.
2. **Install VCMI:**
   ```
   brew install --cask vcmi
   brew install innoextract
   ```
   If the cask doesn't exist, get the arm64 `.dmg` from github.com/vcmi/vcmi/releases instead. If macOS blocks the app from opening: `xattr -dr com.apple.quarantine /Applications/VCMI.app`
3. **Import the data.** Open VCMI. Its first-run wizard can import the GOG installer directly: point it at the `.exe` and keep the `.bin` in the same folder. If the import fails, extract the files yourself:
   ```
   innoextract -d ~/h3gog setup_heroes_of_might_and_magic_3_complete_*.exe
   ```
   Then copy `Data`, `Maps` and `Mp3` into `~/Library/Application Support/vcmi/`.
4. **Start a skirmish map** to confirm it all works.

## Later, for the AI project

You'll build VCMI from source (C++, CMake, Conan). The existing AIs are in `AI/`:
- `Nullkiller` plays the adventure map.
- `BattleAI` handles combat.

To add your own, you write a new AI that implements the same interface. If you want to train with reinforcement learning, look at **vcmi-gym**, an existing project that wraps VCMI combat as a Gym environment. It's a much better starting point than inventing your own setup.

Once you've bought the game, tell me where the installer files are. I can run steps 2–3 for you, and later set up the source build in `/Users/alon/projects/alpha_heroes`.

Weighing the significance of the achievement.
Weighing the significance of the achievement.
Honestly: high as a research milestone and for the Heroes community, modest commercially, and mostly indirect beyond that. Breaking it down:

Research: the biggest impact
No one has a superhuman AI in any grand-strategy or 4X-style game. CivRealm’s conclusion was that both RL and LLM agents fail at the full game. Being first would be a real milestone: long horizon, fog of war, randomness, huge structured action space, slow simulator, and solved at a sane budget rather than DeepMind scale. If the method (horizon curriculum, bootstrapped p(win), imitation start) is the story, that’s a top-venue paper and lasting citations. The caveat is that if it only works on one narrow template, the claim shrinks accordingly.

The Heroes community: the most visible
Think of what engines did to chess:

A non-cheating AI that’s actually hard is something single-player fans have wanted for 25 years.
A training partner and analysis tool for competitive players (“was attacking on day 9 right?”).
A live p(win) bar for streamed tournaments, which is a genuinely fun product, and it falls straight out of your value function.
The meta would shift as players study what the AI does.
Commercial: modest
You can’t sell anything Heroes-branded, and VCMI is GPL. The plausible value is indirect: strategy-game AI is famously weak (most cheat), so studios are a natural audience for the method, and it’s a strong credibility piece for Plumbus. That said, studios have historically been slow to adopt RL opponents because of cost, unpredictability, and difficulty tuning, and “superhuman” isn’t what makes a game fun. I wouldn’t plan around revenue.

Alignment: depends on the framing we discussed
A superhuman agent makes the instrumental convergence study much stronger, because you can show behavior along a capability axis rather than only at weak levels. It doesn’t change the core objection that conquest is built into the game.

Beyond games: be skeptical
Long-horizon planning under uncertainty is relevant to logistics and operations, but transfer from game AI to real-world systems has historically been modest. I’d claim it as motivation, not impact.

Net: worth doing as research and a community project, with the alignment angle as an upside rather than the main justification.

Is there a better game than heroes 3 for that?

Weighing conquest-based versus science-victory game options.
Depends on which goal. For a “first superhuman grand-strategy AI,” Heroes 3 is actually one of the best choices. For the alignment study, a Civ-like game would be stronger.

Why Heroes 3 is good for the AI milestone

An open engine (VCMI) plus existing ML infrastructure for battles.
1v1 competitive format, so “beat experts” is well-defined.
A large, active expert community to validate against. This is rarer than it sounds, and it’s what makes a superhuman claim credible.
Alternatives worth knowing

Freeciv (via CivRealm) or Unciv (open-source Civ clones). Big advantage: non-military victory conditions (science, culture) and diplomacy with multiple players. That directly answers the “conquest is baked in” objection: you can give an agent a science-victory goal and measure whether it goes to war anyway as the horizon grows. CivRealm also gives you a published baseline to compare against. Downsides: very long games, slow engines, and a small competitive scene, so “superhuman” is hard to prove.
Battle for Wesnoth. Open source, turn-based, active multiplayer, has an AI scripting API. More tactical than strategic, so it’s a smaller milestone.
Tribes (an open research clone of Polytopia). A small, fast 4X built for AI research. Great for cheap experiments, but no human expert scene.
Already done or close: StarCraft (AlphaStar), Diplomacy (Cicero), Stratego (DeepNash). Less room for a first.
Closed games (Civ, Paradox titles, Songs of Conquest): no engine access, so not viable.
My suggestion
Keep Heroes 3 as the main project, since it’s the better shot at a credible “superhuman” result. If the alignment angle becomes serious, either add environment knobs to Heroes (peaceful options, abundant resources, costly war) or run the convergence experiment in Unciv or Freeciv, where non-violent paths to victory exist natively. The horizon-curriculum method should transfer between them.

Is the data/code needed to replicate the civ results or improve them available online?

Searched the web, read a page
Yes, the code is all public. Trained models and datasets aren’t, and the project looks dormant.

What’s available

The environment. It installs with pip and needs Python 3.8+ and Docker (fine on your Mac), since it runs a Freeciv-web server.
The RL baseline, a separate repo with a PPO training script, a switch between mini-tasks and the full game, and TensorBoard logging.
The LLM baseline, with both agents from the paper (BaseLang and Mastaba). It needs an OpenAI or Azure API key, or a local LLM, plus a Pinecone key for memory.
What’s missing

No trained models or datasets: Hugging Face shows none linked to the paper. You’d retrain from scratch, which is cheap (see below).
Maintenance: last push in September 2024, 148 stars, 5 open issues. Expect some dependency rot with the Docker images.
Why it’s an easy bar to improve on
Their full-game RL agents were trained for only about 2 million steps, judged by average end-of-game score. That’s tiny by RL standards; vcmi-gym-scale training is orders of magnitude more. They also note the RL agents did well when rewards were immediate but struggled with sparse, delayed rewards needing long-term planning, which is exactly the problem your horizon-curriculum idea targets. So a well-engineered agent would very likely beat their numbers. The weaker part is what “beating” means: their metric is game score against built-in AIs, not human experts.

If you want to try the Civ side, I’d spend a day getting the Docker setup and the tensor baseline running on your Mac before deciding anything. Dependency rot is the main risk.

Sources:

bigai-ai/civrealm
civrealm-tensor-baseline
civrealm-llm-baseline
CivRealm paper (arXiv PDF)
CivRealm on Hugging Face Papers

Designing a game data model for tiles and entities.
Designing a game data model for tiles and entities.
Right, and that’s the standard pattern for it: the grid only holds cheap per-tile facts, and anything rich becomes its own entity token that carries its position. A hero isn’t a tile feature, it’s an object that happens to stand on a tile.

How a hero becomes one vector
Give heroes their own small encoder:

Scalars: level, XP, attack/defense/power/knowledge, mana, movement points left, luck, morale (log-scale the big ones).
Secondary skills: 28 skills × level (none/basic/advanced/expert), a fixed-size vector.
Spells known: about 70 bits.
Artifacts: one learned embedding per artifact ID, placed by slot; the backpack as an unordered set, pooled.
Army: 7 slots, each = creature embedding + log(count).
Hero identity/specialty: an embedding.
Run that through an MLP or a tiny transformer over the slots and you get one vector, say 256 dimensions, per hero. Towns get the same treatment (faction, buildings as bits, garrison as 7 creature slots, available recruits). So do map objects: mines with owner, dwellings with stock, guards (creature + count), artifacts and resources on the ground.

Then combine them
Two standard options, often both:

Transformer over entities: all heroes, towns, and objects as tokens, each with a position encoding (x, y, surface/underground). A small map has a few hundred tokens, which is cheap.
Scatter back to the grid: write each entity’s vector onto its tile, so a convolution over the map sees “a strong enemy hero is 3 tiles from my town.” AlphaStar did exactly this with units.
The trick that makes it data-efficient
Share the creature embedding everywhere: hero armies, town garrisons, wandering monsters, dwellings. And initialize it from the creature’s stat table (attack, defense, damage, HP, speed, cost, abilities) rather than learning IDs from scratch. Then the model knows a stack of 20 Archangels is huge even if it rarely saw them, and the same goes for artifacts and spells. MMAI already encodes battle stacks with their attributes in a similar spirit, so you can borrow from its code.

So the state is: a small grid tensor for terrain and paths, plus a variable-size set of richly encoded entities. That’s a very tractable input.

Comparing core counts across M4 chip variants.
Comparing core counts across M4 chip variants.
MacBook Pro M4 cores (CPU):

M4: 10 cores (4 performance + 6 efficiency)
M4 Pro: 12 or 14 cores (8 or 10 performance + 4 efficiency)
M4 Max: 14 or 16 cores (10 or 12 performance + 4 efficiency)
For parallel simulations, count mostly the performance cores. Efficiency cores help, but at maybe half the speed.

100 days, small map, 1 opponent: I can’t measure it here. VCMI won’t run without the Heroes data files, which you’d need to buy, so I can’t repeat the Freeciv experiment. My rough estimate:

Nullkiller’s slowness reported on the forums is for big maps with many AIs (a minute or two per day). On a small map with one opponent it should be far faster, maybe one to a few seconds per day including its battles.
So a 100-day game is plausibly 1 to 5 minutes per core, AI vs AI.
On an M4 Pro (10 performance cores), that’s very roughly 100 to 500 games an hour.
Treat that as a guess with a wide range. Two things could swing it: how often heroes fight (each battle adds cost), and VCMI’s client-server overhead per instance, including memory, since each parallel game is a separate process.

Once you have the game, this is a 15-minute benchmark: script one Nullkiller-vs-Nullkiller game on a small 1v1 map with 100 days, time it, then run several in parallel to see how it scales. That one number decides most of the project’s feasibility, so it’s worth doing first.

Given the current code, do you think we can make it much more efficient?

Ran 7 commands
Yes, probably by a good factor, though mostly by removing work rather than by clever optimization. I pulled today’s VCMI source and looked at the relevant parts. What I found:

Already in your favor

There’s a built-in headless AI-only mode (--headless, which implies all players are AI, plus --testmap to load a map directly). So no GUI cost, and scripting batch runs is easy.
Nullkiller logs elapsed time per internal method, so you get a profiler for free.
Where the time likely goes, and what to do

Nullkiller replans from scratch many times per turn. Each turn runs up to maxPass cycles of: recompute paths for all heroes, run every behavior (capture, defend, gather army, explore...), score all tasks, execute the best. The setting is 5 passes on the easiest difficulty, 10 on the next, 40 on the two hardest. It’s a config file (config/ai/nk2ai/nk2ai-settings.json), so you can trade strength for speed with no code changes: fewer passes, shorter path search limits, hero chaining off. For generating early data, a somewhat weaker but much faster teacher is a fine trade.

[2026-10-21 22:03] AI: Yes, we'll almost certainly change VCMI, but undoing turns isn't the main reason. You don't need undo to get training data. Self-play just runs games forward and records each state, action and outcome, which is how AlphaStar and OpenAI Five were trained.

**Where undo matters:**
- **Search-based training** (AlphaZero-style) needs to copy the game state, try moves on the copy, and throw it away. That's a choice of algorithm, not a requirement, and the plan from your earlier chat didn't depend on it.
- **In combat it mostly exists already.** VCMI's own battle AI already simulates moves on copies of the battle state. vcmi-gym also modified VCMI so battles can be stepped and reset as a training environment.
- **On the adventure map**, save/load gives you a crude version for free: you can start training games from any mid-game save to get more varied starting situations.

**The changes we'll actually need:**
1. **A control interface for your agent.** It needs to get the game state, pick an action and step the game forward, like vcmi-gym does for battles but extended to the adventure map. This is the core piece.
2. **Fix the headless crash** I hit earlier.
3. **Speed:**
   - run each game in one process instead of client and server talking over the network;
   - one thread per game;
   - optionally resolve battles with a learned predictor instead of playing them out.
4. **Reproducible runs:** fixed random seeds, and recording each game in a form training code can read.
5. **Copying the game state** for search, only if we choose search later.

Since vcmi-gym already did items 1 and 3 for battles, check its VCMI fork before forking vanilla VCMI: how far behind 1.7.5 it is, and whether it's maintained. Building on it could save weeks; if it's stale, fork current VCMI and borrow its ideas. I can compare the two as the first step of the source setup.

[2026-10-21 22:03] USER: will have have to change the game itself while creating an AI that can be plugged into the non changed game?

[2026-10-21 22:04] AI: No. Make the changes only in a training copy of VCMI, and ship the AI as a plugin for normal, unchanged VCMI.

One clarification first: "the game" here means VCMI. The original `Heroes3.exe` from GOG isn't involved at all; VCMI only reads its data files. Plugging an AI into the original exe would need binary hooking, which is a different, much uglier project. Not recommended.

**How the two-builds plan works:**

- **VCMI already loads AIs as plugins.** Nullkiller and BattleAI are separate libraries (e.g. `libNullkiller.dylib`) that you choose per player in the launcher. Your AI would be another one: a C++ library that implements the same interface and runs your trained network inside it.
- **Training build:** a modified copy of VCMI that's headless, single-process and fast, with the control interface for your agent and shortcuts like auto-resolved battles.
- **Release build:** your AI plugin, compiled against stock VCMI, which anyone can drop into a normal install.

**Rules that keep this working:**
1. **The AI may only see what the plugin interface exposes.** That's the player's own view, with fog of war. Compute the agent's inputs from that same interface during training, not from internal game state your training build can reach. Otherwise the shipped AI won't have the inputs it learned on.
2. **No shortcuts the real game lacks.** If training auto-resolves battles, the shipped AI still has to fight real battles, using BattleAI or your own battle model. Regularly test the agent on stock VCMI to catch gaps between training and the real game.
3. **Match the VCMI version.** The plugin has to be built against the exact VCMI version it runs in, so a new VCMI release means a rebuild.

**Practical setup:** keep your agent code in its own folder in the repo, and keep engine changes as a small, separate set of patches on top of VCMI. Each patch is then clearly either training-only or something worth offering back to VCMI, like the headless crash fix.

[2026-10-21 22:06] AI: Right. You're playing in VCMI; the original game isn't installed.

- **What we took from GOG:** only the data files (`Data`, `Maps`, `Mp3`: graphics, sounds, maps, unit tables) in `~/Library/Application Support/vcmi/`. VCMI is a from-scratch rewrite of the engine that reads those files.
- **The original `Heroes3.exe`** is sitting in the leftover `~/h3gog` folder, but it's a Windows program. It can't run on your Mac without Wine or CrossOver, and nothing in the project needs it.

One thing to keep in mind: VCMI copies the original's rules closely but not perfectly, with some small differences in mechanics and AI. For the project that's fine, since training and testing both happen in VCMI. If you ever compare against human players, use the VCMI multiplayer scene or HotA players playing in VCMI. Results from the original game or HotA's own engine won't match exactly.

[2026-10-21 22:30] AI: The monsters were controlled by **BattleAI**, VCMI's hand-written battle AI, not the trained model.

**Why:**
- VCMI's settings choose the battle AI separately for monsters, enemy players and allied players. All three default to `BattleAI`, and your settings file doesn't change them.
- MMAI, the trained neural-network AI, is built into our copy of the game but has no model files yet. It loads them from a separate **MMAI mod**, which isn't installed. Without it, MMAI just falls back to BattleAI anyway.

**What BattleAI actually is:** not a list of fixed if-then rules. It's a hand-coded search: for each possible move, it predicts the damage dealt and taken and picks the best one. It's the strongest AI VCMI ships by default. The "if EnemySpeed is LOW..." rules you saw scrolling past earlier belong to Nullkiller, the map AI, which uses them to estimate how dangerous a fight is.

**To fight the trained model:**
1. Open the launcher with `scripts/vcmi.sh launcher` → **Mods** tab, then find and install the MMAI mod (it contains the trained models).
2. In the launcher's AI settings, set the battle AI for monsters (and enemies, if you like) to **MMAI**.

[2026-10-21 22:39] USER: ok, that seems to have worked. now, lets assume I want to have a new very dumb AI that only goes up, for every move it chooses the tile that's the closest to the upper left corner. How would that work? do I need to create an onnx model or what? what should I create for it and how will it be consumed?

[2026-10-21 22:39] AI: No ONNX needed. ONNX is only how MMAI stores its neural network; a rule like yours is plain code. Let me check VCMI's AI plugin interface so the details are right.

[2026-10-21 22:40] TOOLS: Bash×3

[2026-10-21 22:40] AI: You don't need ONNX for this. ONNX is just a file format for a trained neural network, the way MMAI ships its model. A hand-written rule like "go toward the top-left" is ordinary code: a small **C++ plugin library** that VCMI loads the same way it loads Nullkiller or BattleAI.

## What you'd create

A folder, say `UpLeftAI/`, modelled on `vcmi/AI/EmptyAI`, which is about 150 lines and does nothing except end its turn:

1. **The AI class**, built on VCMI's `CAdventureAI`. The main function is `yourTurn()`, which VCMI calls when it's your player's turn:
   - for each of your heroes (`cb->getHeroesInfo()`), work out which tiles it can reach this turn using VCMI's pathfinder;
   - pick the reachable tile with the smallest `x + y`, i.e. the one closest to the top-left corner;
   - move there with `cb->moveHero(hero, tile, false)`;
   - repeat until no hero can move closer, then `cb->endTurn()`.
2. **Answers to every question the game asks.** Level-up skill choices, "fight or flee?", treasure chests, garrisons, teleporters: each is a function call to the AI, and **the game waits until it answers**. EmptyAI shows the pattern: pick option 0 for everything.
3. **Battles: hand them off.** Through `getBattleAIName()` you return `"BattleAI"`, and VCMI uses the existing battle AI whenever your hero fights. Your code only handles the map.
4. **Three exported functions** in `main.cpp`: name, version and "create an instance". VCMI calls these when it loads the library.
5. **A `CMakeLists.txt`** so the build produces `build/bin/AI/libUpLeftAI.dylib`.

## How VCMI uses it

- **When it loads:** at game start, VCMI loads the library for each AI player by name and calls `GetNewAI()` to create one AI per player.
- **Choosing it per run:** for test games, pass it on the command line: `vcmi.sh client --headless --testmap ... --ai UpLeftAI`.
- **Choosing it in the launcher:** add its name to the allowed AI list in `vcmi/config/schemas/settings.json`, since that list is fixed. Then it shows up as an option.

## Expected behaviour

It will walk into corners and lakes and get stuck, which is fine for a test. The useful part is that it proves the whole path works: your code makes decisions, the game carries them out, and you can watch it in the spectator view. Your real AI will reuse this exact structure. The only difference is that `yourTurn()` will turn the game state into numbers, run the network (an ONNX model, via onnxruntime like MMAI does), and turn its output into moves. Training happens separately, in Python.

**Where to put it:** in `alpha_heroes/agents/UpLeftAI/`, outside the `vcmi` code, with a single line in VCMI's build file pointing to it. That keeps our VCMI changes small and keeps your AI code clearly yours.

Want me to build it and run a headless game with it against Nullkiller?

[2026-10-21 23:01] USER: now tell me how  it works did you make a cpp file that compiles? what is the input that the AI gets from the game engine and what output is it expected to give?

[2026-10-21 23:01] AI: Yes. It's a small C++ library that compiles to `build/bin/AI/libUpLeftAI.dylib`. There's no model and no data file; the game loads the library and calls functions in it.

## The files (`agents/UpLeftAI/`)

| File | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `UpLeftAI.h` / `UpLeftAI.cpp` | The AI class, about 70 lines of logic |
| `main.cpp` | 3 exported functions VCMI looks for when loading: `GetAiName`, `GetGlobalAiVersion`, `GetNewBattleAI` (creates an instance) |
| `CMakeLists.txt` | Builds the `.dylib` into VCMI's `AI/` folder |
| `StdInc.h` | Standard VCMI header boilerplate |

## How it gets used

1. A battle starts. VCMI reads your settings, sees `UpLeftAI`, loads `libUpLeftAI.dylib` and calls `GetNewBattleAI()` to create an AI for that battle.
2. It calls `initBattleInterface(env, cb)`. The AI keeps `cb`, the **callback**: its handle for asking the game questions and sending commands.
3. Every time one of its units has its turn, the game calls `activeStack(battleID, stack)`.
4. The AI must answer with **exactly one action**. If it doesn't, the battle waits forever.

## Input: not a data array, but questions the AI asks

The game doesn't hand the AI a snapshot. It says "it's this unit's turn" (`stack` is a pointer to the unit), and the AI looks up whatever it needs through `cb->getBattle(battleID)`:

- `battleGetStacks(ONLY_ENEMY)`: the enemy units, each with creature type, count, HP, position, abilities and so on.
- `battleGetAvailableHexes(stack)`: the hexes this unit can reach this turn.
- `CStack::isMeleeAttackPossible(stack, enemy, hex)`: whether it can hit that enemy from that hex.
- Plus many more (shooting, spells, damage estimates, obstacles, walls), which UpLeftAI doesn't use.

Everything goes through the player's view of the game, so the AI can't see anything a human player in that seat couldn't.

## Output: one `BattleAction`

The AI sends it with `cb->battleMakeUnitAction(battleID, action)`. UpLeftAI uses three kinds:
- `makeMeleeAttack(stack, enemyPos, fromHex)`: hit an adjacent enemy;
- `makeMove(stack, hex)`: walk to the top-left-most reachable hex;
- `makeDefend(stack)`: stay put.

Others exist: shoot, wait, cast a spell, retreat and so on. The action goes to the game server, which checks it's legal and applies it. Then the next unit's turn comes up, which may be another call to us.

There's also `yourTacticPhase()`, for battles that start with a positioning phase; we just skip it.

## How a trained AI would fit the same structure

Only the inside of `activeStack` changes:

1. **Encode:** query the battle state through `cb` as above and pack it into a tensor (units × features, hex grid).
2. **Infer:** run the ONNX model on it with onnxruntime.
3. **Decode:** turn the model's chosen action number back into a `BattleAction` and send it.

That's exactly how MMAI works, so its code (`vcmi/AI/MMAI/`) is the reference for steps 1 and 3. Training happens outside the game, in Python with vcmi-gym, and the result is exported to an `.onnx` file that this C++ code loads.

[2026-10-21 23:04] USER: when asked for an action what input the AI gets from the engine? the board state? the diff? 
does the AI class get events like enemy hero casts bless on angels and then another event of angels move to tile 1,2?

[2026-10-21 23:04] TOOLS: Bash

[2026-10-21 23:04] AI: Both, in a sense. The AI always has the **full current board** available to look up, and it **also** receives an event for each thing that happens, though it's free to ignore them.

## When asked for an action: no data, just "your turn"

`activeStack(battleID, stack)` passes only which battle and which unit is acting. There's no snapshot and no diff.

The game keeps its own copy of the battle state, always up to date, and the AI reads that copy live through `cb`: all units, their counts, HP, positions, active spell effects like Bless, obstacles, walls, turn order and so on. So at decision time the AI sees the **complete current state as its player is allowed to see it**. It doesn't have to rebuild anything from history; the game has already applied every change.

## Events: yes, exactly like your example

The AI class can receive a callback for each change. Every one is optional, and UpLeftAI ignores them all. For your example:

- The enemy hero casts Bless → `battleSpellCast(...)` (who cast what, and on whom), then `battleStacksEffectsSet(...)` (Bless applied to the Angels).
- The Angels move → `battleStackMoved(battleID, stack, path, distance, teleport)`.
- Then `actionFinished(...)`.

The full list:

| Event | Fires when |
|---|---|
| `battleStart` / `battleEnd` | battle begins / ends (with the result) |
| `battleNewRound` | a new round starts |
| `actionStarted` / `actionFinished` | before / after any unit's or hero's action |
| `battleStackMoved` | a unit moves (with its path) |
| `battleAttack`, `battleStacksAttacked` | an attack happens; damage and kills are applied |
| `battleSpellCast`, `battleStacksEffectsSet` | a spell is cast; effects like Bless/Slow are added or removed |
| `battleTriggerEffect` | one-shot effects (regeneration, morale, …) |
| `battleUnitsChanged` | units added, removed or changed (summons, resurrection, deaths) |
| `battleObstaclesChanged`, `battleCatapultAttacked`, `battleGateStateChanged` | battlefield changes (fire walls, walls, gates) |
| `battleLogMessage` | the text battle log |

The same events go to both sides, so you also hear about the enemy's actions.

**Timing catch:** some events fire **before** the change is applied to the game's copy, and some after. For example, `battleStackMoved` arrives first, so the GUI can animate the move from the old position. If you ever read the state inside an event, check which kind it is.

## What this means for your AI

- **A simple or learned AI** can ignore events and just read the current state on each `activeStack`. That's what MMAI does: it encodes the current board into a fixed set of numbers every turn.
- **Events are useful for things the current state doesn't show:** history, e.g. "the enemy hero already cast a spell this round", or what an enemy did last turn. Use them to build extra features for the model alongside the board.

[2026-10-21 23:06] AI: Interesting: even BattleAI skips the tactics phase. Checking MMAI and how the server runs the phase:

[2026-10-21 23:07] TOOLS: Bash×4

[2026-10-21 23:07] AI: Tactics is its own phase at the **very start of the battle, before round 1**, and it goes to the AI through a separate function.

## When it happens and who gets it

- Only **one side** gets it: the one whose Tactics skill beats the other's. The server works this out when the battle is created.
- Number of columns you can place units in = **1 + your Tactics value − the enemy's Tactics value**. The values are Basic 2, Advanced 4, Expert 6.
  - Expert against no Tactics: 7 columns.
  - Expert against Basic: 5 columns.
  - Equal levels cancel out: nobody gets the phase.
- Some battle types don't allow tactics at all.

## How the AI plays it

1. The battle starts (`battleStart` event).
2. If your side has tactics, the game calls **`yourTacticPhase(battleID, distance)`**. `distance` is the number of columns you're allowed to use.
3. The AI sends as many moves as it likes, with `cb->battleMakeTacticAction(battleID, BattleAction::makeMove(unit, hex))`:
   - only its own units;
   - only into hexes inside the allowed columns;
   - no movement-range limit, any order, and the same unit can be moved again.
4. It finishes with `makeEndOFTacticPhase(side)`. **This is required**; if the AI never sends it, the battle waits forever.
5. Round 1 starts and the normal `activeStack` calls begin.

The other side gets nothing during this phase except `battleStackMoved` events for each repositioned enemy unit.

## No current AI uses it

BattleAI, MMAI, StupidAI and our UpLeftAI all just end the phase immediately; it's one line in each. So with VCMI's AIs, Tactics is a dead skill: a hero with Expert Tactics gets no benefit.

That's a cheap edge for your AI later, because it's a one-off placement decision before the fight. Even something simple helps, like moving slow units forward or tucking shooters behind blockers. For a learned model it's a separate decision type with its own action space: pick a unit and a hex within the allowed columns, or "done".

[2026-10-21 23:10] AI: The trained combat AI is **MMAI**. You have the overall shape right, but two parts are off: it mostly encodes the current board, not the history, and it can't pick every possible action.

## Input: mostly a snapshot, plus a little history

At each decision MMAI encodes the **current** battlefield into one flat array of numbers:
- **Global:** which side it's on, whose turn it is, total army value at the start of the battle and now, and so on.
- **Each player:** army stats for both sides.
- **Each of the 165 hexes:** terrain (passable, moat, quicksand, fire wall, …) plus the unit standing there, if any, with its attributes: creature type, count, HP, attack, speed, position in the turn order, and so on.

The only history it keeps comes from one event, `battleStacksAttacked`. From that it keeps a running log of damage dealt and taken and units killed, and folds the totals into the per-unit and per-player numbers. It doesn't keep a raw list of past events. A spell cast or a move only matters through what it changed on the board (e.g. Bless shows up in the affected unit's attributes).

## Output: one choice out of 2,312

The model picks one number, which maps to one of:
- **2 global actions:** retreat, wait.
- **14 actions per hex × 165 hexes:**
  - move there (or defend, if it's the unit's own hex);
  - shoot at the unit there;
  - **move there and attack in one of 12 directions**. There are 12 because two-hex units have more neighbouring hexes.

A **mask** blocks every action that's illegal right now, so it only chooses among valid ones. The choice is then turned back into a VCMI `BattleAction` and sent with `battleMakeUnitAction`, the same call UpLeftAI uses.

## What the model can't choose

- **Spells:** not in the 2,312 actions at all. Hero spellcasting is off by default (`enableSpellsUsage = false`). When it's turned on, MMAI hands the decision to BattleAI's hand-written spell logic rather than the model.
- **War machines:** the catapult, First Aid Tent and others follow fixed rules.
- **Tactics phase:** skipped, like every other VCMI AI.

So it's a learned policy for **unit movement and attacks only**. Spells, war machines and tactics are handled by fixed code or skipped. For your project, spells are the obvious gap: hero magic decides a lot of real Heroes 3 battles.

[2026-10-21 23:15] AI: Mostly right, but "unit spell casting" needs a check: some creatures cast spells (Genies, Ogre Magi, Faerie Dragons, Archangels resurrecting). Checking how MMAI handles those and each war machine:

[2026-10-21 23:15] TOOLS: Bash×2

[2026-10-21 23:15] AI: You're right on all three, with a small correction about war machines.

## What the 2,312 actions are

**2 global actions:**
- **Wait**
- **Retreat**

**14 actions for each of the 165 hexes** (165 × 14 = 2,310):
- **Move** to that hex. Choosing the unit's own hex means **Defend**.
- **Shoot** at the unit on that hex.
- **Move there and melee-attack in one of 12 directions.** A one-hex target has 6 neighbouring hexes; a two-hex target has 12. The direction is part of the choice because it decides where your unit ends up, and which enemies it can be hit by next.

That's the whole list: movement, melee, shooting, waiting, defending and retreating.

## What's not in it

- **Hero spells:** correct, not in the model at all. They're off by default. If you turn them on, BattleAI's hand-written logic picks the spell and the model is skipped for that turn.
- **Creature spells:** correct, not in it either. Genies, Ogre Magi, Faerie Dragons and Archangels never use their spell abilities under MMAI; they only move and attack. The code even says that during training they made creature spellcasts impossible by fighting on cursed ground. If such an action ever appears, MMAI just logs a warning and doesn't record it.
- **War machines:** mostly, but not all. The **Catapult, First Aid Tent and Arrow Towers** are controlled by fixed rules:
  - Catapult: hit the gate, then the walls.
  - Tent: heal the most damaged unit.
  - Towers: shoot the most valuable enemy.

  The **Ballista** isn't in that list, so it appears to go through the model like a normal shooter, choosing a **Shoot** action. I haven't confirmed that by running it.
- **Surrender:** not available, only retreat.
- **Tactics phase:** skipped.

So for your own battle AI, the actions MMAI is missing are hero spells, creature spells, smarter war-machine control, surrender and tactics placement. Hero spells matter most by far.

[2026-10-21 23:30] AI: Your idea is right, and MMAI already does a version of it, which means part of my earlier explanation was wrong. Here's what MMAI really looks like, what I'd change, and how it was trained.

## Correction: what MMAI actually does

- **Output: already factorized.** The network outputs a main action out of 4 (WAIT, MOVE, MOVE-AND-ATTACK, SHOOT), a hex out of 165 and a second hex out of 165. A lookup table then converts that triplet into one of the game's 2,312 action numbers. So the 2,312 list is the game environment's action list, not what the network chooses from. Retreat isn't one of the network's options.
  - **Known flaw:** the two hex outputs are computed assuming the network's *top* main action. If you sampled a different main action, the hex probabilities wouldn't match it. To avoid that, the code forces sampling temperature to 0, so in-game MMAI always plays its single best guess.
- **Input: large and mostly wasted.** It's 28,114 floats plus a graph:
  - 165 hexes × 170 floats each. About 140 of those 170 floats are the attributes of the unit standing on the hex, so they're zeros on empty hexes and duplicated on both hexes of a two-hex unit.
  - Coordinates, speed and turn-order position are spelled out with one slot per possible value.
  - On top of that, 7 types of graph connections the engine pre-computes: can reach, adjacent, "attacking this unit would deal X% of its HP", retaliation damage, ranged penalty, and so on.

  Those engine-computed damage features are a genuinely good idea and worth keeping. The rest isn't efficient.

## A pushback on the loss argument

Choosing among 2,310 options isn't what makes learning hard. With masking, the training loss only uses the chosen action's probability. AlphaZero's chess network chooses among 4,672 moves without trouble. What actually matters:

1. **Shared weights:** each option's score should come from the same small network applied to that hex or unit, not from one separate output neuron per option.
2. **Masking out illegal options.**
3. **Conditional, not independent, outputs:** two independent outputs, "which hex" and "which direction", would be **wrong**. The network couldn't express "attack the Angels from hex A but never from B", and combining them would produce illegal pairs. You need p(direction | hex). That's exactly MMAI's flaw above.

Factorizing really pays off once **spells** are added: spell × target × second target, e.g. Teleport, is too many combinations to list flat.

## My proposal: input (current state only)

Build it from **entities**, not hexes, with fixed maximum counts and padding plus masks, so it exports cleanly to ONNX:

| Part | Count | Per item | Contents |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Units** | up to 32 tokens | ~90 floats + 2 IDs | creature ID (learned embedding), side, is-active-unit, hex position (ID), count (log scale), top-unit HP fraction, total HP; current attack/defence/damage/speed after spells; shots left, retaliations left; morale, luck; moved/waited/defending flags; ability flags (~25: flying, no retaliation, double attack…); active spell effects (~35); position in turn order; caster info; **engine-computed "if the active unit attacks this one": expected damage %, kills %, retaliation %, ranged penalty** |
| **Heroes** | 2 | ~100 | primary skills, mana, can cast this round, magic school levels, battle-relevant secondary skills, and our own spellbook. The enemy's spellbook is hidden from a real player, so leave it out. |
| **Hex grid** | 165 | ~10 | passable, obstacle, moat, quicksand, fire wall, force field, our land mines, walls/gate, reachable by the active unit, movement cost |
| **Global** | 1 | ~20 | round, terrain type (Magic Plains, Cursed Ground, …), siege, tactics phase and allowed columns, our side |

That's about **5,000 floats instead of 28,000**, and dense.

"No history" works, because everything that matters from the past is already part of the current state: waited, retaliated, spell already cast this round, effect durations.

The network itself: a small transformer over ~200 tokens (units + heroes + hexes), or a graph network like MMAI's. Units get mapped onto hexes **inside** the network, not repeated in the input.

## My proposal: output (any action, including spells)

A chain of conditional choices, each masked:

| Step | Choice | Size |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | action type: wait, defend, move, melee, shoot, hero spell, creature spell, retreat, surrender, catapult, tactics-place, end-tactics | ~12 |
| 2 | move → destination hex | 165 |
| 2 → 3 | melee → target unit → hex to attack from | 32 → 165 (≤12 legal) |
| 2 | shoot / First Aid Tent / creature spell → target unit | 32 |
| 2 → 3 → 4 | hero spell → spell → target (unit, hex, or none for mass spells) → 2nd target (Teleport, Sacrifice) | 70 → 32 or 165 → 165 |
| 2 | catapult → wall section | 8 |

The probability of any full action is the product of its steps, so every legal action, spells included, gets a probability.

**ONNX trick:** compute all the conditional tables in one forward pass (target given type, attack-hex given target, target given spell, and so on). Each entry is just a dot product between two embeddings, so even ~25,000 scores are cheap. The C++ code then samples step 1 and reads the matching row for step 2, and so on. That's one model call per decision, the conditionals are exact, and it doesn't have MMAI's greedy-only flaw.

Add a **value output** on the same network: P(win), or expected army value kept. Reinforcement learning needs it, and it's also the battle-outcome predictor your map-level AI will need for auto-resolving fights.

## How MMAI was trained

From the vcmi-gym docs and the author's journal:

- **Method:** reinforcement learning. Masked PPO with population-based hyperparameter tuning in earlier versions; plain PPO with a shared actor/critic network in the latest (v15). No imitation learning.
- **Objective:**
  - Started as a dense shaped reward: R = Σ ±(5·damage − value lost), friendly positive and enemy negative.
  - Then a small penalty per step was added, to stop it stalling or running away.
  - Finally switched to sparse: −0.02 per step, +1 win / −1 loss, plus a bonus between −1 and +1 based on losses on both sides.
- **Opponents:** first StupidAI, then BattleAI. In the final mix: 85% self-play, 5% BattleAI, 5% a bot that protects its shooters, 5% a hit-and-run bot. Separate models were trained for attacker and defender.
- **Scale and results:**
  - v12: 5 days, 2.5M battles, about $45 of rented GPU time.
  - v13 (the version in our VCMI build): wins 63% vs BattleAI with random armies, 80% with mirrored armies.
  - v15: 71% / 89%.
- **What failed:** the author tried DreamerV3, MCTS, learned world models and I2A, and abandoned them. MCTS was dropped specifically because VCMI can't roll a battle back. That's your earlier "revert turns" question: if we want search, adding state copying to the engine is the price.

Sources:
- [vcmi-gym repo](https://github.com/smanolloff/vcmi-gym)
- [vcmi-gym env_info.md](https://github.com/smanolloff/vcmi-gym/blob/main/doc/env_info.md)
- [vcmi-gym project journal](https://smanolloff.github.io/projects/vcmi-gym/)

[2026-10-22 12:33] AI: I've fixed and tested the runaway-run problem. You need about 1.3 GB of junk deleted, which I'll do if you say yes. I agree with most of your design points, with a few pushbacks below.

## 1. The disk incident

The other AI's analysis is right, but it missed my own main mistake. After each test I "cleaned up" with `pkill -f build/bin/vcmi`. That pattern never matched, because the processes run as `./vcmiclient`. I even printed "ALIVE after 60s" several times and never checked that they actually died.

I also found today that the current battle-only UpLeftAI can produce **endless battles**: both sides park in their best hexes and nobody is next to an enemy. Three of six capped test runs filled 50 MB in 40 seconds that way. So hard limits are required for any AI, not just the old map version.

**Fixed, tested and pushed (`2cd251f`):**
- **`scripts/headless.sh`** is now the only way to run AI-only games. Each run:
  - gets its own folder in `runtime/runs/`, with its own settings and logs;
  - logs at info level;
  - is killed on a time limit, a disk limit, 30 seconds with no log output (it saves a stack sample first), or when the script exits.
  - Tested: a long game was killed at its limit with nothing left running.
- **Your play settings now log at info level.** The launcher is still open, so restart it, or it may overwrite this the next time you change a setting.
- **Tests no longer touch your play settings.** Before, I edited your `settings.json` and restored it afterwards; a change you made in the launcher during a test would have been lost.
- **The built files match the source.** I rebuilt at the latest commit, and the uncommitted map-AI code is gone.

**Data rates I measured, per game:**

| Logging | Rate |
|---|---|
| VCMI default (trace) | ~300 MB/min |
| info (now the default) | ~7 MB/min, about 0.4 GB/hour |
| info + UpLeftAI debug lines | ~40 MB/min |

**Do we need this data? No.** Logs are only for debugging. Training data will be our own compact records, and I'll estimate their size before generating any.

**What I'd delete (~1.3 GB):**
- `runtime/headless_stdout.txt`: 1.1 GB, output of a stuck run.
- `runtime/runs/`: 203 MB, today's test runs.
- `runtime/pid`, `lldb.txt`, `launcher_stdout.txt`, `runtime/vcmi-user/logs/`: about 1.4 MB.
- `~/Library/Logs/vcmi` and `~/Library/Application Support/vcmi`: almost empty, created when I ran `vcmiserver` once without the repo setting.
- 3 VCMI crash reports in `~/Library/Logs/DiagnosticReports`: about 120 KB.

**What to keep:** `build/` (3.4 GB, regenerable in ~10 minutes), `vcmi/` source, game data (~1 GB), the MMAI mod you installed (43 MB). Docker I can't see from here; it may need a restart.

**Still open:** one run went silent at the start of Pink's turn on day 4 with no battle running, which looks like a Nullkiller2 or engine stall. The runner will now catch the next one with a stack sample.

## 2. Input

- **Raw counts:** agreed. It keeps the C++ side a plain serializer and puts every transform in one place. But plan the in-network normalization from day one. Counts go up to thousands and total HP to ~100k, and feeding that straight into a linear layer trains badly.
  - **What I'd count as "raw":** the engine's *current* values, e.g. attack after Bless, speed after Haste. That's game state, not engineering, and re-implementing VCMI's bonus system in torch isn't realistic.
- **Two-hex units:** VCMI stores a unit's position as its front hex. A two-hex unit also occupies the hex behind it: x−1 on the attacker side, x+1 on the defender side (I checked the code). Give both raw: `hex` and `rear_hex` (−1 for one-hex units). The network places the unit token on both hexes, and move actions target the front hex.
- **Royal Griffins:** a cap of 3 is fine. 3 is also the real maximum otherwise (1 + Expert Counterstrike's +2).
- **Abilities in the creature embedding:** agreed, with one addition. Use a learned per-ID vector **plus** a projection of the creature's static stat and ability table, with both stored as constants inside the ONNX and looked up by ID. The input stays one raw ID. Without the table, rare creatures get poor embeddings, and shared traits like "flies" or "no retaliation" don't carry over between creatures. Abilities that change during battle (blinded, berserk, petrified) arrive as effects instead.
- **Effects:** if "3 ids" means 3 effect slots, that's too few. Heavily buffed or cursed stacks often carry 5–8 effects. I'd use 8 slots of (effect id, caster level, rounds left); level matters because Expert Haste ≠ Basic Haste. Creature-inflicted effects (poison, aging, acid, petrify, bind) are mostly spell effects in VCMI, so the same slots cover them.
- **Expected damage as a torch op:** agreed. Build it, then check it against VCMI's own damage estimate on logged states and track the error. The formula has many special cases (hate, elements, jousting, wall/obstacle penalties), and the network can learn corrections on top. Write it with tensor ops only, e.g. `torch.where` instead of Python `if`, so it exports to ONNX.
- **Turn order:** it was in my table, but too vague. Per unit: `queue_this_round` (−1 if it already acted), `queue_next_round`, and `waited`.
- **Heroes:** agreed. One concat of spells known (~70 combat spells), school levels, secondary skills, primary skills and mana → dense layer. Also give the **hero ID**, because specialties (Luna, Solmyr…) change spells. `hero_can_cast_now` goes separately.
  - **Enemy spellbook:** a human player can't see it, so I'd hide it. Otherwise the model learns to rely on information a real opponent wouldn't give it.
- **Things you missed:**
  - **Legality masks must come from the engine**, not torch: reachable hexes, castable spells, valid targets. They're the game's rules, not features. If we re-implement them in torch, the model will send illegal actions, and an illegal action can hang a battle.
  - **Battlefield:** obstacles, moat, quicksand, fire wall, force field, our own land mines (with rounds left), siege walls, gate and towers.
  - **Global:** round number, battlefield type (Magic Plains, Cursed Ground…), siege flag, tactics phase and distance, our side, index of the active unit.
  - **Per unit:** clone and summoned flags. Those units vanish after the battle, so the reward must not count them.
  - **Board flip:** when we're the defender, mirror the board inside the network, so one model plays both sides. MMAI trains separate attacker and defender models.

## 3. Output

- **Dropping rare actions in v1:** agreed. Use fixed rules for the catapult, First Aid Tent, arrow towers and tactics, as MMAI does. Make the action-type list easy to extend, so adding a type later means a new entry and retraining, not a redesign.
- **I'd also leave out retreat and surrender in v1.** Whether to flee is a map-level decision, so the map AI should make it; the battle AI just fights.
- **Hero spells belong in v1.** In Heroes 3 a cast doesn't use up the unit's turn: the hero casts, then the same unit still acts. So casting is its own decision step (spell → target(s)), and the engine masks it out once the hero has cast this round.
- **Use conditional heads, in one ONNX pass.** That avoids MMAI's greedy-only flaw.

## 4. Reward

- **Terminal-only is fine for battles.** They're short, tens of decisions, and PPO with a value baseline handles that. MMAI ended up mostly sparse too.
- **Two of your terms collapse:**
  - On a win, the enemy is always 100% destroyed, so a win scores 2 + own-remaining, i.e. 2 to 3.
  - On a loss, nothing of yours remains, so a loss scores −1 + enemy-destroyed, i.e. −1 to 0.

  Any win still beats any loss, so it works; it's just redundant. Both terms come back to life if retreat is added later.
- **The round penalty also needs a hard round cap**, which ends in a draw or loss. We just saw why: stalemates are real. Keep the penalty small enough that 100 rounds cost less than one cheap unit, e.g. 0.002 per round.
- **Mana as a % of the pool:** good, because it scales with any hero. The right price, though, depends on the map (is there another fight tomorrow?). Later, the penalty weights could be **inputs** to the network, so the map AI can ask for "win cheaply" or "win at any cost" from the same model.
- **Dice rolls after your action:** you're right, that's standard reinforcement learning; the value baseline absorbs the noise. Only search methods need explicit chance handling, and those would need battle-state copying in VCMI anyway.
- **Value head:** predict the parts separately: P(win), own value remaining, enemy value destroyed. That's exactly the battle-outcome predictor the map AI needs for auto-resolving fights.

## Cleanup and open items

- **Deleted, ~1.45 GB of generated data:** the stuck run's output file (1.25 GB; it was still being written until 11:40 today), the test runs, old logs, crash reports and the near-empty `~/Library` folders.
- **Kept:** all code, `build/` (3.4 GB), game data, the MMAI mod and your settings. `runtime/` is now 986 MB, all of it needed.
- **UpLeftAI endless battles: fixed.** From round 10 on, StupidAI plays instead. 12 test games (2,049 battles) all finished, versus 3 of 6 stalling before. That test wrote 23 MB in about 2 minutes, now deleted. Pushed (`13e484d`).
- **Nullkiller2 stall: didn't recur** in 12 more runs. The runner now saves a stack sample automatically if it happens again.

## Spell effects: 3 or more?

- **Rules: no limit.** VCMI tracks every active battle-spell effect on a unit. Only its info window stops drawing after 8 icons; the code says "interface limit reached".
- **The original game's window:** I couldn't confirm online how many icons it shows. Your memory of 3 is probably that display limit, not a rule.
- **My "often 5–8" was wrong.** Typical battles have 0–3 effects per unit. 5 or more only happens in spell-heavy fights between strong casters.
- **Suggestion:** use K slots of (effect id, level, rounds left). Pick K from real data: log the maximum during data generation and count how often it overflows. 8 is a safe start.

## Masks

I agree enforcement belongs at the output: illegal logits are set to −∞ before the softmax. Do that **during training too**, not only at play time; otherwise training wastes effort pushing down actions that could never happen.

As an input I'd lean towards including at least the active unit's reachable hexes. They're exact rules information, always available at play time, and they save the network from learning pathfinding around obstacles, quicksand and two-hex shapes. MMAI does feed them in. But it's an empirical question: train with and without them early on and compare.

## Siege flag

Agreed, drop it. Walls, gate and towers in the hex data already imply a siege.

## Board flip: your caution was justified

VCMI's rows alternate their half-hex offset, and I tested every candidate mirror against the engine's own neighbour rule:

| Mirror | Hexes landing on the field | Neighbour links kept |
|---|---|---|
| x → 16−x | 165/165 | 608/888: wrong |
| parity-aware (16−x odd rows, 15−x even rows) | 159/165 | 888/888 |
| 180° rotation | 165/165 | 608/888: wrong |

**The battlefield isn't left-right symmetric.** The left and right edges zig-zag in the same phase, so the attacker's back edge has a different shape from the defender's. No exact flip exists.

**My recommendation for v1: no flip.** Use a single model with a side input, and measure any attacker/defender asymmetry with mirrored-army tests. If we add a flip later, it must be the parity-aware version on a padded grid with a "playable hex" channel, and tested by converting every logged real decision and checking the engine still accepts it.

## Rare actions: no special flag

- Fix the full **action-type list** now, including catapult, First Aid Tent, tactics, retreat and surrender. In v1, unsupported types are simply always masked off.
- Units the model doesn't control (catapult, tent, arrow towers) and the tactics phase are handled by fixed C++ rules, as MMAI does. The model isn't called for them, so they produce no training samples.
- Supporting a type later means adding or enabling its argument output and fine-tuning. Nothing gets redesigned.

## Reward: retreat, surrender and hero value

What the engine actually does:
- **Retreat:** the hero goes back to your tavern with **1 creature of its default type**; the army is lost.
- **Surrender:** the hero goes back to the tavern **with its army**.
- **Rehiring** either one costs **2500** gold.
- **Surrender cost:** gold cost × count for regular army units (summons and war machines are free) × (1 − discount). Diplomacy gives 20/40/60%, and items like Statesman's Medal stack through the same bonus. The engine computes the exact figure.
- **Retreat is blocked** when:
  - the side has no hero;
  - Shackles of War are in play;
  - the attacker has cast a spell in the first rounds (the anti hit-and-run rule);
  - it's a town defender without an Escape Tunnel.
- **Surrender also requires** an enemy hero, and town defenders can't surrender.
- **Resource prices** in VCMI's config: wood and ore 250, the other four 500. That fits your "gold + resources" unit value.

My suggestions:

1. **Retreat value should be smooth, not a cutoff:** max(0, V_hero − V_fresh), where V_fresh is a fresh tavern hero plus its starting army (computable from the hero's config). With a cutoff, a hero 1 gold above the line jumps from 0 to its full value. The smooth version measures what you actually gain from the retreat.
2. **Hero value:** sum of artifact prices (every artifact has a price in the game data), war machine prices, and a price per level. Tree of Knowledge offers a level for 2000 gold in H3, a game-given anchor. Levels already bring primary and secondary skills, so don't count those twice.
3. **Gold becomes a model input** if surrender is ever an action, because the model can't value surrender without knowing what you can afford.
4. **On "no trainable parameters, no risk":** agreed for stability. The real risk is **mis-pricing, which the agent will exploit**. For example, if an expensive hero's retreat value is too high, it will learn to flee on round 1 instead of fighting. Before training, compute the reward for a table of scenarios (win cheaply, win at heavy cost, flee early, flee late, surrender, die) and check the order looks right.

Sources:
- [Faerie dragon (H3), Might and Magic wiki](https://mightandmagic.fandom.com/wiki/Faerie_dragon_(H3)), [HD mod description](https://www.sites.google.com/site/heroes3hd/eng/description): from the search for the original game's effect-icon limit; neither page confirms it.

[2026-10-22 14:05] AI: Agreed on both, with one correction on the XP curve and one condition on surrender.

## Level value

VCMI's XP table (`CHeroHandler::loadExperience`) isn't Fibonacci. The XP needed for each next level goes 1000, 1000, 1200, 1400, 1600, 1800, 2000, 2200, 2500, 2800, 3100, then **+20% per level** from level 12 on.

So your rule, 1000 for level 1→2 scaled by the XP ratio, works out to **exactly 1 gold per XP point**. That means we can simply use the hero's **actual XP** as its level value: no table needed, and it counts partial progress towards the next level too.

| Level | Value |
|---|---|
| 5 | 4,600 |
| 10 | 14,700 |
| 15 | 34,140 |
| 20 | 81,961 |
| 25 | ~201k |
| 30 | ~497k |

One thing to be aware of: every level gives the same +1 primary skill and one skill pick, while the XP price grows 20% per level. So this measures **cost to rebuild** the hero, not its fighting strength. For "what do we lose if the hero dies" that's the right quantity. But a level-30 hero then outweighs most armies, which pushes high-level heroes towards retreating early. The scenario-table check before training will show whether that's acceptable.

## Gold as an input

You're right. Masking already handles "can't afford it": surrender gets masked out and the next-best legal action is picked, at play time and in training.

The one gap is your own reward rule, "surrender only counts if ≥2500 gold remains after paying". If the model can't see gold, it can't tell a surrender worth a full army from one worth nothing. The fix belongs in the mask, not the input: **surrender is legal only if gold ≥ surrender cost + 2500**. The C++ side computes that from the player's gold when building the mask. Then any surrender the model chooses always qualifies, the reward never hits the "<2500" case, and gold stays out of the input. The cost itself the model can already work out from inputs it has: its army and the hero's Diplomacy.

[2026-10-22 14:54] AI: I'm confident that if you swap in a better ONNX file that keeps the same contract, it will work. One gap remains: **siege battles haven't been tested in-game**.

## What's built

**`battle_model/`, the Python side:**
- `constants.py`, `schema.py`: the contract, i.e. every input tensor (raw engine values plus legality masks) and every output tensor (masked conditional log-probabilities plus a value).
- `model.py`: the small model, 239k parameters, about 2 ms per decision in the game.
- `distributions.py`: probability of a full action, exact entropy, and a reference sampler.
- `losses.py`: PPO policy and value losses with an entropy bonus. `reward.py`: your terminal reward, hero and unit values, retreat and surrender.
- `export.py`: ONNX export, which writes a schema version and a contract hash into the file.
- `codegen.py`: generates the C++ header from the Python contract. `check_dumps.py`: replays decisions the C++ side dumped through the model in Python.
- `tests/`: 18 tests.

**`agents/AlphaBattleAI/`, the C++ battle AI:**
- encodes the battle into the input tensors and builds the masks from the engine's own rules;
- runs the ONNX file and samples a legal action;
- executes it: wait, defend, move, melee, shoot, hero spell (no target, unit, hex or two targets), retreat, and surrender (only when gold ≥ cost + 2500).

War machines, the tactics phase and everything from round 30 on are handled by StupidAI, so no battle can run forever. You can pick AlphaBattleAI in the launcher's AI settings.

## How to replace the model

Train any architecture, run `.venv/bin/python -m battle_model.export --out your.onnx`, then set `ALPHA_BATTLE_MODEL=/path/your.onnx` (or overwrite `battle_model/artifacts/battle_model.onnx`). On load, the C++ side checks the schema version, the contract hash and every input/output name, dtype and shape. If anything differs, it refuses the file with a clear error and StupidAI plays.

## What I verified

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Python tests: shapes, illegal = −inf, all full-action probabilities sum to 1, exact entropy, sampled frequencies, finite PPO gradients, reward ordering, ONNX = torch, header up to date | 18/18 pass |
| C++ decisions replayed in Python (monster side, hero side, bigger model) | 584 decisions: identical outputs (difference 0 to 5e-7), every executed action legal |
| In-game, model on one side or both, ~6,000 battles across all runs | 0 decision errors, 0 server rejections of our actions; median 2 actions per battle, max 33 |
| Hero spells | all four target kinds cast: none, unit, hex, two-target |
| Different architecture (3 layers, 2.4 MB) | worked with no code changes |
| Wrong contract hash; garbage file | both refused with one clear message; StupidAI played and the game finished |
| Greedy mode (temperature 0) | works |

Bugs I found and fixed along the way: an exporter-dropped input, a NaN gradient in the entropy, and a C++ use-after-free when reading the model's types.

## Limits

- **Sieges are untested in-game.** No siege happened in any test game. The server code shows that an illegal action costs the unit its turn rather than hanging the battle, and `check_dumps` will catch mask problems once a siege gets dumped.
- **Reserved action types are always masked off for now:** creature spells, catapult, First Aid Tent, tactics. They're in the contract, so enabling one later means C++ work plus retraining, not a contract change.
- **The random model retreats a lot.** Retreat is legal at nearly every decision, so it gets about 1/7 of the probability.
- **Export uses torch's old exporter** (`dynamo=False`), which is deprecated. A future torch release may require switching.

## Data

The test runs generated up to ~150 MB at a time; all of it is deleted. What remains: `.venv` (889 MB, needed) and the 1.1 MB default model. The `.onnx` file is ignored by git and regenerated by the export command.

Everything is committed and pushed (`905b16b`, VCMI `a0d211c18`).

[2026-10-22 14:55] AI: You're right that it would be unacceptable. But I phrased it badly, and I hadn't actually verified it either. Let me separate the two cases.

[2026-10-22 15:00] AI: You're right; I said it badly, and it was also wrong. An illegal action no longer costs a turn: the AI now checks every action before sending it, and if the server still refuses one, the same unit picks its next-best legal action.

**Your surrender case already worked.** If surrender is legal at our first unit's turn and the model picks it, it executes at once and the battle ends; earlier tests had 36 surrenders and 501 retreats. VCMI only lets a battle AI decide on its own units' turns, so if the enemy has faster units they act first. Those are the game's rules.

**The case I was describing:** our mask says an action is legal but the server disagrees. I checked the server code properly this time:
- **Move, melee, shoot, wait, defend:** the server marks the unit as having acted *before* validating. A rejection here would lose the turn, and nothing can retry it afterwards.
- **Hero spell, retreat, surrender:** the server asks the same unit again. With greedy sampling, the model could have picked the same rejected choice forever.

**What I added (pushed, `d383c53`):**
1. **Check before sending:** every chosen action is checked against the masks first. If it fails, it's removed from the masks and the model chooses again over what's left, so nothing illegal is sent.
2. **Recovery when the same unit is asked again:** if a refused spell, retreat or surrender brings the same unit back, that choice is excluded and the next-best legal action is taken. A spell counts as accepted only if the game's "spell was cast" event arrives.

For move/melee/shoot the first layer is the only protection. That's acceptable because the masks are built with the very same engine checks the server uses.

**Tested with deliberately injected faults** (`ALPHA_BATTLE_TEST_FAULT=surrender,spell,decoder`):

| Run | Refused by server → same unit chose again | Corrupted choice → caught before sending | Errors |
|---|---|---|---|
| sampling, game 1 | 138 / 138 | 119 / 119 | 0 |
| sampling, game 2 | 178 / 178 | 147 / 147 | 0 |
| greedy (most-likely action) | 53 / 53 | 44 / 44 | 0 |
| no faults (baseline) | 0 refusals | 0 | 0 |

**Something the first fault run revealed:** some surrenders our mask forbade were **accepted** by the server. That's because our mask also requires the 2500 gold to rehire the hero, while the server only requires the surrender cost. So with, say, 3000 gold and a 1000 surrender cost, the model can't choose surrender, although the game would allow it. That's your rule working as designed; I mention it because the model will see surrender as unavailable in those cases.

[2026-10-22 16:57] AI: All six points are done and pushed (`c906a4e`). One finding along the way: the engine uses spell/effect IDs up to 103, not the 82 I had assumed, so I enlarged that vocabulary to 128.

## "Check before sending" must never trigger

It's now commented in the C++ as you asked: it must never trigger outside the deliberate test faults, any trigger is a bug in our code to fix immediately, and it can be commented out once full games show it never fires. A real trigger now logs as `BUG:`. The server-refusal recovery has the same comment and the same `BUG:` log. In fairness, it costs about ten mask lookups (microseconds), so there's little reason to ever remove it.

## `schema.py`: strings or waste per batch?

No. The names are only tensor labels: Python uses them as dictionary keys, and the C++ side reads them once, when it loads the model. Per decision, only numbers go in. The metadata is two short values (schema version, a 16-character hash).

The one real cost to plan for later is **mask size**. The masks hold about 33,000 true/false values per decision. Stored as plain bytes, 1M training decisions would be about 33 GB; packed into bits, about 4 GB. The data loader should store them packed.

## `losses.py`: clip, value_coef, entropy_coef

These are PPO's own training settings, not part of the reward. Your mana and round penalties are in `reward.py` (`round_penalty=0.002`, `mana_penalty=0.05`). The split: `reward.py` decides what is good, and `losses.py` decides how the model learns from it.

- **clip** (now `clip_range = 0.2`): limits how far one update can change an action's probability, at most ±20%. That's what keeps PPO stable.
- **value_coef** (now `value_weight = 0.5`): how much the value prediction counts against the policy loss. It weights how well the model predicts the final reward, which PPO needs, and which later becomes the battle-outcome predictor.
- **entropy_coef** (now `entropy_weight = 0.01`): a small bonus for keeping choices spread out, so the model keeps exploring instead of locking onto early habits.

The file now explains all of this at the top.

## Starting biases

`ACTION_TYPE_PRIOR` in `model.py`: melee and shoot +1, spell and move +0.5, wait and defend 0, **retreat and surrender −2**.

I made these the *initial values* of the type head (its weights start at zero), not a fixed offset. A frozen −2 could simply be cancelled out by the network as it learns, and it would make the learned scores harder to read. The value head also starts at 0.

What happened in two test games:

| | Before | With priors |
|---|---|---|
| Retreat, share of decisions | 23% | **3%** |
| Melee, share of decisions | 3% | **14%** |
| Decisions per battle (median) | 2 | **12** |
| Battles ending in retreat/surrender | most | 47% |

The 47% is because battles are now much longer: 3% per decision still adds up. If that's too many, try −3 or −4.

## Who remembers what an ID means?

Before today, **nobody**: the model saw bare numbers. Now:

- **The engine exports its own tables:** a small tool (`scripts/export_vocab.sh`) writes `battle_model/artifacts/vocab.json`. It covers 150 creatures with level, faction, base stats, cost and abilities; 104 spells and effects with level, schools and mana costs; plus 163 heroes, 28 skills, 144 artifacts with prices, 24 battlefields and 125 obstacles.
- **`vocab.py`** reads them, e.g. `name("creatures", 13)` → "Archangel" and `creature_value(13)` → 6500 (5000 gold + 3 gems). It can also print a readable unit list from any model input or dump.
- **Why the engine, not VCMI's config files:** the config files only contain the ID and an internal key. Names, stats and costs are loaded at runtime from the original game data, so the engine is the only source that's guaranteed to match the IDs the C++ side encodes.
- **Not in git:** the names and stats are Heroes 3 game data, so the file is regenerated locally.
- This table is also what the reward's unit values and artifact prices will use, and what you'd use to initialize creature embeddings from stats.

## What each file does, and why it's split this way

The rule: **anything about what the model learns lives in Python. The C++ side only turns the game into tensors and the model's choice back into a game action.**

| | File | Role |
|---|---|---|
| **Science** (yours) | `model.py` | The network, the in-network transforms, the priors. Any architecture is fine; only its inputs and outputs are fixed. |
| | `distributions.py` | The probability of a full action as a product of conditional choices, the exact entropy, and the reference sampler. |
| | `losses.py` | How it learns (PPO). |
| | `reward.py` | What counts as good. |
| | `vocab.py` | What the IDs mean. |
| **Contract** | `constants.py`, `schema.py` | Every tensor's name, shape and meaning. Split out because C++ depends on them: change deliberately, then regenerate the C++ header and bump the version. |
| **Plumbing** | `export.py`, `codegen.py`, `check_dumps.py`, `tests/` | Make the ONNX file, generate the C++ header, prove C++ and Python agree. |

The training loop and data loader will join the science group, as you said. The README now has these tables too.

[2026-10-22 17:16] AI: All three are answered below. One correction on the third: the test battles aren't drawn from any defined or fixed distribution; they're whatever full AI-vs-AI games happen to produce.

## 1. Build the training examples in the data loader, store something small?

Yes, that's the right design. The one constraint is that **masks can't be recomputed in Python.** They're the engine's rules (pathfinding, obstacles, spell targeting), so they must be recorded while playing. But they're almost empty, so record only the legal entries. Measured on 200 real decisions:

| What | Stored now (dense) | Compact |
|---|---|---|
| Masks | 32,800 entries | **~57 legal entries on average** (max ~130), about 115 bytes as index lists |
| Raw inputs | 3,341 numbers as 8-byte ints | All small, mostly padding values: about 1–2 KB compressed |
| Model outputs | ~33k floats | **Don't store them.** Training recomputes them; keep only the chosen action, its old log-probability and the value estimate |

So about **2 KB per decision instead of ~200 KB**: 1M decisions ≈ 2 GB. The data loader turns records back into the dense tensor dict when it builds each batch.

Storing only the mask rows along the chosen action would be enough for `log_prob`, but not for the exact entropy. Since the full sparse masks are only ~115 bytes, I'd keep them exact.

## 2. How your reward turns into the loss

`losses.py` now walks through it in 7 steps, and the step that was missing, computing advantages, is now code with tests (`battle_returns_and_advantages`). In short, for one battle seen by one side:

1. **Reward:** your reward function gives one number R when the battle ends. Every earlier decision gets 0.
2. **Return:** each decision's return is the reward that followed it. With γ = 1 that's R for every decision of the battle. I chose γ = 1 because battles are short and your round penalty already prices slowness.
3. **Value head:** learns to predict, from any state, the R the battle will end with. That's the value loss.
4. **Advantage:** how much better than predicted things went after a decision. This is the key step: once the value head is decent, a decision after which the predicted outcome **jumps** gets positive credit, and one after which it **drops** gets negative credit. That's how one number at the end becomes credit for individual moves. λ = 0.95 mixes "judge by the final outcome" (λ = 1: noisy, since every move of a won battle looks good) with "judge by the change in prediction" (λ = 0: trusts the value head completely).
5. **Policy loss:** raise the probability of decisions with positive advantage and lower the others. PPO's clip limits each update to ±20% per action, because the data came from the old model.
6. **Total loss:** policy + 0.5 × value − 0.01 × entropy.

Every setting now has a comment explaining why it has that value. One more choice: advantages are rescaled to mean 0, standard deviation 1 in each batch, so the learning step doesn't depend on your reward's scale.

## 3. Where the test battles come from

From **full AI-vs-AI map games**. Nullkiller2 plays the map on Elbow Room, Arrogance or Dawn of War, and a battle happens whenever a hero runs into wandering monsters, another hero or a town.

- **Not fixed.** VCMI picks a new random seed for each game, and the only seed we set is for our own sampler. Rerunning gives different battles.
- **Not a distribution we control.** It's whatever those maps and Nullkiller's choices produce. Measured over 635 battles: roughly half against monsters and half against heroes (my log parsing for that split is approximate). The median game day was 71, with a long tail to day 1,225, because the test-mode Arrogance game drags on.
- **Sieges do happen.** That run included town fights, and a follow-up 2-minute run had 166 with walls: 0 errors, 0 bugs, no rejected actions. That closes the gap I flagged earlier.

That's fine for testing the plumbing, but **unsuitable for training**: it's skewed, unseeded and indescribable. VCMI has no "start one battle" mode, so the clean approach (what vcmi-gym did) is to **generate battle maps in Python**. VCMI's own map format is zipped JSON. Each map would hold many armies standing next to each other, with army sizes, factions, hero level, skills, spells, artifacts and terrain drawn from distributions you define in Python. Then:

- the training distribution is explicit and yours to tune, which is also where a curriculum would go;
- a fixed, seeded set of maps becomes the evaluation benchmark.

It's a sizeable piece of work, and I'd make it the next step if you agree.

Pushed: `19f9d5f`. All 26 tests pass.
