[2026-10-22 21:28] AI: You're right that intermediate scoring is a real asset, and I think the idea will help, so I'm building it. Two corrections first: Go engines do use intermediate signals, and your scheme needs a precise definition for rounds that end mid-battle. Details below.

## The multi-horizon value heads

**Why it should help:** a single value head learns from one number per battle, noisy and far in the future. Predicting "the score at the end of *this* round" is a much easier, less noisy target that's strongly correlated with the final result. It also forces the network to understand the immediate exchange of blows. That makes the shared network better, and the final value is what turns your terminal reward into per-move credit. There's strong precedent:

- **KataGo** (the strongest open Go engine) trains extra heads for final score and for the owner of each board point, and reports large training speed-ups from them.
- **Leela Chess** added a "moves left" head.

So Go *does* have intermediate signals; your scheme is the battle version of what made KataGo fast. That's encouraging, not a reason to drop it. And you're right that it's richer than chess's piece values: here it's the game's own reward formula, applied after a round.

**Bonus:** V0, the value at the end of this round, is exactly what a future one-round look-ahead search would need to evaluate its positions.

**Three adjustments I'd make:**

1. **Defining "the formula at the end of a round"** when the battle isn't over yet. The value terms and the penalties so far are well defined, but the ±1 isn't. I'd use **0 for the outcome term while the battle is still going**, and the actual final reward once it has ended. That gives exactly your rule: in the last round V0 = V1 = V2 = V.
2. **True boosting: stop gradients between heads.** If V's logit is z2 + own, then V's loss would also pull on V0's weights, and V0 would stop meaning "this round". So each head adds a *frozen copy* of the previous head's logit, and learns only the difference. It's a flag (`boost_stop_gradient`), on by default.
3. **Loss: cross-entropy on the 0–1-scaled target, not squared error.** With a sigmoid output, squared error's gradient vanishes when the sigmoid saturates on a wrong answer; cross-entropy's doesn't. Same targets, better behaved.

**The sigmoid range:** for it to cover "highest to lowest possible", the reward needs fixed bounds, so the round penalty stops growing after 100 rounds (more than any battle with the current round cap). The range is then −1.25 to 3.0.

## Advantage vs AlphaGo

**Advantage** = what actually followed an action, minus what the value head expected on average from that state. If it's positive, the action was better than usual, so make it more likely.

- **AlphaGo Zero / AlphaZero** learn differently. At every move they run a search (MCTS) that simulates hundreds of continuations using the network. The policy is then trained to **imitate the search's choice** (cross-entropy against the search's visit counts), and the value to predict the game result. There are no advantages: the search is the improvement step, and the network distills it.
- **PPO (ours)** has no search. It improves by trial and error on the actions actually played, weighted by advantage.
- **Which is better:** with a perfect, cheap simulator, AlphaZero-style search is far more data-efficient. Each move gets a much better-informed target than "this battle happened to go well."
- **Why not yet:** search needs to copy the battle, try moves and undo them, which the real VCMI engine can't do. The vcmi-gym author dropped MCTS for exactly that reason. Battles also have dice (damage, luck, morale), which plain AlphaZero doesn't handle; that needs the stochastic variants (Stochastic MuZero).
- **They combine well:** BattleAI already simulates moves on in-engine battle copies (`HypotheticBattle`), so a C++ search over those is possible later. It would use our trained policy as its prior and our value heads, V0 especially, to evaluate positions. **Plan: PPO now; search later, as an upgrade that reuses the same network.**

Implementing it now. The output shape changes, so this is contract version 2, and old ONNX files will be refused. First the reward side: fixed bounds and the end-of-round score.

[2026-10-22 21:31] AI: The boosted value heads are built, tested and pushed (`a2d1900`). This changed the model's outputs, so it's contract version 2, and the C++ side now refuses older ONNX files.

**What was built:**
- **`model.py`:** the value output is `[V0, V1, V2, V]`. Each head is one linear layer on the last hidden state and adds the previous head's logit, then goes through a sigmoid scaled to the reward's full range. They all start mid-range (0.875).
- **`reward.py`:**
  - the range is −1.25 to 3.0, made fixed by stopping the round penalty after 100 rounds;
  - `round_score` is your formula applied at the end of a round of an unfinished battle, with the win/loss term set to 0 because nobody has won yet.
- **`losses.py`:**
  - `value_targets` builds the 4 labels per decision exactly as you described: the V label is the final reward for every decision, V0 changes per round, and Vk equals V once the battle has ended, so in the last round V0 = V1 = V2 = V;
  - `value_loss` splits the loss across heads with `value_head_weights` (equal for now);
  - the number of horizons is one constant (`N_VALUE_HORIZONS = 3`).

**Three choices I made on top of your design** (all explained in the code):

1. **The win/loss term is 0 at the end of a round while the battle is still going.** That's what makes your formula defined mid-battle.
2. **Each head adds a frozen copy of the previous head's logit.** Without that, V's loss would also pull on V0, and V0 would stop meaning "this round". It's a flag (`boost_stop_gradient`) if you want to compare.
3. **The loss is cross-entropy on the 0–1-scaled label rather than squared error.** Same best answer, but its gradient doesn't vanish when the sigmoid saturates on a wrong answer.

Only V, the final-reward head, feeds the advantages. The shorter heads improve the shared network and the base that V builds on.

**Tested:** 32 Python tests pass. They cover the labels for your multi-round example, that the heads stay inside the range, and that stopping gradients really keeps V's label off V0. In a capped game the model played 64 battles with 0 errors, and C++ and Python agreed exactly on all 100 dumped decisions.

**One gap before training:** the data recorder doesn't exist yet. To produce these labels it must log the score at the end of every round (both armies' values, mana used); I'll build that into the data plumbing when we get there.

[2026-10-22 21:49] AI: All done and pushed (`c80198b`). You're right about "moves left" and "final score": they predict facts about the end of the game. They're auxiliary end-of-game targets, not intermediate signals, so your end-of-round heads really are something extra.

**Changes:**
- **Outcome term = 0 mid-battle:** kept, with the one-line comment in `reward.py` that it's a choice, not a necessity.
- **Stop gradients between heads:** it's the `boost_stop_gradient` flag on `BattleModel`, on by default.
- **Everything on 0..1 with cross-entropy:**
  - each value head now outputs `sigmoid(logit)`;
  - every label (V0, V1, V2, V) is the reward rescaled to 0..1 (0 = worst possible reward, 1 = best);
  - all heads train with cross-entropy;
  - the advantage calculation uses the same scale.

  The model no longer knows the reward settings at all. `reward.to_unit` / `from_unit` convert to and from reward units for reading results. The policy outputs were already softmax probabilities. The output's meaning changed, so this is contract version 3.
- **100-round cap:** comment added saying the number is arbitrary and no battle should reach it.
- **`losses.py` header:** now says what PPO stands for, the plan (PPO now, search later reusing the same network), and why the vcmi-gym author dropped MCTS.

**Verified:** 33 tests pass. A capped game played 160 battles with 0 errors, and C++ and Python agreed exactly on all 100 dumped decisions.

**PPO = Proximal Policy Optimization.** "Policy optimization": it improves the policy directly from games it plays itself. "Proximal": each update is kept close to the policy that collected the games (the ±20% clip), because once the policy drifts far from it, those games no longer show what the *new* policy would do. It's the standard workhorse because it's simple and stable, and it needs nothing from the environment except "play and report what happened".

**Why vcmi-gym dropped MCTS:**
- **Copying the battle state.** Search (MCTS, AlphaZero's core) needs to copy the current battle, try a move, look a few moves ahead, then return to the original state, hundreds of times per decision. VCMI's real battle engine can't copy or restore a battle, so every "what if" would mean replaying the whole battle from the start. The author called it impractical and dropped it.
- **Dice.** Search also needs chance handled explicitly (damage rolls, luck, morale), which plain AlphaZero doesn't do.

Our route around both is the one I mentioned: BattleAI already simulates moves on lightweight in-engine battle copies (`HypotheticBattle`). A later search could run on those, using our network's policy as its prior and V0 to evaluate positions one round ahead. That's an upgrade on top of PPO, not a replacement.

[2026-10-22 22:04] AI: Yes, I can do both. Here's how, plus a few choices for you to confirm.

## 1. One battle: untrained model vs the default AI

Yes. The default battle AI is **BattleAI**. VCMI can't start a battle on its own, so it has to happen on a map:

- **A tiny map** with two heroes standing next to each other and nothing else, so no other battles can happen.
- **A small map AI that forces the fight.** Nullkiller2 might simply decline to attack an equal army. A tiny map AI (like the UpLeftAI scaffolding) would make one hero attack and the other wait.
- **Different battle AIs per side.** It would also choose the battle AI by side, e.g. ours for Red and BattleAI for Blue. VCMI's settings can't do that by themselves, because both computer players would get the same battle AI.
- **Exactly one battle.** With no towns, whoever loses (dies, retreats or surrenders) is eliminated and the game ends.
- **Output:** a summary of who won, rounds, losses on both sides and the reward. I can also run it in a window so you can watch, but VCMI's spectator mode crashed earlier; I'd need to check that the fixes we took from upstream cured it.

## 2. A permanent settings file for a balanced battle

Also yes. The settings would be a Python-side file, e.g. `battle_model/scenarios/castle_vs_inferno.json`, and a Python script would turn it into a VCMI map. VCMI's own map format is zipped JSON, and it supports custom hero armies, primary skills, secondary skills and spellbooks.

- **Armies, one week's base growth:** Castle 14 Pikemen, 9 Archers, 7 Griffins, 4 Swordsmen, 3 Monks, 2 Cavaliers, 1 Angel. Inferno 15 Imps, 8 Gogs, 5 Hell Hounds, 4 Demons, 3 Pit Fiends, 2 Efreet, 1 Devil.
- **Heroes:** attack, defense, power and knowledge all 5; secondary skills explicitly empty; a spellbook with 10 spells.
- **"Random 10 spells" drawn once with a seed and written into the file**, so it stays fixed and you can read exactly which spells they are.
- **Balance check:** I'd report each side's total value from `vocab.py`. A week of Castle against a week of Inferno is roughly even, but not exactly.

## Choices to confirm

1. **Creatures:** basic or upgraded? I'd assume basic.
2. **Spells:** the *same* 10 for both heroes (fairer), or a different random 10 each?
3. **War machines:** none for either side? I'd recommend none.
4. **Heroes:** specialties matter (some boost a creature or a spell). I'd pick heroes whose specialties don't affect battle, such as resource specialists.
5. **Sides:** the attacker/defender position matters a little. Which side should our model play, or should we run both?
6. **Battlefield:** a fixed terrain, e.g. grass.
7. **Reproducibility (repeatable results):** obstacle placement and damage rolls come from the game's random number generator, which VCMI seeds randomly. To make the battle exactly repeatable I'd add a fixed-seed option to our VCMI fork. I believe that's a small change, but I haven't verified it yet.

The main unknown is the map format. VCMI's JSON map format isn't formally documented, so I'd have to work out the exact fields from its loader code and test until the map loads. Everything else uses pieces we've already built. Once you've answered the choices above, I'll build it.

**Why the map trick can't replace the engine fix.** I checked VCMI's battle-only code; this mode doesn't work the way the trick assumes:

1. **More heroes breaks the auto-start.** The server only starts the battle by itself if the map has *exactly two heroes and no towns*. With a third hero it takes the town branch instead, and with no towns it would crash.
2. **The end doesn't depend on anyone losing.** In this mode, *any* victory/loss check ends the game for everyone at once. Paying for a surrender triggers such a check, so the game ends before the battle result is sent, however many heroes each side has elsewhere.

So the only map-side route is to drop battle-only mode entirely. Then we'd need a normal map plus a small map AI of our own to force the attack, which is more of our code, not less.

**My fix is a genuine VCMI bug fix:** a human in VCMI's own Battle Only lobby who surrenders hits the same thing, and the game ends without showing the result. It's about 10 lines, including one helper function. I'd keep it, but it's your call:

- keep it as a bug fix, or
- I revert it once the run finishes, and we switch to a normal map with our own attack AI.

I haven't touched the engine now, because rebuilding during the run would swap binaries under it. The other engine change I made, the headless crash at game end, is a plain bug fix and stays either way.

**Each epoch takes 1.77 s on average** (1.5–2.8 s): two battles in parallel plus the update, so about 30 minutes for 1,000 epochs.

**TensorBoard: http://localhost:6006.** It shows reward (mean, attack, defence), wins per side, the policy/value/entropy losses, and decisions, rounds and seconds per epoch. I'd only written a CSV before, which was a miss.

- **This run:** it started before TensorBoard existed, so a background follower mirrors its CSV into TensorBoard every 10 s.
- **Future runs:** they log to TensorBoard directly.
- **The server:** it's running in the background. To start it yourself later: `.venv/bin/tensorboard --logdir runtime/train`.

**No code bug. Three problems combine:**

1. **The scenario is decided by one spell.** With spell power 5, Armageddon (24 mana) deals about 280 damage to every stack, enough to wipe a week-1 army on *both* sides. Armageddon was one of the 10 random spells, which was bad luck. Winning in this setup takes a two-step plan, e.g. Anti-Magic on your own strongest unit in round 1, then Armageddon in round 2. That's a real Heroes 3 tactic, but hard to find by random exploration.
2. **The reward makes mutual destruction worth about 0, an even trade.** Any real fight the untrained model tried lost worse, so this is a genuine local optimum, not reward hacking through a bug. A win would score 2–3.
3. **Exploration collapsed, and part of that is my implementation:**
   - Entropy fell to ~0.003 around epoch 350. With fixed seeds, the battle is then identical every epoch.
   - The defence battle is now **one decision long**, but my loss rescales advantages *per batch* to mean 0 / std 1. With 1–3 samples that rescaling is meaningless: it just ranks the samples against each other, whatever the actual result was. That should be done over a whole epoch's decisions, or skipped when there are too few. That one's on me.

**What I'd change (your call):**

| | Change | Why |
|---|---|---|
| A | **Fix the advantage rescaling:** across the epoch, and skipped for tiny batches | A bug in my code, fix regardless |
| B | **More battles per epoch:** e.g. 8 per side, different sampler seeds, still in parallel | Exploration; about 4× slower per epoch (~6–7 s) |
| C | **Entropy weight 0.01 → 0.03** | Keeps the policy from collapsing this early |
| D | **Scenario:** re-draw the 10 spells without Armageddon, or keep it as a deliberately hard test | Your modeling choice; I'd remove it so the first test measures learning to *fight* |
| E | **Value of a draw:** both armies dead, currently counted as a loss (−1) | Your choice; counting it as 0 would make Armageddon even more attractive |

[2026-10-22 23:03] USER: what's the batch size? 1 or 2?

[2026-10-22 23:03] TOOLS: Bash

[2026-10-22 23:03] AI: Neither. The batch is counted in decisions, not battles: it holds **all the decisions from both battles of the epoch**, so in battle terms it's 2.

- **`batch_size = 64` decisions per gradient step.** One decision is one unit's action or one hero cast.
- **An epoch so far has about 49 decisions** on average (9 to 83) across its two battles.
- **Usually that's one batch of about 49 decisions.** In 35 of the 244 epochs so far, the epoch had more than 64 decisions and split into two batches (64 plus the rest).
- **Each epoch's data is used for 4 gradient steps** (`ppo_passes = 4`), so roughly 4 updates per epoch, all on the same two battles.

So each update learns from just one attack battle and one defence battle. That's very little data per update, and probably why progress is noisy.

[2026-10-22 23:18] AI: Short answer: **yes, more battles per epoch will help most**; lowering the learning rate might help a little; the model is not the problem yet. The model-vs-BattleAI numbers below come from playing the epoch-527 model directly, not from the training log.

## What the results show

**1. The attack side has genuinely learned to win, and better than BattleAI does.**
- Our Castle beats BattleAI's Inferno in **9 of 10** sampled battles, and its single most-likely line of play also wins (+2.04).
- BattleAI playing Castle against the same opponent loses **12 of 12**, across 12 different game seeds.
- So the model found a way to win that the default AI doesn't. Caveat: it's on a fixed seed, so it may be a memorized line rather than general skill, which is exactly what this overfitting test is for.

**2. The defence is winnable; we just haven't found how.**
- BattleAI playing Inferno beats BattleAI's Castle **12 of 12**, so the defence side is actually the easier one for a good player.
- Our model's defence loses every battle (0/10 sampled, and its most-likely play loses too), and its entropy is falling, so it's settling into a losing strategy.
- That's an exploration problem, not a capacity problem.

**3. The noise is mostly real behaviour.**
- The logged reward comes from *sampled* play: a 90% winner scores about +2.3 or about −0.7 depending on the battle, so the curve jumps between two values.
- On top of that there's genuine instability: the attack's win rate rose to 7/50, collapsed to 0/50 for 50 epochs, then recovered. That's the classic sign of updates too big for the data behind them: 4 gradient steps on 2 battles.

**4. The value loss can't be read as it is.** With cross-entropy on soft (0..1) labels, the loss can't go to 0: its minimum depends on the labels themselves. So "0.51 → 0.62" doesn't mean the value head got worse. I should log the value error in reward units instead.

## Your three questions

- **More battles per epoch, batch still 64: yes, this is the main fix.**
  - Each update averages over many battles instead of 2, which directly targets the collapse in point 3.
  - The epoch gets several mini-batches instead of reusing one batch 4 times.
  - The defence gets far more chances to stumble onto a win.
  - The M4 Pro has 14 cores and the battles run in parallel, so 4 per side (8 total) should cost about 3–5 s per epoch instead of 2.8 s. I'd estimate rather than promise that.
- **Lower learning rate:** it would reduce the instability, but more slowly than more data. I'd first add two measurements of how far each update moves the policy (the average KL divergence and the share of clipped decisions). Then lower it to 1e-4 only if steps are still too large with more data.
- **Changing the model:** no evidence it's the bottleneck. A 239k-parameter model learned to beat BattleAI on attack, where BattleAI itself fails. Revisit once we move to varied battles.

## What I'd change

1. **4 battles per side per epoch** (8 total), each with a different sampler seed; batch size stays 64.
2. **Clean evaluation:** every 10 epochs, one most-likely-action battle per side. With fixed seeds that's an exact measure of what the policy does, a flat line until it changes, which removes the noise from the curve you're judging by.
3. **Extra logging:** step size (KL and clip fraction), plus value error in reward units.
4. **Learning rate:** unchanged for now.

[2026-10-22 23:28] AI: Yes, it's a real issue, and the log shows it clearly. It's also starting to recover right now:

| Epochs | Attack wins | Mean reward |
|---|---|---|
| 491–530 | **37/40** | +2.0 |
| 531–570 | 22/40 | +1.1 |
| 571–610 | 5/40 | 0.0 |
| 611–650 | **0/40** | −0.3 |
| 651–710 | 4/60 | −0.2 |
| 711–729 | 11/19 | +1.1 (relearning) |

So it learned to win reliably, lost it within about 80 epochs, stayed lost for about 100, and is only now finding it again. That's the classic instability of small-data PPO, known as "policy collapse". It's not a problem with the game or the reward.

**Likely causes** (inferred from the data; I didn't log enough to prove each one):

1. **Too little data behind each update.** Every epoch makes 4 gradient steps from just 2 battles, and nothing limits how far the policy moves in total over many epochs. The clipping only limits each single step relative to the previous one.
2. **The two sides share one network and interfere.** While the attack was winning about 90% of the time, its decisions had advantages near 0: the value head already expected a win, so there was little left to learn. The defence always lost, so its large advantages dominated every update. Those defence-driven updates changed the shared network, and that likely degraded the attack. The rescaling over the whole epoch makes this worse, because it standardizes both sides together.
3. **It became nearly deterministic in a bad mode.** Entropy fell to about 0.38 during the losing streak. Since the battle is fully repeatable, the model then saw the same losing battle every epoch, and its value head learned to expect it, leaving little advantage signal to climb back out. Recovery came after entropy rose again (1.37 at epochs 651–670).

**What I'd do** (stop this run and restart):

1. **4 battles per side per epoch**, with batch size still 64. Each update is then backed by 4× the data, and both sides are always well represented.
2. **Limit how far each epoch can move the policy:** stop an epoch's PPO passes once the average KL divergence to the policy that played exceeds about 0.02. That's standard PPO practice ("target KL"), and it directly prevents "learned then lost" jumps. It's a better tool than a lower learning rate; we can still lower the rate if needed.
3. **Rescale advantages per side** (attack separately from defence), so one side's large advantages can't drown out the other's.
4. **Evaluate every 10 epochs** with one most-likely-action battle per side, and keep a **best checkpoint**, so a collapse can never cost the best model found.
5. **Log** the KL, the share of clipped decisions, and the value error in reward units, so we can see whether updates are too large instead of guessing.

The entropy bonus stays as is, since you said not yet. With 3 in place, the defence should also start learning.

Should I stop the run (now at epoch ~730) and restart with these five?

[2026-10-22 23:34] AI: I found a predictor, and the answer to your learning-rate question is **effectively yes**. What matters is the step size *relative to how much real signal is in each update*, and ours is far too large once the problem is solved. The checkpoint replay shows it:

| Epoch | Greedy play | Wins (last 20) | log p(winning line) | Weights moved since previous | Gradient signal (\|m\|/√v) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 300 | LOSS | 0/20 | −82 | 3.2 | 0.069 |
| 450 | WIN | 8/20 | −28 | 2.8 | 0.066 |
| **500** | **WIN** | **19/20** | **−2.6** (peak) | 2.8 | 0.061 |
| 550 | WIN | 11/20 | **−23** | 2.7 | **0.042** |
| 600 | LOSS | 0/20 | −72 | 2.2 | **0.031** |
| 650 | LOSS | 0/20 | −120 | 1.9 | 0.026 |
| 750 | WIN | 19/20 | −71 | 2.6 | 0.064 |
| 800 | LOSS | 10/20 | −174 | 2.5 | **0.019** |

"Gradient signal" is |m|/√v, the ratio of Adam's averaged gradient to its typical gradient size. Near 1 means every step points the same way; near 0 means the steps are mostly noise that cancels out.

**What's happening:**
1. **The weights move the same distance every 50 epochs**, about 2.5–3.2, whether the model is still learning or has already solved the attack (epoch 500). Adam takes steps of roughly the learning rate in size *no matter how small or noisy the gradient is*. So a solved problem doesn't make it stop.
2. **Once the attack is solved, its gradient is almost pure noise.** At about 0.03–0.04, roughly 97% of each step is noise. And because we rescale advantages to mean 0 / std 1, even a solved side whose true advantages are near zero gets its noise scaled up to full strength.
3. **So the weights random-walk away from the solution**, and the model forgets. That's your intuition from supervised learning, with an RL twist: about 50 noisy decisions per update makes the signal far weaker than in a normal supervised batch.

**What would have predicted it:**
- **How likely the model still finds a known winning line** peaked at epoch 500 and dropped to −23 by epoch 550, while training still showed 11/20 wins. It falls **before** the win rate collapses.
- **Gradient signal dropping below about 0.045** came at the same time, and it's at its lowest yet (0.019) at epoch 800, where wins have halved again. A second collapse looks underway now.
- **Weights still moving at full speed after the problem was solved** (no drop in that column after 500) is the underlying cause, and is visible immediately.

**What I'd do:**
1. **Log all three every epoch in TensorBoard:**
   - the gradient signal ratio (read from Adam's state);
   - how far the weights moved;
   - how likely the model still finds the best line seen so far.

   Add the raw advantage size per side, before rescaling.
2. **Lower the learning rate to 1e-4 and restart.** Success would mean the weights move less once the problem is solved, and no collapse. If it still drifts, the next fixes are stopping each epoch's passes once the policy has moved a set amount (target KL), and not rescaling a side whose raw advantages are tiny.

[2026-10-22 23:46] AI: The new run is training: 1000 epochs at Adam lr 1e-4, as `runtime/train/lr1e-4`. It started at about 2 s per epoch, so it should finish in about 35 minutes. The changes are committed and pushed (`6aa0724`), and all 43 tests pass.

**What changed:**
- **Reward scale:** rewards, labels and value heads are now on −1..1, where −1 is the worst possible reward and +1 the best. The value heads output tanh. This is cosmetic, not a learning change: tanh(z) = 2·sigmoid(2z) − 1, and I train on the equivalent cross-entropy, so the model and the gradients are the same as before. The contract is now v6, and AlphaBattleAI is rebuilt against it.
- **Optimizer:** Adam at 1e-4, not Muon. Muon also normalizes the step size, so it would still drift on noisy gradients once the scenario is solved, which is the failure we saw.
- **Seeded init:** the model's starting weights now come from a fixed seed. The old run wasn't seeded, so the two runs differ in their starting weights as well as the learning rate.
- **Old run converted:** I rewrote `old_lr3e-4`'s progress.csv and TensorBoard logs to the −1..1 scale (raw rewards are kept in their own columns), so both runs are on one scale. I restarted TensorBoard on localhost:6006.

**What to watch in TensorBoard:**
- `best_line_logp/attack` and `best_line_logp/defence`: the log-probability that the current model replays each side's best battle so far. The battles are deterministic given our moves, so exp(this) is the chance of replaying that battle. This is the early warning: in the old run it fell for about 100 epochs before the wins collapsed. A healthy run should push it toward 0 and keep it there.
- `stability/signal_ratio`: Adam's median |m|/√v. It is now bias-corrected, so it starts near 1 and isn't inflated in early epochs. If it drops toward ~0.02 after the scenario is solved, the steps are mostly noise.
- `stability/weight_change`, `approx_kl`, `clip_fraction`: how far each epoch's update moved the weights and the policy.
- `advantage_std/*` and `value_error/*`, per side: how much is left to learn, and how wrong the value estimate is.

**Disk use:** about 75 MB for the whole run. Most of that is 20 checkpoints at about 3 MB each. `best_lines.pt` adds about 3–8 MB and is rewritten only when a new best battle appears. Per-epoch battle records (about 10 MB) are deleted after each epoch.

Progress so far:
- **Bug fixed (C++, waiting for a rebuild):** creature spells could target a dead unit's slot. The engine then hit whatever live unit stood on that tile, so two different pointers did the same thing.

- **Design mistake fixed:** the retreat/surrender prior was −2, which made about 2 in 3 untrained battles end in giving up. It is now −6.
- **Added:** several battles per side per epoch, a generic `--set` flag, optional mask features for the model, optional self-imitation, and a checkpoint evaluator.
- **Verified:** PyTorch and ONNX agree exactly, the masks, reward, labels and distributions are correct, and battles are fully deterministic given the seeds.

[2026-10-23 01:32] AI: Your usage limit was reached, so I'm stopping here. The best solution is in: by epoch 100 the model wins both sides of the fixed scenario. Three experiments are still training and will stop on their own at 1000 epochs, around 04:35. TensorBoard is still running at localhost:6006.

## Best solution
**Go-Explore plus self-imitation**, run as `--set games_per_side=8 explore_from_best=3 ppo.self_imitation_weight=1.0 ai=AlphaBattleAIDev`:
- **Replay:** battles are fully deterministic given the VCMI seed and our actions; two identical games gave identical records. So replaying an action prefix restores any battle state. I added this to the AI (`ALPHA_BATTLE_PREFIX`). It removes the reason the vcmi-gym author gave up on search ("VCMI can't copy battle state").
- **Go-Explore:** 3 of each side's 8 battles per epoch replay a random-length opening of that side's best battle so far, then play on.
- **Self-imitation:** an extra loss raises the probability of the best battle found so far.
- **Results (E3, epoch 100):**
  - Playing greedily, it wins both sides.
  - Sampled at temperature 1, it wins 13/16 per side.
  - On battle seeds it never trained on, the attack still wins 16/18. The defence wins 0/18: it memorized one winning line.
- **On the corrected scenario (E5, map fix below):** the defence won 52% in epochs 51–100, the attack 81%.

## Bugs fixed (all committed and pushed)
1. **Map tool ignored the scenario's hero setup.** Heroes kept their hero type's starting skills: Caitlin had Wisdom + Intelligence, so 62 mana instead of 50. Calid also knew Haste. Fixed; every earlier run used the wrong scenario.
2. **Spells could target dead units.** VCMI hits the live unit standing on that tile, so the dead slot was just another name for it, splitting the model's probability.
3. **Enemy hero stats were hidden.** The encoder showed the enemy hero's skills and mana as 0, although the VCMI client shows them to a human. The fix is committed but not yet built into either AI library (both are in use by running experiments).

## Dumb things we did
- **Advantages normalized across both sides together.** One side's luck shifted the other side's learning signal, and the defence learned as if at a ~10× smaller rate. This is why the defence stalled once the attack started winning. Now normalized per side.
- **One battle per side per update** (about 40 decisions). With 8 per side (E2), the attack hit 97% by epoch 55, versus 55% at best after 600 epochs before.
- **Give-up prior of −2.** It compounds over ~40 decisions, so about 2 in 3 untrained battles ended in retreat or surrender. Now −6.
- **Records were ~75 files per decision.** At 16 battles per epoch that was about 35 GB/hour of SSD writes and ~2.5 cores spent on file events. Now one ~16 KB `.npz` per decision. I should have reported the data rate before starting E2.

## Other results
- **lr 1e-4 alone (E1):** the attack peaked at 55% wins, then fell back; the defence never won. Lowering the learning rate was not enough.
- **Pure PPO with the fixes (E2):** the defence is climbing (41% by epoch ~355), but the attack slipped from 96% to about 75%.
- **Data:** each 8-battles-per-side run writes about 6 MB per epoch, deleted after each epoch, and keeps about 70 MB (checkpoints and logs).

## Left to do
- **VCMI crash during game start-up**, about 1 in 1000+ games. It's a race between the start-up loop and the network thread in `CServerHandler::debugStartTest`, which in headless mode has no lock at all. The fix is designed but not written. Retries absorbed every occurrence so far.
- After the runs finish: rebuild both AI libraries (enemy-hero fix), evaluate the final checkpoints with `battle_model/evaluate.py`, and run the ablations on the fixed scenario (self-imitation only; pure PPO).
- `runner.finish` treats a crashed game as a clean finish; it should check the exit code.
- I left one 0.1 MB folder, `runtime/runs/20261022-235810-98116-castle_vs_inferno`, because the safety check blocked deleting it. I kept two crash folders in `runtime/runs` for debugging. Please delete the first; delete the crash folders once the start-up race is fixed.

[2026-10-23 07:24] AI: I found and fixed four bugs (three in our C++, one in VCMI) and three dumb design choices. The model now wins `castle_vs_inferno` consistently from both sides by about epoch 100, and stays there through epoch 1000.

## Best solution
Go-Explore plus self-imitation, on top of the fixes below:

```sh
.venv/bin/python -m battle_model.train --run NAME --set games_per_side=8 explore_from_best=3 ppo.self_imitation_weight=1.0
```

- **Go-Explore:** battles are fully deterministic given VCMI's seed and our actions (I checked: identical records twice). So the AI can now replay an opening of a recorded battle (`ALPHA_BATTLE_PREFIX`), which restores any state. 3 of each side's 8 battles per epoch replay part of that side's best battle so far, then play on from there.
- **Self-imitation:** an extra loss makes the best battle found so far more likely.
- **Final run (E6), all fixes, corrected scenario, after 1000 epochs:** greedy play wins both sides. Sampled at temperature 1, it wins 32/32 attacks and 31/32 defences. From fresh starts the win rates held at about 95% (attack) and 85–91% (defence) from epoch 100 to 1000, with no forgetting.

## Bugs fixed (all committed and pushed, including the VCMI fork)
1. **The scenario wasn't the one we wrote.** The map tool left VCMI's "use defaults" markers on the heroes. Both got their starting skills (Caitlin had 62 mana instead of 50) and Calid also knew Haste. Every run before 01:40 used this wrong scenario.
2. **The enemy hero was invisible.** The encoder showed its skills and mana as 0, while the VCMI client shows them to a human in battle. The model now sees what a human sees.
3. **Spells could target dead units.** VCMI hits the live unit standing on that tile, so the dead slot was only an alias for it.
4. **A VCMI bug:** a data race in `--testmap` start-up crashed about 1 in 1000 headless games (15 times tonight; retries absorbed it). Headless mode had no lock at all. After the fix: 0 crashes in about 40,000 games.
5. **Runner fixes:** a crashed game counted as "finished", and a retry could overwrite records of games still running. Both fixed.

## Dumb things we did
- **Joint advantage normalization across attack and defence.** Each side's luck shifted the other's learning signal, and the defence learned as if at a ~10× smaller rate. That's why the defence stalled once the attack started winning. Now normalized per side.
- **One battle per side per update** (about 40 decisions). Standard PPO uses about 50× more.
- **Give-up prior of −2.** It compounds over about 40 decisions, so about 2 in 3 untrained battles ended in retreat or surrender. Now −6.

## Experiments
| Run | What changed | Attack wins | Defence wins |
|---|---|---|---|
| E1 | lr 1e-4 only | peaked 55%, then forgot | 0% |
| E2 | the fixes above + 8 battles per side | 98% | 83% (takes ~500–800 epochs) |
| E7 | + self-imitation only | 93% | **0%** (locks onto the best *losing* line) |
| E9 | + Go-Explore only | 95% | 65%, unstable |
| E5/E6 | + both | 95–97% | 89–91% by epoch ~100, stable to 1000 |
| E8 | plain PPO, random seed per battle | 87%, 16/16 on unseen seeds | 14%, 0/16 on unseen seeds |

Go-Explore finds the defence win, and self-imitation makes the policy play it from the start; neither works well alone.

## Open problem
The attack generalizes: trained on random seeds, it beats BattleAI-Inferno on seeds it has never seen. The defence doesn't (0/16 on unseen seeds); with a fixed seed it only memorized one winning line. That is the real next challenge. Directions worth trying: Go-Explore per seed over a seed pool, more battles, feeding the legal-move masks into the model as features (written, off by default), or imitating BattleAI/MMAI first.

## Housekeeping
- **Data:** each run keeps about 65 MB of checkpoints and logs (468 MB in total). Battle records now take about 16 KB per decision, about 19× less than before, and are deleted every epoch.
- **Process and files:** TensorBoard is still running at localhost:6006 with every run in it; stop it with `pkill -f tensorboard`. All leftover run folders are deleted.
- **README and memory:** the README documents the new options and `evaluate.py`. The findings are saved to memory for future sessions.

[2026-10-23 07:32] AI: Nothing is training. The only process running is TensorBoard at localhost:6006 (`pkill -f tensorboard` stops it), and there are no leftover games or run folders.

Most of the fixes carry over to training on real battles. The headline result does not: Go-Explore plus self-imitation memorized one battle per seed.

**Carries over to real data:**
- **The enemy hero is visible.** The model now sees the enemy hero's skills and mana, as a human does. This barely mattered in the fixed scenario, where both heroes are identical, but it matters a lot in real games.
- **Spells can no longer target dead units,** so the model's spell pointer always names the unit actually hit.
- **The VCMI start-up crash is fixed** (about 1 in 1000 headless games). This affects all headless training at scale.
- **Runner robustness:** crashed games are detected as failures, and a retry can no longer overwrite games still running.
- **Records are about 19× smaller and one file per decision.** Real-data training needs this to scale; the old format cost about 35 GB an hour of SSD writes at 16 battles per epoch.
- **Several battles per PPO update.** This is the single biggest stability gain, and it's basic PPO practice.
- **The give-up prior of −6** stops untrained models ending most battles by retreating. It's only a starting point; the model can still learn to retreat when that pays.
- **Advantages normalized per task, not across tasks.** The principle carries over, but the current code groups by side only. Real data, with many battle types of very different reward scale, will need finer groups or a running per-task scale (PopArt-style).
- **Fixes to our scenario map tool** (heroes now get exactly what the scenario specifies) apply to any scenario we build. Real campaign maps define their own heroes, so this one is ours only.
- **Tooling:** `evaluate.py`, `--set` overrides, the dev AI library, per-run maps, and the extra diagnostics (KL, clip fraction, Adam signal ratio, per-side win rates).

**Doesn't carry over as is:**
- **Go-Explore plus self-imitation.** It replays and imitates one recorded battle, so it only works for a fixed seed. The defence it learned won 0/18 on seeds it never trained on.
- **The best-line log-probability warning signal,** for the same reason.
- **Lowering the learning rate** was never shown to help in general.

**What a real-data-like run showed:** E8 gave every battle its own random seed and used only the transferable fixes with plain PPO. The attack learned a general strategy: it won 16/16 battles on held-out seeds. The defence only reached 14% (0/16 held-out), so generalizing the defence is the open problem.

Battles being deterministic given the seed should also carry over, as an enabler rather than a method: when training on generated battles, any state can be restored by replaying its actions, so search (MCTS or Go-Explore per seed) is possible despite VCMI having no state copy. That's my best candidate for the defence problem, ahead of more PPO tuning.

[2026-10-23 07:44] AI: ## Spells that target corpses
My earlier rule was only half right, so I've replaced it.
- **The old rule** excluded any dead unit lying under a living one. For Resurrection and Animate Dead that happened to match VCMI, which itself forbids reviving a corpse any unit stands on.
- **Sacrifice broke it.** VCMI lets Sacrifice revive a corpse an enemy stands on (it only can't revive one under our own living unit), and my rule wrongly blocked that.
- **The new rule asks the engine.** A slot is a legal target only if VCMI says the cast is allowed and that the cast actually affects that unit. No per-spell special cases, so it follows each spell's own rules.
- **The sacrificed body is handled by the engine:** VCMI removes the victim completely, so its slot disappears and it can't be targeted later.
- **Tested** with a scenario giving both heroes Resurrection, Animate Dead, Sacrifice and Lightning Bolt, plus undead units. Over 24 games all four spells were cast (Resurrection 20, Animate Dead 6, Sacrifice 9, Lightning Bolt 43) with zero rejections. Lightning Bolt was never legal on a corpse, and Sacrifice's first target was always a corpse.

## What those two things were
- **Go-Explore plus self-imitation.** Battles are deterministic given the seed, so the AI can replay the first N actions of a recorded battle and reach exactly that position. Each epoch, 3 of each side's 8 battles replayed a random-length opening of that side's best battle so far, then played on from there. Self-imitation was an extra loss that made the best battle found so far more likely.
- **The best-line log-probability.** It was a warning signal: the log-probability that the current model replays its best recorded battle move for move.

Both only make sense when the same battle can happen again, which only holds with fixed seeds. You're right that they don't belong in training that has to work on real battles. I've removed them from the trainer, and the README no longer recommends them. The replay capability stays in the C++ as a building block for search, but training doesn't use it.

## Did we make it?
Yes for plain PPO, and it isn't yet confirmed on the final code.

**Already shown:** E2 used plain PPO with only fixes that carry over to real training: advantages normalized per side, 8 battles per side per update, the give-up prior at −6, and the bug fixes. It learned both sides of the fixed scenario: 98% attack and 83% defence wins in its last 100 epochs, and greedy play wins both. Nothing in that setup depends on fixed seeds.

**Not yet shown:** E2 ran before three later fixes: the corrected map, the visible enemy hero, and the engine-based spell rule. The best results I reported last time (E5/E6) depended on the tricks I just removed, so they don't count as evidence.

**Running now (E10):** the trimmed trainer, plain PPO with default settings, on the final code and the corrected scenario. It takes about 1.7 hours. I'll report its win rates and a greedy and sampled evaluation when it finishes.

Plain PPO with a random seed for every battle (E8) is still the honest picture beyond this test. The attack generalized (16/16 wins on unseen seeds); the defence did not (14% in training, 0/16 on unseen seeds).

**How the run works:**
- **Training:** each epoch plays 8 battles with our model on both sides, half on the attack seed and half on the defence seed, and learns from both sides' records. That's about 800 decisions per epoch, at about 8 s per epoch.
- **Eval:** every 10 epochs, plus once before training as a baseline, an eval round plays BattleAI on the fixed seeds: 8 sampled battles and 1 greedy battle per side. Eval battles are never trained on.
- **Cost:** about 2.3 hours for 1000 epochs, roughly 14 MB of records per epoch (deleted each epoch), and about 70 MB kept.

**What to watch in TensorBoard (run `selfplay`):**
- `eval/won/attack`, `eval/won/defence`: win rate against BattleAI. These are the lines that answer "are we getting better".
- `eval/won_greedy/*` and `eval/reward/*`: the greedy battle, and rewards on the −1..1 scale.
- `won/*` and `reward/*`: self-play stats only. The attack side's win rate against itself mostly reflects how balanced the two policies are, not how strong they are.

**So far (epoch 10):** against BattleAI it has won nothing yet at epoch 0 or 10, as expected this early. In self-play the attacker wins 75–88%.

**Fixed now (all were hand-made rules where the engine has its own):**
- **Hypnosis (a real bug).** Melee and shooting targets compared battle sides instead of asking the engine. When our hero hypnotized an enemy stack, that stack was offered *our own* units as targets, not its former allies. The server doesn't check ownership for melee, so those attacks really happened. Melee targets now use the engine's `battleCanAttackUnit`. Note that VCMI still lets our units attack a hypnotized enemy, even while it acts for us.
- **Shooting** now uses the engine's `battleCanShoot` alone. Our extra side check would also have blocked berserk units.
- **Long-weapon attacks:** the server allows them from where the unit stands, and our masks never offered them. No vanilla creature has this, so it only matters with mods.
- **The model's "ours" flag** was computed from battle sides. It now comes from the engine's owner (`battleGetOwner`), via a new input; this changed the contract to v7. The battle side stays as a second flag, so the model can see that a unit is hypnotized.
- **Tested:** 24 games with Hypnotize on both sides produced 58 turns of hypnotized units under our control. There were 0 targets breaking the engine's rule, 0 server rejections, and C++ and PyTorch outputs matched.

**Already asking the engine:** move hexes, shooting legality, retreat, whether surrender is possible, whether the hero can cast, all spell targets, hex accessibility, obstacles and turn order.

**Still our own rules, and why:**
1. **Wait only once per turn.** The server doesn't check this, but the client's Wait button uses exactly this rule, so it matches what a human can do.
2. **Attacks from a stopping hex (quicksand, moat) are excluded.** The server treats such an attack as just a move, because the attack is aborted when the unit lands there. The engine has no query that answers this before moving.
3. **Surrender requires gold ≥ cost + 2500.** This is the one deliberate design choice, not an engine rule: the engine only needs gold ≥ cost, and we added the price of rehiring the hero. **Do you want to keep it?**

One more gap, which is a limit of our action space rather than a bug: shooting at an empty hex is legal in VCMI for area attackers (e.g. Magogs), but the model can only shoot at units.

**Self-play:** I restarted the self-play run on the fixed code, because a v7 library can't be swapped in under a v6 run. That lost about 45 minutes. It's at epoch 3, at about 8.5 s per epoch, under `selfplay` in TensorBoard. The earlier attempt is kept as `selfplay_old_masks`.

[2026-10-23 08:09] AI: Yes, training can avoid files entirely, but that alone won't make it faster. Records are written and read back within seconds, so macOS serves them from RAM anyway. What's slow is the record *format* and the training step, not the disk. The real disk cost is wear: about 13 MB per epoch, roughly 5–6 GB of SSD writes an hour. A RAM disk, or streaming records to the trainer over a pipe, would remove that.

Here is where a self-play epoch's time goes, measured while the run was also using the CPU:

| Phase | Time | Share |
|---|---|---|
| PPO update | 4.6 s | ~50% |
| Playing 8 battles in parallel | 2.5 s | ~27% |
| Reading records | 2.0 s | ~22% |
| ONNX export | 0.2 s | ~2% |

The eval rounds against BattleAI add about 4% on top.

What would speed it up, best value first:
1. **One record file per battle instead of per decision.** Reading currently means about 65,000 small compressed arrays per epoch (about 75 per decision). The AI can keep a battle's decisions in memory and write them once at the end, either as a single file or streamed to the trainer. It also doesn't need to save the model's outputs: Python can recompute them in one batched pass, and they already match exactly. Reading would drop from about 2 s to about 0.2 s.
2. **Train on the Apple GPU (MPS).** It's 1.3–1.7× faster per step (66 vs 83 ms at a 64-decision minibatch, 163 vs 269 ms at 256), and it leaves the CPU cores free for VCMI games. It needed one small fix (a tensor created on the CPU), now committed and pushed.
3. **Play the next epoch's battles while the current one trains.** That hides most of the 2.5 s. The battles would come from a model one update older, which PPO handles correctly because each record holds the probabilities of the model that actually played.
4. **Keep VCMI processes alive between battles.** Each battle starts a fresh VCMI process, and about 0.9 s of each game is start-up. This needs an engine change, so it's the biggest job.

Items 1–3 should take an epoch from about 8.5 s to about 3 s, roughly 2.5–3× faster. Want me to implement them in that order and then restart the self-play run? The current run keeps going until then.

[2026-10-23 09:07] AI: Done: the efficiency changes are in, and the battle copy that MCTS needs is built and tested. Everything is committed and pushed.

## Efficiency (committed)
- **Training data no longer touches the disk.** The AI keeps each battle's record in memory and sends it to the trainer over a local socket when the battle ends. That was about 13 MB written and deleted per epoch, roughly 5–6 GB an hour. Records streamed this way are byte-identical to the old files, and writing files is still available for debugging (`--set keep_records=true`).
- **Torch trains on the Apple GPU.** Each epoch's decisions are copied to the GPU once instead of once per minibatch, and loss logging no longer forces a sync every step.
- **Battles are produced in the background.** A producer thread plays the next epoch's battles on the CPU while the GPU trains on the current one. That's the "dataloader creates the data" idea. The torch DataLoader still only cuts minibatches; moving battle generation into its worker processes would add a copy of about 100 MB per epoch between processes and gain nothing, since VCMI already runs as separate processes.
- **Result:** a 4-battle self-play epoch takes 2.2–2.7 s instead of about 6 s, and the trainer waits 0 s for battles.
- **What's slow now:**
  - **Training itself is the bottleneck.** A bigger minibatch would use the GPU about 2× better, but it changes the optimization, so I left it alone.
  - **Each VCMI game still writes about 0.1 MB of logs** to a temporary folder, about 1–2 MB per epoch. Removing that means changing our runner's stall detection, which watches that log grow.
  - **Each VCMI game spends about 0.9 s starting up.**

## MCTS: the battle copy is built and tested
**Alternatives I ruled out:**
- `fork()` of the game: VCMI is multithreaded and a fork keeps only one thread.
- Snapshotting the live game: the clients hold pointers into the battle, so swapping it is fragile.
- VCMI's own copy-on-write battle (`HypotheticBattle`): the server's rules read units in a way it doesn't cover, so they'd see stale units.
- Replaying from the start with the fixed seed: works, but costs a VCMI start-up (about 1–2 s) per try.

**What works:** a private copy of the battle that VCMI's own server rules run on, inside our AI. No network, no clients, and no hack of the live game, so it also works when our AI plays a real game.

**Stage 1 (engine refactor):** the server's battle rules now talk to a small interface instead of the game handler. Replaying 24 fixed-seed battles (882 decisions, including Hypnotize, Resurrection, Animate Dead, Sacrifice and self-play) gives byte-identical records before and after.

**Stage 2 (the simulator):** a new library method, `CGameState::cloneWithBattle`, copies the game state, the battle and each unit's current state. The copy applies packs with VCMI's own code and rolls its own dice. A check mode in our AI plays each real action in a copy first, then compares after the real game applies it. Over 1,244 actions:
- 0 actions refused by the copy;
- 0 mismatches in positions, flags, shots, retaliations, spell effects or hero mana;
- count and health differ only after melee and shots, which is expected since the copy rolls its own dice.

A copy takes 3.4 ms and an action 0.05 ms.

**Not covered yet:**
- The turn flow (morale, new rounds) runs on the server's own code but isn't compared against the real game.
- Sieges aren't supported.
- On big maps a full game-state copy will be slower, so the copy should then be battle-only.

**Next (not started):**
- **Stage 3, a search API.** Copy, encode with our existing encoder (it works on copies unchanged), legal moves, apply, terminal check, reward, plus a small connection to Python so the search code lives in Python.
- **Stage 4, AlphaZero-style MCTS.** It would use our network's priors and values and train on visit counts.

The design doc with all of this is `docs/battle_search.md`.

## Engine changes, labelled
- **Bug fix:** the headless start-up race (from earlier).
- **New, no behaviour change:** the battle-rules interface and `CGameState::cloneWithBattle`.

## Self-play run
It's at epoch 665 of 1000, about 50 minutes left. Against BattleAI the attack wins 98% of eval battles (greedy 7/7 in the last evals), but the **defence wins 0%**. In self-play the attacker wins almost every battle, so the defending side rarely sees a win to learn from.

[2026-10-23 10:02] AI: ## 1. The whole epoch on the GPU
You're right, it doesn't scale.
- **NVIDIA:** that part is fine. The code picks the device automatically (cuda, then mps, then cpu) and has nothing Apple-specific.
- **Memory:** an epoch today is about 400–900 decisions at about 60 KB each, so 25–50 MB on the GPU. With real rollouts it would grow without bound.
- **Where the 2× came from:** it didn't come from keeping data on the GPU. The copy to the GPU was only 12 of 141 ms per minibatch. The win was skipping the per-minibatch collate, the DataLoader overhead and the per-step syncs. So a bounded design keeps most of the gain.

Plan:
- **Battles come from an IterableDataset running in DataLoader worker processes.** That's where "generate the next battles on demand" belongs. Tensors come back through shared memory, so there are no big copies, and a bounded prefetch keeps RAM bounded. This replaces my background thread, which is the same thing with one worker. PPO needs a whole rollout before minibatching, to normalize advantages per side, so the dataset hands over one rollout at a time. With k workers the data can be up to k updates stale. PPO handles a small k, because each record holds the probabilities of the model that played it; a large k would need V-trace (the off-policy correction from IMPALA).
- **Compact data types.** Most inputs fit in int16, and the masks can be booleans, so a decision drops from about 60 KB to about 10–15 KB.
- **Minibatches go to the GPU one at a time,** from tensors collated once, using pinned memory and asynchronous copies on CUDA. GPU memory is then bounded by one minibatch, with an optional cache only when a rollout fits a memory budget.

## 2. Checkpoints
Done: training now keeps only `checkpoint_latest.pt`. I deleted the checkpoints and model files of every run we've moved on from, which freed 518 MB (`runtime/train` went from 558 to 40 MB). The running self-play process still has the old setting loaded, so it will write one 3 MB file every 50 epochs; I'll delete those when it finishes.

## 3. Getting the battle copy exactly right
The copy runs VCMI's own battle code, so the risk is in what the copy carries and assumes. Known gaps today:
- **Luck and morale:** VCMI 1.7 tracks a per-army streak state for these rolls, and the copy starts that state fresh, so its odds differ from the real game.
- **Charged artifacts:** their charges aren't used up in the copy.
- **Sieges:** gate state changes are skipped.
- **Tactics phase:** untested.
- **Turn flow:** who acts next and morale turns aren't compared yet.

Your plan is right. I'd make the check stricter by running it **on the server, exactly**. With a debug setting, before each real action the server copies the battle *and its own random generators* into a simulation and plays the action there. Then it plays the action for real and compares everything bit for bit: every unit, obstacles, mana, the round, who acts next, and the result. With identical dice there's no "it was just dice" excuse: any difference is a bug, found where it happens.

This needs the simulator moved into the server library, plus a small debug hook there, as a labelled engine addition. The check roughly doubles the server's work per action, so it stays off in training and real play.

The battles for it come from your idea: random encounters with the AI playing. That means random factions, armies, heroes, skills, artifacts, spells and terrain, plus sieges, war machines and tactics. Run thousands of them with the check on, and fix every mismatch immediately.

## 4. Maps
Agreed, the map doesn't matter. A battle depends only on:
- the two heroes (artifacts, skills, specialty, mana);
- their armies;
- the defended town, in a siege;
- player-wide and global bonuses;
- the player's gold, for surrender.

So:
- **Copy the game state once,** when the battle starts. The cost doesn't matter there.
- **Search snapshots copy only the battle part:** units, obstacles, round, mana, and the dice state. That makes their cost independent of the map. A minimal "battle world" (heroes, armies, town and bonuses only) can replace the one-time copy later.
- **World-map information** (e.g. a well nearby) goes in as a short vector at battle start.

## Revised plan, in order
1. **Data pipeline:** IterableDataset rollouts, compact types, bounded GPU memory, working on CUDA, MPS and CPU.
2. **Simulator in the server library:** the exact server-side check and battle-only snapshots.
3. **Mass checking:** a random-encounter generator, mass checks, and fixes until there are zero mismatches. That includes luck/morale state, sieges, tactics, war machines and charged artifacts.
4. **Search:** the search API, then MCTS.

[2026-10-23 10:49] AI: You're right: those were symptoms, not causes. A dense kill reward should keep improving the defence whether or not it wins, so something is blocking that signal. Two candidate mechanisms fit the data, and each can be tested:
1. **No per-decision credit.** If the value estimate is flat, the advantage of each decision is just "how this battle went versus average", shared by all ~25 decisions of the battle. The effect of one decision ("attack instead of waiting") is then tiny next to the battle-to-battle noise, and the gradient can't find it.
2. **Exploration collapse.** If passive actions are near-certain, the alternatives are rarely tried, so there's no data showing they're better.

Testing both on the current checkpoint:
- How much of the reward variation the value head actually predicts, per side.
- How confident the passive choices are.
- A counterfactual. Battles are deterministic, so I can replay the greedy defence up to each WAIT/DEFEND, force an attack there instead, let the model play on, and see whether the outcome is better.

[2026-10-23 10:57] AI: I found the cause of the plateau, fixed it, and started a controlled test to confirm it. The test isn't done yet.

**The cause.** I measured how the value head's estimate changes between consecutive decisions in self-play, and regressed that on what happened in between:
- In the defence, the estimate dropped by 0.57 per fraction of its own army lost, and also dropped by 0.21 per fraction of the enemy army killed.
- For a lost battle (the defence lost 23 of 24) the honest numbers are about 0 and +0.47: only kills count toward the reward.

So the training signal told the defence to avoid exchanges. That is where the passivity comes from (42% WAIT/DEFEND).

The source is the chained value design. The final head added the round-horizon heads' outputs, and their targets count our surviving army, so the final head inherited their swings. The decision-to-decision changes of the final estimate correlated 0.63–0.67 with those of V1/V2, and were about 4× larger than the variation in actual outcomes.

It became flat rather than starting flat because, once both sides played consistently, outcomes varied little, and this bias was what remained in the signal. Normalization then scaled it up to full-size updates.

My first version of this check was confounded by GAE's time weighting. The untrained model also showed "negative credit for kills" under it. The version above uses the value estimate's own changes and doesn't have that problem.

**Your idea.** The policy already maximized only the final value, and V0–V2 were already just auxiliary losses. The leak was the chaining between the heads. So the fix is your idea: the final head now stands alone, and V0–V2 are purely auxiliary. That's committed.

**The test, to prove the cause rather than assume it.** Three self-play runs from scratch on the same seeds, 1000 epochs each:

| Run | Final value head | Auxiliary value-loss weights |
|---|---|---|
| A (control) | Chained, the old design | Equal |
| B | Stand-alone | Equal |
| C (your proposal) | Stand-alone | 0.1, with 1 on the final head |

All three log, every epoch:
- `credit_<side>/value_ev`: how much of the outcome the value head predicts;
- `credit_<side>/credit_own_lost` and `credit_killed`: what the value head's changes reward;
- the action shares and the evals against BattleAI.

If A's defence shows the same biased credit and plateaus, while B and C show honest credit and keep improving, the cause is confirmed. The runs started at about 10:55 and should take 2–2.5 hours with all three sharing the machine.

**Run names.** New run folders are named `<start time>-<name>`, and I renamed all the old ones the same way, so name order is time order. `--resume` finds the newest run with a given name. If TensorBoard still lists the old names, a restart of it will clear them.

But TensorBoard sorts run names ascending, so the newest run is at the **bottom**, not the top. To put it on top there are three options:

1. **Sort descending in TensorBoard:** click the "Run" column header in the runs table. TensorBoard remembers the choice in the browser. Names stay readable, and nothing changes on disk.
2. **An inverted time prefix**, e.g. `7973-...`, which counts down as time goes up. Newest-first in any ascending sort, but the dates become unreadable in the name.
3. **Archive finished runs** into a folder outside TensorBoard's log directory, so it only shows the runs we're working on. That also keeps the list short.

[2026-10-23 11:11] AI: Not everything. The hardest piece, a fast and correct copy of a battle, exists. But several technical parts are missing, and a few design decisions are yours to make.

**What I have:**
- **A battle copy running VCMI's own rules.** It was checked against the real game on 1,244 actions: 0 mismatches outside dice. Copying takes 3.4 ms and an action 0.05 ms.
- **The encoder works on copies, for either side.** So the network can give priors (policy) and leaf values (value head) for any simulated state.
- **The model already runs in C++ (ONNX),** at about 3 ms per evaluation.
- **Determinism:** with fixed seeds a battle can be replayed exactly, which helps with testing.

**Missing on the technical side** (I can do these):
1. **Verified correctness beyond what's tested.**
   - **Turn flow:** next unit, extra morale turns, new-round effects. It runs the server's code but has never been compared against the real game.
   - **Luck and morale streak state:** VCMI keeps per-army streak state for these rolls, and the copy doesn't carry it, so its odds differ from the real game.
   - **Untested or unsupported:** sieges, the tactics phase, war machines and charged artifacts.
   - **Checking tools:** the exact server-side check (same dice) and the random-encounter generator that would find the rest.
2. **Cheaper copies.** Today each copy includes the whole game state. A tree needs a state per node or per simulation, and I haven't measured a copy's memory. A copy of just the battle (plus mana and dice state) would fix both cost and memory.
3. **Speed.** About 3 ms per network evaluation at batch size 1. With 200 simulations per decision that's 0.6–1 s per decision, so a 40-decision battle takes about half a minute per core. Evaluating several leaves together in one batch would help a lot.
4. **The search interface** (that's what I meant by "search API"): copy a node, encode it, list legal actions, apply one, step to the next decision, check for the end, read the reward. The pieces exist in C++ but aren't wired up as one interface, nor reachable from Python.
5. **Training from search.** Turning visit counts into a loss on our factorized policy (type → target → hex) is doable, but not written.

**Decisions I need from you:**
1. **Where the search algorithm lives.** In Python, which fits "science in Python", calling the C++ copy as a service with batched GPU evaluation? Or in C++ next to the ONNX model, which is faster but harder for you to review? I'd do Python plus a thin C++ service.
2. **What each side maximizes in the tree.** Our two sides' rewards aren't exactly zero-sum. Options:
   - each side maximizes its own reward, which is more faithful;
   - treat the battle as zero-sum, which is the simpler AlphaZero setup.
3. **Dice.** Either sample the dice anew in each simulation, which is simple and standard, or model them as explicit chance nodes, which is more exact and more expensive.
4. **The huge action space.** A decision often has hundreds of legal actions. I'd use the policy's priors to search only a sampled handful (Gumbel AlphaZero, designed for few simulations and large action spaces), rather than plain MCTS over everything.
5. **Opponents that aren't our network.** When we play BattleAI, the tree needs a model of its moves. Either our own network stands in for it (cheap and imperfect), or BattleAI itself runs inside the copy (exact, but slow and fiddly).
6. **Hidden information in real games.** The copy starts from our client's view. In a real game we wouldn't know things like the enemy's spellbook. Either the search assumes something about them, or we accept that it may "know" more than a player would.

**Before trusting it:** the plateau test (run B) should pass first. Search on top of a biased value head would amplify the bias, not fix it.

[2026-10-23 11:39] AI: Here is what the research changes about the plan:
- **Gumbel AlphaZero is the right choice.** It's the simplest search with *guaranteed* policy improvement at low simulation budgets, and it handles big action spaces by searching only a sampled top-k at the root. EfficientZero V2 reports Gumbel needing about half the simulations of Sampled MuZero, its main alternative.
- **Stochastic MuZero isn't needed.** It models dice explicitly with "afterstates" and chance nodes, which is exactly the complexity you said to avoid. Re-sampling the dice each simulation is simpler. A 2048 study combining Gumbel with a stochastic environment worked with only 3 simulations during training.
- **The training target is not visit counts.** With so few sampled actions, visit counts are a poor target. The target is Gumbel's "improved policy", and I'll keep that for when we train from search.

Sources: [Policy improvement by planning with Gumbel (ICLR 2022)](https://iclr.cc/virtual/2022/poster/6418), [EfficientZero V2](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2403.00564), [MiniZero comparison](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2310.11305), [On RL for 2048 (Gumbel + stochastic)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2212.11087), [LightZero benchmark](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2310.08348), [Multiagent Gumbel MuZero](https://ojs.aaai.org/index.php/AAAI/article/view/29121), [Sampled MuZero](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/hubert21a).

Your "each side maximizes its own reward" needs one model change. At a leaf, the tree needs *both* sides' values. Today the network gives only the side-to-move's value, so a second forward pass from the other side's view would make search **about 2× slower**, since network evaluation dominates. Instead I'll add a small extra head that predicts the opponent's final reward from the same pass, at about the same cost as now. That changes the model's inputs/outputs contract to v8 and needs training labels, which I can compute from the battle records.

The plan, in order, each piece tested; no full training runs, and the main AI library stays untouched while A and B run:
1. The hidden-information TODO in the model.
2. The opponent-value head, with its labels.
3. The reward in C++, with a parity test against `reward.py`. The search needs both sides' rewards at the end of a simulated battle.
4. The Gumbel search core in C++, plus the readable Python twin, with an equivalence test that fails if their logic drifts apart.
5. The battle environment for the search, wired into the AI, then a smoke test.

[2026-10-23 13:42] AI: I changed the default: the policy now follows V and V2 (`policy_heads=0,0,1,1`). All 55 tests pass, and it's committed and pushed. The comment next to the setting records the evidence and that it rests on one run per setting.

**Why the search didn't help, most likely cause first:**

1. **Dice noise gets amplified.** With 16–32 simulations, each candidate action gets only 1–8 tries, each with one random roll of damage, luck and morale. So the gap between two actions' scores is mostly luck. The search method I used rescales those scores at every node so the best equals 1 and the worst 0, then multiplies them by 5 to 9. That turns a lucky roll into a bigger preference than the network's own. That design assumes score differences are real, as in Go or chess. The evidence fits: more simulations made it worse (the multiplier grows with visits), and turning off the search's random noise didn't fix it.

2. **The search looks only one step ahead.** One simulation adds about one new position: 16 simulations used 17 network calls. So the search mostly compares the position right after our unit acts, before the enemy answers. The consequences (retaliation, the enemy's counter-attack) come only from V's guess.

3. **V is too rough to rank sibling moves.** V's error is about 0.12–0.18 on the −1..1 scale, while the positions after two candidate moves differ by much less than that. The policy, trained over thousands of decisions, ranks such moves better than one look at V does.

4. **The opponent in the tree is the wrong one.** The tree assumes BattleAI plays like our sampled network, and V learned only from self-play. This explains why search hurt against BattleAI but was neutral against our own network. It doesn't explain why search didn't help in that matchup; causes 1–3 do.

**How to confirm:** play search against our own network, which removes cause 4, in two variants:
- 4 candidate actions instead of 16, so each gets about 8 tries;
- scores kept in reward units instead of rescaled per node, which needs a setting in both the C++ and Python copies.

If search then beats the plain network, cause 1 is confirmed. The real fix would be to average several dice rolls per tried action before comparing. That is the dice question the one-line comment marks for revisiting.

Want me to run that test?

[2026-10-23 13:46] TOOLS: Bash×3

[2026-10-23 13:46] AI: The measurement shows the opposite of flooding: the numbers are drowned out, not dominant.

1. **Counts don't flood here.** In this scenario stacks are 1–15 units, so log count is 0.7–2.8. Even 30,000 imps would only reach 10.3.

2. **The numbers are a small part of each unit token.** A unit token is the sum of about six learned embeddings plus one projection of the numbers:

| Part of a unit token | Typical size per dimension |
|---|---|
| creature embedding | 0.99 |
| hex position embedding | 0.99 |
| "ours" embedding | 1.08 |
| token-kind embedding | 0.93 |
| all the numbers together | 0.79 |

Multiplying a stack's count by 10 moves the token by only 0.28, against a token of size about 2.4. PyTorch starts embeddings at std 1, and after 400 epochs they're still about that size. So the network mostly sees which creature is where, and only faintly how many there are and how hurt they are.

3. **Among the numbers, the stats that decide fights are about 10x weaker.** Log HP, damage and count arrive at 1.7–3.2. Attack, defence and speed (divided by 50, 50 and 20) arrive at about 0.3. The learned weights haven't scaled them up: each feature's weights are about the same size, so its influence is just its input size. Speed and the attack–defence gap are what decide who reaches whom and how much damage lands.

**What I'd change** (Python only, all in `_embed`):
- Bring every numeric feature to about the same scale with fixed constants. For example, log features divided by about 3, and attack, defence and speed rescaled so typical values are about 1.
- Start the embeddings small (std about 0.1 instead of 1) so the numbers aren't drowned at the start, or normalise each part before adding them.
- Keep the log-scaling: it fits H3, since damage and kills behave like ratios, and it keeps a 30,000-imp stack from swamping everything in other scenarios.

This changes the network, so old checkpoints wouldn't carry over in a meaningful way and it needs a fresh run to judge. Want me to make it, or is there more about the architecture you want to go through first? The network is also tiny: 64 dimensions and 2 attention layers.

- `g_active_unit=6`: the unit in slot 6 acts now.
- `g_hero_can_cast=1`: our hero may cast this turn.

**Units (`u_*`, 40 slots, 14 used):** each slot is one stack, and every field is one array indexed by slot.

| Slot | Creature × count | Side | Hex | HP (first/max) | Att/Def | Speed | Shots | Turn queue |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | Pikeman ×14 | 0 | 1 | 10/10 | 10/11 | 5 | 0 | 10 |
| 1 | Archer ×9 | 0 | 35 | 10/10 | 12/9 | 5 | 12 | 12 |
| … | … | | | | | | | |
| **6** | **Angel ×1** | 0 | 171 | 200/200 | 26/26 | 13 | 0 | **0 (acting now)** |

| … | … | | | | | | | |
| 13 | Devil ×1 | 1 | 185 | 160/160 | 24/26 | 11 | 0 | 1 |

- The other fields have the same layout: moved, waited, defending, morale, luck, retaliations, spell effects (`u_effect_*`, 8 per unit) and the next round's queue.

**Heroes (`h_*`, index 0 = attacker, 1 = defender):**
- Both have types 15 and 60, primary skills 5/5/5/5, and 50/50 mana.
- `h_spells[0]` is our spellbook: Lightning Bolt, Bless, Slow, Hypnotize, Blind and 5 more.
- `h_spells[1]` and `h_skills[1]` are all zeros: a player can't see the enemy's spellbook or skills.

**Hexes (`x_*`, 187 each):** accessibility per hex, and obstacle type, id and side. 8 hexes have obstacles here.

**Masks (`m_*`, booleans from the engine's legality checks):**
- `m_type`: wait, defend, move, melee, hero spell and retreat are legal; shoot isn't, because the Angel has no shots.
- `m_move_hex`: 114 hexes the Angel can reach.
- `m_melee_target`: only slot 13, the Devil.
- `m_spell`: 8 castable spells, with `m_spell_unit` and `m_spell_hex` giving each one's legal targets.
- `m_melee_from` (40×187): for each target, the hexes it can be attacked from.

The tensors are `int64` except the masks (`bool`). `forward` gets a batch of these, e.g. 64 such positions stacked during training, or 1 when the C++ AI plays.

[2026-10-23 14:22] AI: The numbers below come from VCMI's `config/gameConfig.json` and `lib/battle/DamageCalculator.cpp` in our fork.

**Attack and defence: only the difference counts, between the attacker's attack and the target's defence**

- **Attack higher (A > D):** each point of A − D adds 5% damage, capped at +400% (×5), which is reached at A − D = 80.
- **Defence higher (D > A):** each point of D − A removes 2.5% damage, capped at −70% (×0.3), which is reached at D − A = 28.
- **In full:**
  - damage = base × (1 + sum of attack factors) × product of (1 − each defence factor)
  - base = count × a random roll in [dmin, dmax]
  - The skill bonus is one attack factor, added to the others (luck, bless, archery, jousting, hate, …).
  - The skill penalty is one defence factor, multiplied with the others (armourer, shield spells, range penalty, …).
- **Kills:** the first creature dies when damage ≥ its remaining HP; then one more dies per full max HP, up to the stack's count.

**Luck: rolled on each attack**
- Chance of a lucky strike = [1, 2, 3][luck − 1] / 24, so 4.2%, 8.3% or 12.5% for luck +1, +2, +3.
- A lucky strike adds +1.0 to the attack factors: base damage again, on top of the other bonuses.
- **Bad luck is switched off** in this config (`badLuckChance: []`). In original H3 it halves the damage.

**Morale: rolled on each unit turn**
- **Good morale:** chance [1, 2, 3][morale − 1] / 24 (4.2%, 8.3%, 12.5%); after acting, the unit acts again at once.
- **Bad morale:** chance [2, 4, 6][|morale| − 1] / 24 (8.3%, 16.7%, 25%); the unit loses its turn. That's double original H3's 1–3/24.
- Morale and luck are capped at ±3. Non-living units (undead, elementals, war machines) ignore morale.
- **The rolls aren't independent.** Luck and morale use a "bias" of 8 that pushes the odds toward the stated chance based on recent rolls, which prevents streaks. So the true chance depends on hidden history; this is the "luck/morale streak state" still on our TODO list.

**What this means for your feature 2:**
- **An attack/defence ratio isn't what the game computes.** An Angel (26/26) and a Peasant (1/1) both have ratio 1. Against a target with defence 10, the Angel deals +80% damage and the Peasant −22.5%. The raw numbers matter through A − D.
- **The effect is per pair, not per unit.** It depends on who hits whom. The formula-based feature you mention should therefore be pairwise: for each attacker–target pair, the damage multiplier from the formula, or better, the expected kills (count × average damage × multiplier, against first HP and max HP).
- **Per unit,** keep raw attack and defence (scaled) so the network can work out differences itself. The ratios can be added if you want them as a "style" signal.
