[2026-10-23 14:58] AI: I checked the details these features depend on in VCMI's code, and found one encoder bug on the way. Then the design.

**Facts the features rest on (VCMI source):**
- **Damage roll:** a stack's damage range is count × dmin to count × dmax. The actual roll is the average of min(10, count) uniform rolls in that range, so big stacks deal close to the mean.
- **Morale:**
  - It can't chain: one extra turn per round at most (`hadMorale`).
  - There's no morale roll after waiting or defending.
  - Expected actions per round are 1 + p_good, or 1 − p_bad.
- **Luck:** a lucky strike adds 1.0 to the attack factors, so expected damage rises by about p_luck.
- **Bug in our encoder:** `u_morale` and `u_luck` are the raw sum of bonuses, not the game's effective value. The game clamps to ±3 and uses 0 for units morale doesn't affect, such as undead. So an undead stack under a +2 morale hero is encoded as +2 but really has 0. Fixing it is one line each in `Encoder.cpp`.

## Your proposals

| Proposal | Verdict |
|---|---|
| HP = first_hp / max_hp | Yes. |
| Ratio of current attack to defence, and of base attack to defence | Fine as cheap "style" features, but not what the game computes. The base ratio mainly helps rare creatures, whose embeddings are poorly trained. |
| log(1.05^att), log(0.975^def) | This is just 0.049·att, a linear rescale, not a multiplier. The game's multiplier is linear in A − D with caps, and only exists between an attacker and a specific target. Per unit I'd use 0.05·A and 0.025·D (one point = 5% or 2.5% damage), and put the real multiplier in the pair features. |
| Luck and morale as log(1 + p) or log(1 − p) | Yes, and it has a clean meaning: for morale, log(expected actions per round); for luck, log(expected damage multiplier). Use the effective values once the bug is fixed. |
| value / mean value − 1 | It's heavy-tailed: one 50-dragon stack among peasants gives 30+. Use log(value / mean value of the living stacks) instead: 0 is average, symmetric, about ±4. Add value / its side's total (0–1, "how much of its army this stack is"). Count the injured top creature as a fraction: value = cost × (count − 1 + first_hp / max_hp). |
| Damage = max damage / HP | Yes, as log(average damage / max HP): how many of its own kind one creature kills per hit. Use current damage, which includes Bless and Curse. Add the spread, (dmax − dmin) / (dmax + dmin). |

## The per-unit encoder

All values are about 0–1 or about ±2; there are no raw counts.

| Feature | Formula |
|---|---|
| health | first_hp / max_hp |
| value in the battle | log(value / mean value of the living stacks) |
| value in its army | value / its side's total value |
| damage per HP | log(average damage / max HP) |
| damage spread | (dmax − dmin) / (dmax + dmin) |
| attack, defence | 0.05·A, 0.025·D |
| buffs (hero skills, spells) | 0.05·(A − A_base), 0.025·(D − D_base) |
| style | log(A / D), log(A_base / D_base) |
| speed | min(speed, 15) / 15: share of the field crossed per turn |
| shots | min(shots, 12) / 12: share of a full quiver |
| morale | log(1 + p_good), or log(1 − p_bad) |
| luck | log(1 + p_luck) |
| turn order | position in this round's and the next round's queue, as a share of the units in it |
| unchanged | retaliations, casts, alive, moved, waited, defending, clone, summoned, two-hex, acting now |

**Constants:** base attack/defence and creature costs come from the vocabulary as tables built into the model. The combat constants (5% / 2.5%, the caps, the luck and morale tables) should be exported with the vocabulary from VCMI's config, so Python can't drift from the game.

## Pair features: use 40×40, not 7×7

- **Size:** we already have 40 unit slots. A battle can have more than 7 stacks per side (war machines, summons, clones), and a "first 7" rule would silently drop units. 40 × 40 pairs × 8 features is about 13k numbers per decision, smaller than the existing melee-from mask (40 × 187). All of it is computed inside the network from the existing inputs, so neither the C++ side nor the contract changes.
- **Features for attacker i and target j,** for living units on opposite sides (0 otherwise, plus a self/ally/enemy relation):
  1. log of the skill multiplier, log m(A_i − D_j), between log 0.3 and log 5;
  2. expected kill fraction: expected kills / count of j, from expected damage = count × average damage × multiplier, and the game's kill rule on first HP and max HP;
  3. p(wipe): the chance the damage covers j's total HP, using the roll's real spread (the average of min(10, count) rolls);
  4. the share of j's army value this hit destroys (the reward counts exactly that);
  5. retaliation: the kill fraction j's survivors inflict back, if j can retaliate and i is melee;
  6. turns to reach: hex distance / speed of i, capped at 3 (ignores obstacles);
  7. whether j acts before i this round;
  8. for shooters: the range penalty (×0.5 beyond 10 hexes) and the penalty for shooting in melee.
- **How the network uses them without confusing who is who:** pair (i, j) only ever enters where both units are already fixed:
  - **Attention bias** (as in Graphormer and AlphaFold): in every attention block, unit i's attention score for unit j gets a learned per-head term from (P_ij, P_ji, relation). It needs no row matching; it says "what I can do to j and what j can do to me".
  - **Target choice:** the melee and shoot logits for target j get a learned term from P[active unit, j]. That's exactly the "whom do I hit" decision.
  - **Per-unit summaries:** the best hit a unit has and the biggest threat it faces, added to its token. These help V and movement.

**Limits:** the formula ignores abilities: no-retaliation, double attack, hate, breath, death stare, jousting, magic resistance. It's a strong prior, not the truth; the creature embeddings learn the exceptions.

**Proposed order** (needs fresh training; each step compared with today's network on the greedy eval):
1. Split `_embed` into `normalize_units`, `normalize_heroes` and `normalize_battle_features` (the last two unchanged), with the new per-unit features and the morale/luck fix.
2. Add the pair features with the attention bias and the target-logit term.

Doing them as separate steps shows which one helped. Each formula gets a unit test on hand-made cases, e.g. Angel vs Devil: A − D = 0, so the multiplier is 1.0.

[2026-10-23 15:21] AI: Agreed on both points: a small fixed slot layout, and the active unit as the anchor for the cross features. Here's the design.

## Principle: ops first, trainable layers on top

The network gets the same intermediate quantities a hand-written AI computes: expected damage, kills, chance of a wipe, retaliation, threat. They're computed by exact tensor ops from the raw inputs and checked against the engine. Rules are explicit named terms with one weight each, added to the action logits. The trainable network adds its own scores on top.

So a rule model is the same network with the rule weights set and the rest untrained. Training then learns the exceptions rather than the basics.

## Slots (contract change)

- **10 slots per side, 20 in total** instead of 40.
- **Slots 0–9 hold the units we control, and slot 0 is always the acting unit.** That's how the current unit is easy to identify: it's always slot 0. The rest follow by value, descending.
- **Slots 10–19 hold the units the enemy controls**, by value, descending.
- **Grouping is by controller**, so a stack we hypnotised is ours.
- **Dead stacks come after living ones** within the cap, as targets for Resurrection and Animate Dead.
- **Sort by value, not turn order.** If something gets cut, it should be the least valuable stacks, and value order stays stable during a battle. Turn order stays a feature.
- **Legality is unaffected.** Units beyond 10 per side aren't encoded, so actions on them vanish from the C++ masks, and every action left is legal. Wait and defend always remain legal.
- **Other contract changes:** `g_active_unit` goes away, the per-unit tensors and masks shrink to 20, and the schema version goes up to 8. Records shrink by about half, and old checkpoints won't load.

## The functions

```python
def forward(self, x):
    stacks = stack_features(x)          # [B, 20, F] per stack: the normalised features we agreed on
    cross = cross_stack_features(x)     # ours→theirs [B, 10, 10, F] and theirs→ours; the acting unit = ours[0]
    heroes, battle = hero_features(x), battle_features(x)       # unchanged this turn
    context, tokens = self.trunk(stacks, summarize(cross), heroes, battle, hexes)   # trainable
    logits = self.heads(context, tokens)                        # trainable scores
    return self.rules(logits, cross, x)                         # + named rule terms, then the masks
```

**`cross_stack_features`:** your 8 features for every (ours i, theirs j) pair, plus the reverse direction for threats. Computed separately for melee and for shooting, because the multiplier, the range penalty and retaliation differ.
1. log of the skill multiplier, from A_i − D_j;
2. expected kill fraction;
3. p(wipe);
4. the share of j's army value destroyed;
5. retaliation kill fraction (melee only);
6. turns to reach;
7. j acts before i this round;
8. can shoot, with the penalties folded into the multiplier.

They use the game's own rules:
- expected damage = count × average damage × multiplier;
- the roll's spread is the average of min(10, count) uniform rolls;
- kills follow first HP, then max HP per creature;
- p(wipe) mixes a lucky strike (+1.0 to the attack factors) with probability p_luck.

**`rules`:** each rule is a named term over the acting unit's row, `cross[:, 0, :]`, so the 10 numbers line up exactly with enemy slots 10–19:

```python
wipe = self.w_wipe * cross.melee_wipe[:, 0]                          # [B, 10]: acting unit vs each enemy slot
logits.melee_target[:, 10:] += wipe
logits.type[MELEE] += masked_max(wipe, x["m_melee_target"][:, 10:])  # best legal target lifts the type
```

Shooting works the same way. The masked max carries a rule up the action hierarchy (type → target → hex): a type's rule score is the best rule score among its legal sub-actions. Masks are applied last, so illegal actions stay −inf.

## The test you described

With `w_wipe = 1000` and everything else untrained, the network must always attack the most wipeable stack when it can.
1. **Hand-made positions:**
   - an Angel next to a lone Imp and a Devil must melee the Imp;
   - with no wipe possible, the rule adds nothing;
   - a shooter must choose shoot when the wipe is a shot.
2. **Real recorded decisions:** whenever a legal target has p(wipe) > 0, the greedy action must attack the legal target with the highest p(wipe).
3. **Live in VCMI** with the exported ONNX model:
   - Checks the C++ slot layout end to end: every decision with a possible wipe attacked.
   - Checks p(wipe) itself: its predicted values must match how often the engine's dice actually wiped the stack. This tests the op against the game.

For training, `w_wipe` shouldn't start at 1000. A near-certain action has almost no gradient, so the policy could never learn the exceptions. It should be a learnable weight starting at a few nats; 1000 is for the rule test.

## Later, more ops of the same kind

The same pattern adds more of a hand-written AI's intermediate calculations, each used by one layer:
- **Moves:** a per-hex threat map, the most any enemy that can reach a hex could do to the acting unit standing there. This is what BattleAI computes, and it would be added to the move-hex logits.
- **Spell targets:** spell damage against each stack's total HP.
- **Attention bias:** the full 10×10 matrices feeding the attention, once the trainable trunk is worth it.

**Step 1** is the slot layout, `stack_features`, `cross_stack_features`, the wipe rule, the morale/luck encoder fix and the three tests. Then the comparison against today's network with the rule weight learnable.

## The rule layer (`model.py`)

The heads now produce raw logits; the rule adds to them; the masks come last. The ugly 1000:

```python
self.kill_rule_weight = nn.Parameter(torch.tensor(1000.0))

def add_kill_rule(self, logits, x):
	"""Rule: attack the most valuable enemy stack the acting unit kills whatever the dice, if it can.

	A stack it kills for sure (rules.cross_stack_features) gets kill_rule_weight * (1 + its share of its army's
	value) added to its target logit, so the most valuable one wins. The type (melee or shoot) is chosen before
	the target, so that type's logit gets the score of its best legal target.
	"""
	f = rules.cross_stack_features(x, self.creature_tables())
	score = self.kill_rule_weight * (1 + f["value_share"])
	logits = dict(logits)
	for kills, target, mask, action in (("kills_melee", "melee_target", "m_melee_target", ActionType.MELEE), ("kills_shot", "shoot_target", "m_shoot_target", ActionType.SHOOT)):
		rule = score * f[kills]  # [B, U], 0 where it does not kill for sure
		logits[target] = logits[target] + rule
		best = (rule * x[mask]).amax(dim=1)  # the best legal target's score
		logits["type"] = logits["type"] + best[:, None] * self.type_one_hot[action]
	return logits
```

## The op (`rules.py`): the engine's least damage, exactly

```python
def least_damage(count, single_min, attack, defense, reduction_percent, halvings):
	over = (attack - defense).clamp(min=0, max=ATTACK_POINTS_CAP)       # +1/20 a point, at most 80
	under = (defense - attack).clamp(min=0, max=DEFENSE_POINTS_CAP)     # -1/40 a point, at most 28
	numerator = count * single_min * (ATTACK_STEPS + over) * (DEFENSE_STEPS - under) * (100 - reduction_percent)
	denominator = ATTACK_STEPS * DEFENSE_STEPS * 100 * 2**halvings
	fractional = (over > 0) | (under > 0) | (reduction_percent > 0) | (halvings > 0)
	exact = numerator // denominator
	floating_point_slip = fractional & (exact * denominator == numerator)  # a whole number the engine may round down
	return (exact - floating_point_slip.long()).clamp(min=1)
```

`cross_stack_features` computes, for the acting unit (slot `g_active_unit`) against every slot:
- **Its least damage per creature:** Curse forces the minimum, Bless the maximum.
- **Halvings:** shooting from more than 10 hexes from every hex of the target, Air Shield on the target, basic Forgetfulness on the shooter, and a shooter fighting in melee without NO_MELEE_PENALTY.
- **Shield and Air Shield percentages.**
- **Each target's total HP and value:** cost × creatures, with the top creature counted by its remaining health.
- **The result:** `kills_* = alive enemy & least damage ≥ total HP`.

## Things to discuss before training

1. **The 1000 can't stay.** At 1000 the rule's action has probability 1, so its gradient is about 0 and PPO can never learn an exception. It should start at a few nats: strong, but learnable.
2. **"Most valuable" is welded to the kill weight.** `1000 × (1 + value share)` uses one weight. Two learnable weights, one for "kills for sure" and one for "value of what dies", would let training change one without the other.
3. **Bonuses the op ignores make it miss real sure kills.** Only reductions are counted, so "for sure" never lies. But kills that are only sure thanks to a bonus are missed, for example Angels hating Devils (+50%) in our own scenario. Adding hate, jousting and the like needs the ability parameters (which creature is hated) in the vocabulary.
4. **"For sure" means sure from what we can see.** The enemy hero's Armorer is hidden. Petrification, elemental special cases, walls and damage caps aren't modelled; they're listed in the `rules.py` docstring.
5. **The game constants stay fixed.** 5%, 2.5%, the caps and 10 hexes are exact rules, not magic numbers. They should be exported from VCMI's config with the vocabulary so they can't drift.
6. **The next ops follow the same pattern:**
   - expected kill fraction and p(wipe) for kills that aren't sure;
   - retaliation;
   - a per-hex threat map for moves.

   The "best legal target lifts its type" step is already a general way to push any rule up the action hierarchy.
7. **Not done yet:** the 20-slot layout and the per-unit feature encoder we discussed.

**The only gap is an input we don't have.** StupidAI caps each target's estimated damage at `getTotalHealth()`. That is the stack's full health at its starting size (max HP × the count at the start of battle), not its remaining health. The contract carries only the current count, so max HP × count is used instead. That is exact until the stack loses a creature. Adding the starting count to the contract would make it 100%; it would also be a meaningful feature for the learned model ("how much of this stack is gone").

## The forward pass

```python
def forward(self, x):
	units = rules.units_of(x, self.tables())
	me = rules.select(units, x["g_active_unit"].clamp(0, MAX_UNITS - 1))
	batch = torch.arange(units["count"].shape[0], device=units["count"].device)
	enemy = units["alive"] & (units["side"] != x["g_our_side"][:, None])

	# 1. Shoot the most profitable stack it can shoot
	shoot_target, can_shoot = first_best(self.profit(me, units, shooting=True), x["m_shoot_target"])

	# 2. Else hit the most profitable stack it can reach, from the hex with the most shooters around
	melee_target, can_hit = first_best(self.profit(me, units, shooting=False), x["m_melee_target"])
	melee_from, _ = first_best(self.shooters_around(units), x["m_melee_from"][batch, melee_target])

	# 3. Else towards the enemy closest by path (moveStackToClosestEnemy)
	flying = me["flying"][:, 0] | rules.effect(me, rules.FLY)[1][:, 0]
	distances, predecessors = rules.reachability(x, me, flying)
	to_reach = self.distance_to_reach(me, units, distances)
	closest, any_enemy = first_best(-to_reach, enemy & (units["hex"] >= 0))
	closest_distance = to_reach[batch, closest]
	destination = self.go_towards(me, rules.select(units, closest), distances, predecessors, flying)
	walks = any_enemy & (closest_distance < N_HEXES) & (destination >= 0)

	kind = torch.where(can_shoot, ActionType.SHOOT, torch.where(can_hit, ActionType.MELEE, torch.where(walks, ActionType.MOVE, ActionType.DEFEND)))
	return self.outputs(x, kind, destination, torch.where(can_shoot, shoot_target, melee_target), melee_from)
```

(The file also has two lines that read `u_base_count` when it's given; only the check script adds it.)

The pieces it calls:
- **`profit`:** the engine's average damage dealt minus the target's average retaliation. The retaliation is estimated after our least hit and after our most, as the engine does, and capped by the *target's* health. That last part is StupidAI's own bug, kept on purpose.
- **`go_towards`:** goes to a hex next to the target if one is reachable this turn. Otherwise it flies to the reachable hex nearest the closest such hex, or walks back along the engine's path from it.
- **`rules.damage_range`:** the engine's `calculateDmgRange` in double precision, with the factors in the engine's order, so the rounding matches too.
- **`rules.reachability`:** the engine's breadth-first search layer by layer. It reproduces the engine's queue order, because StupidAI follows the path predecessors and those depend on it.
- **Ties:** go to the first slot or the lowest hex, as `std::max_element` does.

## Engine facts that mattered

- **Hate is real here:** Angels and Devils hate each other (+50%) in `castle_vs_inferno`. The vocabulary didn't export which creature is hated, so the vocabulary exporter now writes ability values too.
- **Blind weakens retaliation:** a blinded stack's retaliation is reduced by the spell's percentage. Shield and Air Shield are percentages, and in VCMI any Air Shield also halves shots.
- **The sorts are stable:** libc++'s `std::sort` on these short hex lists (fewer than 24 elements) behaves stably, so tie order can be reproduced.

## Costs and limits

- **The exported graph has 12,000 nodes and takes 4.5 s to load** per battle process. It's the 64 unrolled search layers; an ONNX Loop would shrink it.
- **Not modelled,** and absent from our scenarios: hero skills and artifacts, Slayer, sieges, invincible units, and a few rare effects (listed in the docstrings).

[2026-10-23 16:21] AI: Mostly right, but "simulations" covers two different things, and one of them would actually fit in a forward pass. Checking BattleAI's code to be precise:

[2026-10-23 16:21] TOOLS: Bash×3

[2026-10-23 16:21] AI: Yes, both parts are right. One correction: it isn't impossible in principle. Everything BattleAI does is deterministic, finite computation, and a simulation on a copy is just more computation. What makes a faithful forward pass impractical is *what* it simulates, plus two things that aren't about simulation at all. From `AI/BattleAI`:

1. **Spells (the real wall).** For every castable spell and every viable target, it casts the spell with VCMI's own spell code on a hypothetical copy of the battle (`attemptCastingSpell`, `HypotheticBattle`), in parallel. Then it plays out the remaining turn queue with estimated attacks and scores the result. Copying that means reimplementing the engine's spell mechanics as tensor ops: damage, buffs, debuffs, summons, teleport, and how buffs feed through the bonus system.

2. **Attack exchanges (feasible, but big).**
   - `AttackPossibility::evaluate` scores every target and attack hex: damage dealt and taken, retaliation, and collateral from multi-hex attacks.
   - `calculateExchange` then simulates the next turns: who can reach whom in one or two turns, and the attacks applied in turn order on a copy.

   This uses the same damage formula we already have, applied in sequence, so it could be unrolled into a forward pass like StupidAI's search. But it's about 1,600 lines of branchy logic.

3. **It has memory across decisions.** When a unit waits, BattleAI caches that turn's best attack and score (`cachedAttack`) and compares against it when the unit acts later in the round. So its decision isn't a pure function of the current position. A memoryless forward pass can't match it exactly without that cached value as an input.

4. **It sees more than our inputs:** the enemy's spellbook and skills, which our contract deliberately hides.

5. **Then it picks by a formula.** Everything ends up in one score, "damage reduce": the army value of damage dealt minus damage taken, over the simulated exchange. It takes the best option among attack, move towards a target, wait, and cast.

**What we can take from it:** its formulas and intermediate quantities, ported as ops the same way as StupidAI's:
- the attack-possibility values per target and hex;
- the value-weighted "damage reduce" score;
- the one-turn reachability threat maps.

Those can become features or rules for our model. Spell evaluation needs either ops for a handful of common spells (damage spells, Bless/Slow/Haste, Shield) or the real engine. The engine route is the search we already built: casting on actual copies, not a forward pass.

[2026-10-23 16:24] USER: ok, are there any fixes of formulas that are useful and can become an op for us?

[2026-10-23 16:24] AI: Reading BattleAI's actual formulas so I can say concretely which ones are worth porting:

[2026-10-23 16:25] TOOLS: Bash×6

[2026-10-23 16:25] AI: Yes, several. They're all closed formulas on top of the damage estimate, which we already compute exactly, so each fits our op pattern. In rough order of usefulness:

**1. Pairwise damage matrix (`DamageCache`).** For every pair of stacks: the average damage one creature of A deals to B (the engine's estimate ÷ count). It's the foundation of everything below. We have it exactly via `rules.damage_range`, including hate and Blind. One op, all pairs.

**2. Damage value, "damage reduce" (`calculateDamageReduce`).** It values a hit by how much of the enemy's damage output it removes, not by gold:

```
value = enemy's damage per creature (against the attacker) × (0.5 × creatures killed + 0.5 × hp_value)
hp_value = full creatures destroyed + hpFunction(partial damage)
hpFunction = a trapezoid: the last HP of a creature count more than the first ("the goal is to kill, not hurt")
```

- So a target counts by its threat to *you*, which gold value doesn't capture. Both are worth having as features: gold is aligned with our reward, threat with winning the fight.
- It has three magic numbers: KILL_BOUNTY 0.5, HEALTH_BOUNTY 0.5 and the 2/3 in `hpFunction`. Exactly your pattern: trainable parameters starting at those values.

**3. Attack value per legal action (`AttackPossibility::evaluate` → `damageDiff`).**
- The formula: damage reduce dealt − damage reduce taken in retaliation, from the damaged target, as the engine estimates it − friendly fire from multi-hex attacks + the value of blocking shooters.
- It runs over the attacker's total attacks, so double strikes count.
- It's computed per (target, attack-from hex) and per shot. That maps one-to-one onto our melee-target/melee-from and shoot heads, so it becomes a rule term with a learnable weight, like the kill rule.
- Friendly fire needs each attack shape (breath, attack-all-adjacent) from the creature's abilities. That's moderate work and can come second.

**4. Blocking enemy shooters (`evaluateBlockedShootersDmg`).**
- An enemy shooter with an enemy next to it can only melee, usually at half damage.
- The formula: for each attack hex, sum over the enemy shooters next to it of (ranged average − melee average + 1) × their damage ÷ ranged average.
- It's a per-hex value for the "attack from where" choice and for moves. It's cheap.

**5. Reach and threat maps (`getOneTurnReachableUnits`, `canBeHitThisTurn`).**
- For each hex: which units can reach it this turn and next. That's our search run from every unit at once, then distance ≤ k × speed.
- Combined with item 2, it gives a per-hex threat: the damage reduce the enemies can inflict on a unit standing there.
- That's the op the move head is missing: "don't step where you get wiped". It's also BattleAI's input to its exchange simulation, which we'd skip.

**6. Strength ratio and risk attitude.**
- ratio = Σ count × AI value (ours) ÷ Σ (theirs); the weight on our own losses = 1 if ratio ≥ 1, else ratio².
- So when weaker, it tolerates riskier trades.
- It's a single global feature (the vocabulary has `ai_value`), and the formula is a cheap prior for how much to fear retaliation.

**Not worth porting:**
- **The exchange simulation** (`calculateExchange`): attack sequences over several turns on copies. That's search, and our MCTS does it with the real engine.
- **Spell evaluation:** the same reason.
- **Movement through enemy stacks** priced by turns to kill them: a weighted pathfinding variant, low value for the work.

**What I'd build first:** items 1 and 2 as ops, then item 3 as a rule term with learnable weights, checked the way we checked StupidAI, against BattleAI's own numbers (it logs `+defenderDamageReduce −attackerDamageReduce = score` per attack). Items 4 and 5 after that. All of it is pairwise, so it's also the moment to move to the smaller slot layout (10 per side) we discussed.

[2026-10-23 17:18] AI: The new model is built, tested and pushed. Its forward is a few named steps; everything it computes from the game's rules is bounded, built only from this battle, and assigned to the exact action or hex it describes. All 94 tests pass, including the alignment and scale checks below.

## The forward

```python
def facts(self, x):  # features.py: no parameters; training computes these once per decision and epoch
	with torch.no_grad():
		b = F.read_battle(x, self.tables())
		reach, to_enemy = F.distance_maps(b)
		maps, can_attack = F.threat_maps(b, reach, to_enemy)
		return {"melee": F.attack_features(b, shooting=False), "shot": F.attack_features(b, shooting=True),
		        "maps": maps, "can_attack": can_attack, "stack": F.stack_features(b),
		        "summaries": F.unit_summaries(b, can_attack), "battle": F.battle_features(b, maps)}

def forward(self, x, facts=None):
	facts = self.facts(x) if facts is None else facts
	b = F.read_battle(x, self.tables())
	heroes, hero_valid = self.heroes(b)
	units, unit_valid = self.units(b, facts)
	context, units, hexes = self.trunk(b, self.battle(b, facts), heroes, units, self.hexes(b, facts), hero_valid, unit_valid)
	actions = F.action_features(b, facts["melee"], facts["shot"], facts["maps"], facts["can_attack"], facts["battle"])
	return {**self.actions(context, units, hexes, actions, x), **self.values(context)}
```

## The features (`features.py`, one small function each)

| Function | What it gives | Shape |
|---|---|---|
| `stack_features` | health fraction; value vs the average stack (v/(v+mean)); share of own army; damage per HP; damage spread; attack/defence on the formula's scale; buffs (tanh of 5% / 2.5% a point); style; speed of 15; ammo of 12; retaliations; log(1±p) morale and luck; turn order; flags | per unit |
| `attack_features` (melee and shot) | damage fraction; kills; exact sure kill; wipe chance; enemy value destroyed; share of the threat against me removed; retaliation; our value lost; net value | per target |
| `threat_maps` | the share of enemy value that can attack me there next turn; danger; enemy shooters I'd block; new targets I could attack next turn; progress toward the nearest enemy | per hex |
| `unit_summaries` | per unit: the most of an enemy it can take; the most an enemy in reach can take of it; vs the acting unit both ways | per unit |
| `battle_features` | strength = our value / (ours + theirs); round; danger and threat where I stand | global |

All damage goes through the engine-exact estimate, including hate, Bless/Curse, shields, range and melee penalties, and Blind's weaker retaliation.

## How each score lands on its own action

Every choice's logit = **pointer score** (learned) + **`RuleScore`** + **its best follow-up's logit**.

- **First order:** one weight per named feature, applied to that choice's own features. A melee from-hex gets its own hex's numbers; a target gets its own numbers. There are about 30 weights in total, starting at hand-chosen values (`RULE_START`).
- **Second order:** rank 4, the choice's features against a summary of all legal choices and the context. It starts at 0.
- **Best follow-up:** a type gets its best target's or hex's logit, so a sure kill lifts MELEE itself, not just the target.
- **Untrained,** pointer scores start at 0, so the model plays its rules.
- **The kill rule is just two weights:** `set_rule_weights("melee_target", sure_kill=1000, value_destroyed=1000)`. In VCMI that attacked the most valuable sure kill in 180 of 180 chances over three checks, and every target died.

## What the tests and traces showed

- **Exact alignment:** with untrained pointer scores, each hex's and target's logit equals its own features' first-order score (plus the best hex, for targets).
- **Bounded:** every feature stays in its range on random battles.
- **Scale-free:** with every stack ×10, everything except creature counts matches to 0.005. Counts are lumpy for real: 2 Angels can't kill one Devil, 20 kill 6 of 20. Between ×10 and ×100 everything matches.
- **Cost:**
  - C++ inference: 5.5 ms per decision (was 1.0 ms).
  - Training step on the Apple GPU: 61 ms per batch of 64 (old model 52 ms), plus 86 ms per 64 decisions once per epoch for the cached facts.
  - Integer `amin` over the 6 neighbours was taking 95% of CPU time; with that fixed, a CPU step went from 3.4 s to 0.15 s.
- **Three design mistakes, found by reading traces and fixed:**
  - Averaging a type's options made the untrained model wait and defend. Most hexes are threatened, so the average sank. Now a type gets its best option.
  - Hex threat was absolute, which made staying put look free. Move and attack-hex features are now changes from where the unit stands.
  - "Progress" sent Archers walking toward the enemy. It's now 0 for units that can shoot.
- **Untrained strength:** −0.77 attacking and −0.84 defending against BattleAI, about StupidAI's level (−0.72 / −0.82). BattleAI is far stronger; the rest has to be learned.

## What I did not do, before you look for it

- **Spells have no features yet**, only learned pointer scores. Spell ops are next: damage against stack HP, and Bless/Slow/Haste effects.
- **Heroes:** only made bounded; no new content.
- **The 40-slot contract is unchanged:** cross features are the acting unit against every slot, plus pooled all-pairs damage. The 10-per-side layout is still open.
- **Reach treats two-hex units as one hex.** Threat maps ignore that the unit's own move changes who can reach where.
- **Feature scales are fixed constants** (20 and 40 for attack/defence, 15 for speed, 12 for ammo), not learned.
- **Engine morale/luck bug, fixed:** the encoder now sends the engine's effective values (clamped to ±3, 0 for undead). Old records have raw values.
- **Not trained yet:** model v2 is now the training default, but no run has used it yet.

[2026-10-23 18:19] AI: All your points are done, the slot count is down to 20, and I found and fixed two more bugs. All 90 tests pass, StupidAI still matches exactly given the starting sizes, and everything is committed and pushed.

**Sure kill and the demo are gone.** `wipe_chance` is the p(stack wipe) feature. The `sure_kill` feature, the hand-chosen weights, `check_rules.py` and its tests are deleted.

**Every action's feature weights now start at 0 and are learned,** in the three parts you described (`ActionScore` in `model.py`):
- one linear weight per feature, added to that action's own logit (about 35 weights across the heads);
- a tiny layer (8 wide) from all of the action's own features to 1;
- a wider layer (32 wide) from the action's features plus everything else (the context, and the mean and max of all legal actions' features).

The untrained model is therefore uniform apart from the biases.

**Shooter safety.** I zeroed shooters' progress earlier; that was wrong. Now there are:
- `melee_threat_change`: the change in the share of enemy value that could reach me in melee there. It's what keeping distance escapes, for any unit.
- `progress` split into `progress` (melee units) and `shooter_progress` (shooters), so each can learn its own sign.
- `shooter_next_to_enemy`: a shooter standing there couldn't shoot.

**Spells:** embeddings only, plus biases, all wrapped in `priors.py`:
- `battle_model/priors/spells.csv` (name, id, bias): my judgment, −1 to +0.75. Slow and Prayer +0.75; Lightning Bolt, Haste, Bless and Blind +0.5; Earthquake, Remove Obstacle and Armageddon −1; adventure spells 0. It's the spell head's starting bias. Yes, each spell already had one bias unit; it started at 0 before.
- `battle_model/priors/action_types.csv`: the same format for the type priors, e.g. −6 for retreat and surrender (values unchanged).
- A learnable bias per spell × our hero's mastery of the spell's school (none/basic/advanced/expert, the best of its schools), starting at 0 / 0.25 / 0.5 / 0.75 for every spell.

**40 slots: no, we don't need them.** It's now 20, 10 per side (contract v8). An army has 7 stacks plus war machines and an occasional summon. Over 10, the C++ encoder keeps the acting unit, then living stacks by value, then dead ones (Resurrection targets), in the engine's order. Old checkpoints still load: neither model has a parameter per slot.

**Other bugs, fixed:**
1. **Half the unit summaries were blind.** Our units' "best hit on an enemy" was always 0, and so was the enemies' "worst hit from us". They got a reach map already masked to enemies. A regression test pins it.
2. **Summoned units and clones counted at full value** in the value features. The reward counts them as 0 because they vanish after the battle.

Two other bugs were already fixed earlier in this session: morale and luck were sent as raw bonus sums, and all 256 eval seeds were the same.

**Known gaps, not bugs:**
- Reach maps treat two-hex units as one hex.
- StupidAI equality needs the stacks' starting sizes as an input.
- In very rare cases where the engine's damage is a whole number, the features the C++ AI computes during play and those training recomputes on the Apple GPU can differ by one hit point.

**Cost:** 264k parameters; 4.6 ms of C++ inference per decision (the old model 0.9 ms); a 60 ms training step for 64 decisions (the old model 45 ms).

[2026-10-23 18:29] AI: Two-hex units are fixed and checked against the engine. Both answers are below.

**Two-hex reach: fixed and verified against the engine.**
- **What changed:**
  - a two-hex unit stands only where its second hex is free too;
  - it can attack from positions where its second hex is next to the target;
  - it can be attacked through either hex.

  That applies everywhere: threat, danger, next-to-enemy, blocked shooters and the per-unit summaries.
- **The check:** in every recorded decision, the engine's own masks say where the acting unit can move and which positions it can attack each target from. In 418 real decisions, 104 of them by two-hex units, our maps give exactly the same hexes, with no differences.
- **Kept:** that check as `check_reach.py`, plus unit tests. One test shows our distance maps equal the engine-exact search on random battles. All 95 tests pass; committed and pushed.

**Do we still need StupidAI?** Not for anything we ship: training doesn't use it, and the C++ fallback is VCMI's own StupidAI, not ours.
- **It made my job easier once, a lot.** Matching it decision by decision found engine facts I had wrong or missing:
  - hate (Angels vs Devils, +50%);
  - `getTotalHealth()` meaning the stack's starting size;
  - Blind weakening retaliation;
  - the search's predecessor order.

  It also proved our damage estimate is engine-exact, and every feature of the new model is built on that.
- **It won't find bugs in the new model's features.** Neither of today's bugs (the half-blind summaries, two-hex reach) is something it could catch. Direct engine checks like `check_reach.py` are what find those.
- **My recommendation:** keep it frozen, don't extend it. Its unit tests take seconds, and its engine-exact search is now the reference the distance-map test compares against. If it ever costs real work, delete it and replace it with a direct check of the damage estimate against the engine's.

**The whole-number damage issue: ignore it, as you guessed.** I measured it. Over 1.6 million attacker–target pairs, the CPU (where the C++ AI plays) and the Apple GPU (where training runs) give identical damage. So play and training always see the same features. Against the engine's double precision, our float32 is 1 HP off in about 1.7% of pairs, which only nudges a feature by a fraction of a percent. A full fix would need exact integer arithmetic that can overflow with huge stacks, so it isn't free; I left a one-line note in `damage_range`.

1. **Reward:** the demons from Summon Demons stay in your army after the battle, but our reward counted them as worth 0. Fixed in both the Python and C++ rewards; they now agree to 1e-16 on battles with summons.

[2026-10-23 19:08] USER: ok, now while the run is ongoing lets talk about something else. let's say I want to have an Iterable dataloader that generates new scenario according to a set of rules I define, do I need to persist a new map to disk for every example or can the example generation happen in memory alone? Do not code it yet, just tell me

[2026-10-23 19:08] AI: **Short answer:** with the current pipeline, yes, each different scenario needs its own map file on disk. But that's cheap and doesn't stop the dataloader from working in memory. Truly disk-free generation means moving off VCMI maps altogether, which is worth doing for speed anyway.

**Why a file today:** a battle starts when a fresh VCMI process loads a map by name from its maps folder (`play.build_map` writes `runtime/vcmi-user/<name>.vmap`, and `headless.sh` passes the name). VCMI has no way in our setup to receive a map from memory, so every distinct scenario needs a `.vmap`.

**Why that's fine for now:**
- A `.vmap` is a zip of a few small JSON files, a few KB, written in milliseconds. Disk space and speed aren't the problem.
- The dataloader can still feel in-memory. Each example is: your rules produce a scenario dict, it's written as a temporary map with a unique name, the battle plays, and the file is deleted. That's the same pattern `play.py` already uses for prefix files.
- A new VCMI process starts for every battle, so it sees each new file.
- The parts to get right are unique names across parallel battles, and deleting the file even when a battle fails.

**Options for no disk at all:**
1. **Patch VCMI to load the map from a memory buffer.** I believe VCMI has an in-memory map loader, but I haven't checked. The server's game start only takes a map name, so this needs a change to the engine, and not a bug fix. That goes against keeping engine changes minimal, and saves only a few KB of writes. Not worth it.
2. **Build the battle directly in our own process, with no map.** Stage 2's `BattleSimulation` already copies a battle and runs it with the server's own rules inside our process. A battle-only runner would:
   - set up the battle straight from a scenario description (heroes, skills, spells, armies, battlefield);
   - run both AIs in the same process, many battles in a row.

   That removes the map, the disk and the per-battle process startup. The startup is probably the real per-battle overhead (two processes and all the game data loaded every time), but I haven't measured it.

   The risk is that battle setup has to match the real game exactly: obstacles, starting positions, tactics, war machines, hero bonuses. We'd check it the same way as stage 2, by comparing records from both paths on the same seeds; they must be identical. `BattleSim.h` already has a TODO for battle-only copies, which is part of the same work.

**My recommendation:** start with temporary maps. That works now with no engine or C++ changes, so you can build and test the scenario rules immediately. Then time how long a battle takes to start and stop. If startup is a large share of each battle, as I expect, build the battle-only runner; disk-free scenarios come with it.

[2026-10-23 19:12] USER: is there a way to make the maps MUCH smaller? like 2x1 in size and just enough for 2 heroes? will that need a patch?

[2026-10-23 19:12] TOOLS: Bash×2

[2026-10-23 19:12] AI: **You don't need to.** The maps are already tiny: `alpha_make_battle_map` builds a 10x10 map in VCMI's battle-only mode, about 1.8 KB as a `.vmap`. Most of that is the zip wrapper and the two heroes with their armies, skills and spells. The 100 terrain tiles compress to almost nothing, so a 2x1 map would save a few hundred bytes at most.

**Would 2x1 need a patch?** Probably not. Width and height are plain fields the tool sets. But it could break things that a 10x10 map gets right for free: a hero takes up more than one tile on the map, and objects near the map edge can be rejected or placed wrongly. I'd expect to fiddle with it and gain nothing measurable, so I wouldn't try it.

**What actually costs time is not the map.** Each battle starts a new VCMI process that loads all the game data and mods, then sets up the game; reading a 2 KB map is a rounding error next to that. I haven't timed it separately, so that's the number to measure before optimising anything. If startup turns out to dominate, the fix is the battle-only runner I described before, not smaller maps. With temporary maps, disk use stays around 2 KB per scenario that exists at any moment, if each file is deleted after its battle.

Unrelated, but I noticed it while checking: `runtime/vcmi-user/Maps/alpha/` holds 29 leftover map copies from old runs, about 50 KB in total. They're harmless, and I can delete them when the current runs finish.

[2026-10-23 19:17] USER: lets say I want to run a new training run where each combat needs a new map, moreover while I will be getting all the labels for all turns in the combat, I only plan on using 2 turns (not full rounds, just 2 random turns) for my training. Assume batch_size=64 so I need to have 32 maps for it. How much of my time/compute will be taken by the need to work the way the system is wired today instead of just generating the scenario in memory. This doesn't have to be exact, just a ballpark answer so I'll know if we need to invest effort in this.

[2026-10-23 19:17] AI: Measuring the two costs that come from the current wiring: building a map, and starting VCMI for each battle. The two training runs are loading the CPU, so these numbers are pessimistic.

[2026-10-23 19:18] TOOLS: Bash×2

[2026-10-23 19:18] AI: **Ballpark: the current wiring costs roughly 30–40% of the compute.** That's real, but it isn't where the cost of your plan is. Measured just now, with two training runs loading the CPU:

| Per battle | Time | Caused by the wiring? |
|---|---|---|
| Building the map (`alpha_make_battle_map`, 1.8 KB file) | ~0.25 s | yes |
| VCMI process start until the AI is up | 0.5–1.0 s | yes |
| Script, process launch and teardown outside VCMI's own log | ~1 s | yes |
| Playing the battle itself (≈50 decisions, untrained model) | 3.3 s alone, 7.5–8.5 s with 8 in parallel | no |

So per battle about 1.5–2 s is overhead and 3.5–8 s is the battle. Building scenarios in memory with a battle-only runner would remove most of the overhead. That makes it about 1.4–1.6× faster, not 10×.

**For your batch:** 32 battles is about 32 × (3.5 s of battle + ~1.7 s of overhead) ≈ 170 core-seconds. On ~12 cores that's roughly 15 s per batch of 64, against a training step of ~60 ms. Data generation dominates by about 250×, with or without the overhead.

**The bigger cost is the 2-turns choice, not the maps.** Each battle has about 50 decisions with full labels, and you keep 2 of them, so 96% of the expensive part is thrown away. You can't save by stopping early either, because the labels need the battle's ending (the final value target). If the goal is to keep training examples from being correlated, a shuffle buffer gets you that without the waste: mix decisions from many recent battles and draw your batch of 64 from it, so each battle feeds many batches. That's about a 25× gain against about 1.5× from fixing the wiring.

**My recommendation:**
1. **Don't invest in in-memory scenarios for this run.** Temporary maps work today and cost a few KB each.
2. **Optional cheap fix:** build all 32 maps in one call of the map tool, taking a list of scenarios, instead of 32 processes. That's a small change to our own tool, not the engine, and saves ~8 s of CPU per batch.
3. **Reconsider keeping 2 turns per battle.** If you need it for decorrelation, use the buffer. If it's for a different reason, tell me what, because that changes the answer.
4. The battle-only runner is worth building later, when battles get cheap enough that the ~1.7 s startup is most of the time. Faster battle setup for the search would push the same way, but today it isn't urgent.

the use case is the following: 
in the future training data for battles will come from campaigns that the campaign AI plays against itself. That means that a lot of battles will come from the same map and for close days (game stage) one after the other. This can cause the network to train again and again on similar data, I want to use this generator to either:

4. **Force ratio:** 60% within 1.25:1, 28% up to 3:1, 12% up to 10:1, plus a per-battle-type chance for which side is the stronger one.
5. **Armies:**
   - one main faction per army; each stack 85% from that faction, 10% from another, 5% neutral creatures;
   - higher-level stacks hold more of the value;
   - war machines;
   - neutrals are one creature type, sometimes with an extra upgraded stack.
6. **Heroes:** faction (85% the army's own), might or magic class. Skills grow 80% of the time as the engine levels a hero up, and 20% are drawn freely to get unusual heroes. Also spellbook, mage guild spells plus a few random ones, mana, and artifacts by class.
7. **The rest:** which side we play, whether we have the gold to surrender, a random VCMI seed.

**Where you're wrong, or it doesn't hold up:**
1. **"Town assault like we have now":** we have none. Every scenario so far is two heroes on a 10×10 map. Sieges need towns in the map tool.
2. **"Make sure the stats are right" for terrain and artifacts:** that's already handled. The engine applies native terrain, artifact and skill bonuses, and the encoder reads each unit's current stats from the engine. The real problem is what the model **can't see**:
   - **Special battlefields:** the model's spell-mastery bias reads the hero's secondary skills. On magic plains every spell is cast at expert, and the elemental fields raise their own school, so the bias is wrong there. Fix: give the model each school's effective level from the engine instead.
   - **Artifacts with no visible stat effect,** such as Orb of Tempestuous Fire (stronger air spells), Orb of Vulnerability or Recanter's Cloak, are invisible to the model. It needs an input listing our hero's artifacts.
   - These two inputs are what you meant by "modelling value/bias in special terrains". I'd add them before generating these battles.
3. **"Close to equal most of the time"** is right for hero vs hero, but not against neutrals: a hero attacks monsters it can beat, so even fights there are rare. I kept your ratio distribution and made neutrals the weaker side 85% of the time.
4. **Army value isn't strength.** A level-25 hero with 30 attack makes a "1:1" fight lopsided. That's fine for variety, but force ratios in the logs will be misleading unless hero strength is counted too.
5. **Tactics must stay out for now.** Our AI skips the tactics phase (it's scripted), while BattleAI uses it. The config excludes the Tactics skill until our model plays that phase.
6. **On collapse:** a generator alone won't stop the network over-training on one game's similar battles. What prevents that is mixing: a buffer holding battles from many games and generated ones, with each batch drawn across them. Your plan of one game per dataloader worker helps; the buffer is the piece I'd add on top.

**Missing from the tooling before this can run:** the map tool (`alpha_make_battle_map`) supports terrain, heroes with primary and secondary skills, spells, war machines and armies only. It still needs:
- hero level, artifacts and mana;
- special battlefields;
- towns with fortifications for sieges;
- neutral monsters and creature banks;
- boats.

[2026-10-23 22:46] TOOLS: Bash×3

[2026-10-23 22:46] AI: The Q-values are rescaled to 0..1 at each node (the mctx default), so the scale isn't the bug. Checking how the 16 simulations are spread over the 16 root actions the search considers:

[2026-10-23 22:46] TOOLS: Bash

[2026-10-23 22:46] AI: PPO wins clearly: the network trained on the search's policy is much worse.

| Greedy eval vs BattleAI, mean of evals 200–400 | PPO | Search (16 simulations) |
|---|---|---|
| Attack win rate | 99% | 42% |
| Attack reward minus BattleAI's on the same seeds | −0.03 | −0.90 |
| Defence reward minus BattleAI's | +0.17 | −0.05 |
| Seconds per epoch | 7 | 31 |

- Both were equal at eval 40; after that the search-trained network fell apart and never recovered.
- In its own training games, where the search picks the moves, it still wins 99% attacking. The search plays fine; the network it trains is what's bad.
- The crash and resume at epoch 56 is not the cause: loss, entropy and KL run smoothly across it.

**Likely cause: the search's target is mostly dice noise.**
1. With 16 simulations and 16 root actions considered, the halving schedule gives every considered action exactly 1 visit. All 16 simulations go to the first round, so there is no halving at all.
2. Each action's Q-value is therefore one sample: one roll of the dice, then one value-head estimate.
3. Those Q-values are rescaled to 0..1 at each node, and the target adds up to (50 + 1) × 0.1 = 5.1 to the log-probability of the best one.
4. So the training target says "strongly prefer whichever action got the luckiest single roll". mctx's defaults assume a deterministic game, where one visit is a fair estimate; ours has dice.
5. This fits what we see: the gap between the target and the network (KL) sits at 0.45–0.55 for all 400 epochs and never shrinks, because the network is chasing noise that changes every time.

I haven't confirmed it directly. A direct check: search the same position many times with different dice and see how often the chosen action changes.

**Options, cheapest first:**
1. **Consider 4 root actions instead of 16:** the same 16 simulations then give 2 visits per action, then 4 for the best two. No code change, it's the `ALPHA_BATTLE_SEARCH_CONSIDERED` setting.
2. **64 simulations, 8 considered:** about 8 visits per action, at roughly 4× the cost (~2 min per epoch).
3. **Lower the 0.1 value scale for noisy Q,** so one lucky sample can't dominate the target.

I'd run option 1 for 400 epochs alongside the PPO run, which is now continuing toward 4000. Want me to start it?

The search run's folder is 12 MB, and it was written at about 0.04 MB/min.

[2026-10-24 08:13] USER: ok, lets stop the current run. Now tell me, why is defense getting a reward that is so much lower than attack? what is the difference between the two? what is the reason for the low score?

[2026-10-24 08:14] TOOLS: TaskStop, Bash

[2026-10-24 08:14] AI: The PPO run is stopped at epoch 2320 of 4000; no processes are left. Now the numbers behind the question: both armies' values, and how the reward formula splits for each side.

[2026-10-24 08:14] TOOLS: Bash×2

[2026-10-24 08:14] AI: **The low defence score is about which army you play, not about defending.** In this scenario "defence" means playing Inferno against Castle, and Castle is the stronger army. Our defence is actually our best result relative to BattleAI.

**Evidence: BattleAI playing both sides, 16 seeds each.**

| Attacker (left) vs defender | Result | Attacker's reward |
|---|---|---|
| Castle vs Inferno (as the scenario is) | Castle wins 16/16 | +0.85 |
| Inferno vs Castle (sides swapped) | Castle wins 16/16 | −0.73 |

Castle wins every battle from either side, so attacking itself gives no measurable advantage. Castle is stronger on every measure, with identical heroes (5/5/5/5, no skills):

| | Castle | Inferno | Castle ahead by |
|---|---|---|---|
| Gold value | 15,930 | 14,430 | +10% |
| Hit points | 1,085 | 964 | +13% |
| Engine's AI value | 23,980 | 21,183 | +13% |
| Engine's fight value | 18,678 | 17,664 | +6% |

Battles last about 3 rounds, so the edge decides them quickly. My earlier note that "the scenario favours the attacker" was wrong; it favours Castle.

**Why the gap in score is so large: the reward formula.**
- Reward = outcome (win +1, loss −1) + fraction of the enemy army killed + fraction of our army kept − small round and mana penalties.
- **A loser keeps nothing**, so its best possible reward is −1 + (just under 1) ≈ 0 raw, which is −0.41 on the −1..1 scale.
- **A winner that kills everything** gets at least +2 raw, +0.53 scaled.
- So even an even, close battle puts about a full point between the two sides. A side that always loses can never score above −0.41.

**What our model actually achieves** (last eval, epoch 2300):

| Seat | Ours | BattleAI's | What it means |
|---|---|---|---|
| Attack (Castle) | +0.83 | +0.85 | wins and keeps ~65% of its army, about BattleAI's level |
| Defence (Inferno) | −0.59 | −0.75 | kills ~65% of Castle's army before losing, vs ~30% for BattleAI |

So the defence isn't failing: it loses a battle BattleAI also always loses, and makes Castle pay twice as much for the win. That's why I've used "reward minus BattleAI's on the same seed and seat" as the measure; the raw reward mostly shows which army you were given.

**What I'd change:** play every scenario with both factions on both sides, or use mirror matches. Then "attack" and "defence" mean the seat, and a matchup this lopsided can't masquerade as a defence problem. The random scenario generator would take care of this by drawing both armies independently.

[2026-10-24 08:18] USER: how is the data modeled, in the input is the order of creatures mine first, theirs later OR attacker first defender later?

[2026-10-24 08:18] TOOLS: Bash×3

[2026-10-24 08:18] AI: **Neither.** The slots are in the engine's own order, by unit id, and are the same whichever side we play.

**How the slots are filled** (`pickUnits` in `Encoder.cpp`):
- The encoder keeps up to 10 units per battle side. On each side it takes the acting unit first, then living stacks by value, then dead ones.
- It then sorts the 20 slots by the engine's unit id.
- VCMI numbers units at the start of the battle with the attacker's stacks first, so a fresh battle looks like "attacker first, defender later".
- Units created later get new, higher ids whatever their side: summoned elementals, clones, Pit Lords' demons. So it's not a strict side split either.

**Why the order doesn't matter to the model:**
- Unit tokens have no slot-number embedding. Each token is its creature, its effects, its hex position and its features, and the trunk is attention over all tokens. Shuffle the slots and the model's outputs shuffle with them, nothing else changes.
- "Mine" comes from `g_our_side`, plus per-unit features computed in `features.py` from it:
  - `ours`: we control it now, so a hypnotised enemy counts as ours;
  - `on_our_side`: its battle side is ours;
  - `acting`: it is the unit acting now.

  The feature values are relative too: our army's value against theirs, threat to us.

**What is absolute, attacker vs defender:**
- **The board.** Hex positions are the engine's indices, not mirrored, so the attacker always starts on the left. As defender, our army starts on the right.
- **Heroes** are indexed by battle side (0 = attacker). The hero token carries an "ours" flag.

**The design issue in this:** the model has to learn every positional pattern twice, once from each side ("advance right" as attacker, "advance left" as defender). In our runs each side only ever played one faction, so it never had to share anything across the two. Mirroring the board for the defender, so we always start on the left, would let both sides share what they learn. That means flipping hex indices horizontally, with the row-offset rule of the hex grid and the direction of two-hex units' second hex. It's doable but not trivial. Sieges make it less clean, since the walls would then appear on our left when we defend, which the model would simply learn.

I'd only do it if we see the two sides learning different, non-transferring habits. With the random generator mixing factions and sides, it's worth testing.

[2026-10-24 08:20] USER: in our model, there is a stack-to-vector function and a hero-to-vector function with trainable parameters right?

[2026-10-24 08:21] TOOLS: Bash

[2026-10-24 08:21] AI: Yes. Both are trainable, and both are shallow: a sum of lookups plus one linear layer, with no hidden layer.

**Stack to vector: `UnitEmbedding`** (`battle_model/model.py`), 32,576 parameters. A stack's vector is the sum of:

| Part | Parameters | What it holds |
|---|---|---|
| Creature embedding | 9,664 | one row per creature id |
| Effects | 8,256 + 192 | an embedding per active spell effect plus a small linear layer for its turns left and value, summed over the stack's effects |
| Position | 11,968 | the hex it stands on, plus the second hex for two-hex units |
| Numbers | 2,496 | a linear layer over the stack's features from `features.py`: count, health and value as bounded ratios; ours / on our side / acting; plus its summaries against the other units, like best hit and worst hit taken |

**Hero to vector: `HeroEmbedding`**, 23,360 parameters, the sum of:

| Part | Parameters | What it holds |
|---|---|---|
| Hero type embedding | 16,448 | one row per hero |
| "Ours" embedding | 128 | our hero or the enemy's |
| Numbers | 6,784 | a linear layer over: present; attack, defence, power and knowledge as x/(x+10); mana left of its pool; pool size against 50; 28 secondary skill levels; 70 spellbook flags. Skills and spellbook are all 0 for the enemy, since a player can't see them |

**What this means:**
- Each vector is a linear function of its inputs: lookups add up and the numbers enter through one linear layer. Interactions such as "Archangel × count" or "Wisdom × spellbook" aren't formed per token. They only appear later, in the attention trunk (2 blocks) and in the action heads' small layers.
- Each token sees only itself (and, through the summaries, its best and worst matchup) until the trunk. After the trunk each unit's vector is contextual, and the action heads point at those contextual vectors.

If you want per-token interactions before attention, the change is small: replace the sum with a 2-layer MLP over [creature embedding, effects, position, numbers], for each of the two. It costs a few thousand parameters and would let, for example, "a single stack of a strong creature" be encoded differently from "many weak ones" before attention mixes things. Whether it helps is an experiment, not something I'd claim in advance.

[2026-10-24 08:22] USER: is the side of the hero (ours or enemy) in the input?

[2026-10-24 08:22] AI: **Yes.** It's passed in the form "ours or the enemy's"; the hero's battle side (attacker or defender) isn't an input of its own.

- **Raw inputs:** heroes have no side field because the side is the index: `h_*[0]` is the attacker's hero and `h_*[1]` the defender's. `g_our_side` says which side we are.
- **In `HeroEmbedding`:** from those two it builds `ours = (side index == g_our_side)` and adds a learned "ours"/"enemy" embedding to each hero's vector. That's the 128-parameter `ours` table.
- **Indirectly:** the enemy hero's secondary skills and spellbook are always zero, since a player can't see them. So "ours" is also visible from which fields are filled in.

**Not given:** whether a hero is the attacker or the defender. The hero vector has no attacker/defender embedding, and slot order doesn't help either, because attention ignores order. The model can only infer it indirectly, from which side's units the hero commands (units carry `u_side`) and from where they stand on the board.

That's probably fine: attacking or defending changes almost nothing about a hero in battle. Sieges are the exception, where the defender gets towers and walls, and those are inputs already (`x_wall_state`, `g_gate_state`). If you want it explicit, it's a one-line addition: an attacker/defender embedding next to "ours".

[2026-10-24 08:23] USER: why is this input 128 numbers?

[2026-10-24 08:23] AI: **The input isn't 128 numbers; it's one bit**: is this hero ours or not. The 128 is the size of the learned table that bit looks up: 2 rows (enemy, ours) × 64 numbers (the model's width, `dim = 64`).

The bit selects one of the two 64-number vectors, and that vector is added to the hero's token. That's how every small categorical input enters the model: hero type, creature, effect and position work the same way, just with more rows.

Half of it is redundant. The hero's `numbers` linear layer already has a 64-number bias added to every hero, so "ours vs enemy" really only needs one 64-number difference vector. The other 64 parameters duplicate the bias. Merging them, by adding the flag as one more number to the linear layer, would give the same model with 64 fewer parameters. That's not worth changing unless you want it tidier.

[2026-10-24 08:33] USER: why are we not better than the BattleAI on attack? If I remember correctly there are things we can do and it can not

[2026-10-24 08:34] TOOLS: Bash

[2026-10-24 08:34] AI: **Short answer: on attack there's almost nothing left to beat.** BattleAI already plays this matchup close to the ceiling, and our training setup gives the attacker almost no signal to improve with.

**How much room there is.** An attacker that wins and kills everything scores 1 (win) + 1 (enemy destroyed) + the fraction of its army kept, minus tiny round and mana penalties.

| Last eval, 16 seeds | BattleAI | Ours |
|---|---|---|
| Win rate | 16/16, ~3 rounds | 16/16 |
| Scaled reward | +0.85 | +0.83 |
| Army kept | ~70% | ~66% |

The whole gap is about 4% of the army, roughly 640 gold or one Crusader. The best conceivable result, winning with nothing lost, would be +1.00, but against Inferno's Arch Devil and Efreet Sultans some losses are unavoidable. Realistically there's maybe +0.05 to win on this seat.

**What we can do that BattleAI can't** (learned long-horizon planning, exact damage features, judging retreat and surrender) matters little here: the battle lasts 3 rounds, and the attacker is never in a position to consider giving up.

**Why we don't even get that +0.05:**
1. **The attacker's learning signal is almost all noise.** It wins 100% of its training battles, so its reward only varies by a few percent of army kept. The advantages are then normalised per side, which blows that small, mostly dice-driven spread up to full size. The defence has a far richer signal: how much it kills varies a lot.
2. **It trains on one seed and is tested on 16 others.** These runs used the default `random_seeds=False`, so every training battle is on VCMI seed 1 (attack) or 2 (defence), while eval uses 16 unseen seeds. Earlier we saw the model memorise single lines. This setting was part of "same settings as before", and it's likely the bigger factor.
3. **It trains against a different, tougher opponent.** In self-play our attacker faces our own Inferno, which kills about twice as much as BattleAI's. Eval is against BattleAI's weaker defence.
4. **The Archangel never resurrects.** Our attacker spent 0.1% of its decisions on creature spells and none in the eval traces, while BattleAI resurrected in every one of its recorded attacks. One Resurrection brings back 100 HP of creatures, about one Crusader — nearly the whole gap. The model has no feature for what a resurrection is worth; that needs each stack's starting size as an input. So it would have to discover it purely by trial and error, from the weak signal in point 1.

**What I'd do, in order:**
1. Train with `random_seeds=True`. It costs nothing and tests whether point 2 is the cause.
2. Add stack starting sizes as an input and a "value restored" feature for creature spells, so resurrection is easy to learn.
3. Use matchups where the attacker can actually lose (mirror matches, or the random generator). An always-won seat teaches little.

[2026-10-24 08:40] USER: so there might be somethings that are off with this design. Specifically for a hero/stack, being strong is good and being weak is bad if they are on our side, the exact opposite is true if they are on the enemy side, we model it by adding a side_embedding to everything_else embedding and it sounds silly and I think it would make this feature very hard to learn. How should we fix it, do not code yet, just tell me

[2026-10-24 08:40] AI: You're partly right. "Content + side embedding" isn't silly in general; it's how BERT marks which sentence a token belongs to, and attention plus the nonlinear layers can learn the sign flip. But in our small model (2 attention blocks, one context MLP) it makes the most important interaction, "strong × ours = good, strong × theirs = bad", something the network has to build itself. And nothing guarantees it builds it cleanly. Two things soften it today:
- The features are already computed from our point of view: our army value against theirs, threat to us, value we'd lose.
- The units' `ours` flag goes through the same linear layer as their numbers.

The structural problem stands, though.

**How I'd fix it, most important first:**

1. **Shared content, side-specific projection.**
   - **What:** encode what a stack or hero *is* with one shared function, since "Archangel is strong" should be learned once from both sides' data. Then project it with weights chosen by its side: `token = W_ours·h` for our units and `W_enemy·h` for theirs. A cheaper version that builds the sign in directly: `token = A·h + s·B·h`, with s = +1 for ours and −1 for the enemy's. "Strength, signed by side" is then a linear readout, not an interaction the trunk has to discover.
   - **"Ours":** the controller, so a hypnotised enemy counts as ours.
   - **Cost:** about one more linear layer per token type.

2. **Separate pooling per side into the context.**
   - **The gap:** the value and type heads read a context built from the global token, the acting unit and our hero. No direct "sum of our side vs sum of theirs" exists anywhere; the balance of power is only reachable through attention.
   - **What:** pool our tokens and the enemy's tokens separately (sum and max of each) and feed both into the context MLP.
   - **Why:** the value head then sees the balance immediately. With the signed tokens from 1, our-minus-theirs is a single linear step.
   - I expect this to help the value head the most, and the value head drives the advantages.

3. **Attention that knows ally from enemy.**
   - **What:** each attention head gets a learned bias, or better separate key/value projections, for same-side pairs vs opposite-side pairs.
   - **Why:** a unit can then look at "who threatens me" differently from "who covers me". It's the same idea as relative-position attention, with only 2 relation types, so it's cheap.

4. **Share data with the role flip.** We play both sides in self-play, so the same situation shows up from each perspective. Signing by s (fix 1) plus mirroring the board for the defender, from the previous question, would let both seats teach one model.

**What I wouldn't do: fully separate encoders for ours and theirs.** Each would see half the data and learn every creature twice. Splitting only after a shared encoder gets the benefit without that cost.

**How to test it cheaply:** no new battles needed. Train the current model and the variant on the same recorded battles and compare how well the value head predicts outcomes: explained variance, and its change per unit lost or killed (`credit_*`). That's minutes, not a 400-epoch run. Only if the variant clearly wins is a full training run worth it.

2. **Spells:** the engine now reports, for every legal spell target, the value the cast would change for each side. Damage, healing, resurrection and summons are priced as the reward prices armies, and damage to our own units counts against us.
   - Each target gets three features: our gain as a share of both armies; our gain against the largest gain or loss at any legal target (−1..1); and whether the spell changes health at all.
   - A spell that changes no health (Slow, Bless) gets a learned value per spell instead, starting at 0.5, not 0.
   - Checked on real casts: Lightning Bolt on 4 Horned Demons +911, Summon Demons −250 from the attacker's view, Slow 0.

3. **Signed half:** the first 32 of each unit's and hero's 64 numbers are multiplied by +1 if ours, −1 if the enemy's, as you proposed. "Ours" means who controls it now, so a hypnotised enemy counts as ours. The norm is RMSNorm, so the sign survives normalisation.
4. **Side strength:** each side's value and health, weighted by learned per-unit factors that start at 1. Our share, ours / (ours + theirs), goes into the context. Untrained, it's exactly our share of value and health.
5. **Attention knows ally from enemy:** each head has a learned bias for same-side and opposite-side pairs. It lets a unit weigh enemies and allies differently by default.
6. **Best practice:** tokens are built by small gated MLPs over [embeddings, numbers] with normalisation, instead of sums of linear projections. The trunk uses RMSNorm and SwiGLU, and board positions also come as "column counted from our own starting side".

**Side strength — my idea, not built:** you're right that a few shares concatenated among ~270 context inputs can be ignored. Two changes, cheapest first:

1. **A strength formula from combat theory, with a few learnable exponents.**
   - Lanchester's square law says a side's fighting strength is roughly its total health × its damage per round. Who wins an exchange of blows follows from the ratio of the two sides' products.
   - Per side: S = (Σ health)^α × (Σ expected damage against the other side)^β. α and β start at 1, so it's the theory, and training can bend it.
   - The damage term is the expected damage of each unit against the enemy's units: a 10×10 cross-side matrix. Unlike the 40×40 per-pair features you rejected, that's cheap, and `rules.damage_range` already computes it exactly.
   - Hero strength enters through those damage numbers, since the engine applies the hero's attack and defence to every unit.
   - A few variants make the short vector you asked for, about 2 parameters each: everyone; only units that can act this round; shooters vs the rest. Each gives our share, S_ours / (S_ours + S_theirs).

2. **Give it its own path, so it can't be drowned out.**
   - Feed the shares straight into the value heads and the type head through their own small linear layer, not only through the context MLP.
   - Start the final value head from that prior: V ≈ 2·share − 1 times a learned scale starting at 1. That's the same pattern as the priors files: a sensible starting value that training is free to change.
   - It also directly attacks the value head's poor calibration, which drives the advantages.

I'd build 1 and 2 together and test on recorded battles first: how well the untrained share alone predicts who wins, and whether the value head's explained variance improves. That takes minutes and needs no new runs.

## Why the search training failed while PPO worked, and how we could have predicted it

AlphaZero-style training only works if the search's target knows more than the network. That depends on one measurable quantity: how reliable the estimated value of each root action is. Here it was close to zero, and every number needed to see that was known before the run.

**1. Each action got one noisy sample.** With 16 simulations and 16 root actions considered, the halving schedule gives every action exactly 1 visit. Its value is one roll of the dice for that action, then one value-head estimate of the next position. AlphaGo used 800–1,600 simulations in a deterministic game, so one visit was already the exact value and there were dozens per move.

**2. Battles don't let the search reach the end.** Each simulation adds one node, so the tree is 1–2 decisions deep, while a battle lasts about 40. Almost no simulation ends a battle, so nearly all the information comes from the value head. Go and chess searches hit finished positions constantly near the end of games, and that real information flows back.

**3. Our value head was close to uninformative.** It was trained on very few battles. In the PPO runs its explained variance was below zero within each side, so its estimates carried little real information. (Fixed seeds keep that variance small, so it isn't directly comparable to the 72% above, but it was nowhere near informative.)

**4. The target amplifies whatever ranking it sees.** The values are rescaled to 0..1 at each node and then multiplied by (50 + 1) × 0.1, so the best-looking of the 16 actions gets +5.1 nats: about 164× its prior against the worst-looking one. If the ranking is noise, the target says "put most of the probability on a random action". PPO's update, by contrast, is clipped to ±20% per step and averages actual battle outcomes over 16 battles and 4 passes, so noise cancels and the signal adds up.

**What that predicts, and what happened:**

| Predicted | Observed |
|---|---|
| The gap between target and network won't shrink, because the target is fresh noise every time | It stayed at 0.45–0.55 for all 400 epochs |
| Greedy play gets confidently wrong (the network learns to be sure of near-random choices), but not worse than the untrained model | Attack fell from −0.01 at epoch 40 to about −0.9, against −1.55 untrained |
| Self-play still looks fine, because the search picks moves and both sides are equally noisy | The attacker still won 99% in its training games |

**The pre-run check that would have caught it** costs one minute: search the same position several times with different dice and compare the chosen action. If the choices agree about 1 in 16 times, the target is noise. A second check: what fraction of simulations reach the end of a battle, against how good the value head is. Near zero reaching the end with an uninformative value head means the search has nothing to add.

**What changes the prediction now:**
- The strength prior gives the value head 72% explained variance from the start, so search values finally carry real signal.
- 4 considered actions instead of 16 give 2–4 visits each, and a lower value scale stops one lucky sample from dominating.
- I'd rerun search training only after the repeated-search check shows high agreement.
