[2026-10-24 11:34] AI: **What the measurement is.** Every epoch, training logs `credit_<side>/killed` and `credit_<side>/own_lost`. Between two consecutive decisions of a side, it records:
- the fraction of our army we lost;
- the fraction of the enemy army we killed;
- how much the value head's estimate of the final reward (V) changed.

A least-squares fit then gives "change of V per whole enemy army killed" and "per whole own army lost". These changes of V are exactly what turns into advantages: with GAE, a decision after which V rises gets a positive advantage, one after which V falls a negative one. So the two coefficients show what the value head is teaching the policy.

**What was wrong.** For the side that loses here, the reward is −1 + the fraction of the enemy army killed. Every kill raises its reward, so an honest V should rise when it kills: roughly +0.47 per whole enemy army on our −1..1 scale. Instead:

| Run, epochs 1–40 | Defence: V change per enemy army killed | Per own army lost |
|---|---|---|
| v2 `up-ppo` | +0.12 (right sign, weak) | −0.06 |
| v3 without strength | **−0.06 (wrong sign)** | −0.10 |
| v3 + strength, fixed seeds | **+0.24** | −0.71 |
| v3 + strength, random seeds | **+0.36** | −0.60 |

In v3 without strength, kills made V *fall*, so killing earned negative advantages: the policy was taught to avoid it. That fits what we saw: the defence got worse than v2's (+0.07 vs +0.17 vs BattleAI), and the random-seed run drifted to defending in 40% of its decisions.

**Why the value head learned that, as far as I can tell.** I can't prove which cause it is:
- In these runs the defence loses every battle, from 2 fixed seeds, so V's label barely varies. Nothing makes V depend on kills in the right direction; it can latch onto whatever in the state happens to correlate with its small label differences.
- Kills mostly happen in exchanges, where the defence loses units at the same moment. "Engaged in melee" then looks like "about to be wiped out", and the fit puts part of that on the kills.
- v2 had the same weakness only mildly. Something in v3's changes made it worse, and I haven't pinned down what.

**How the strength prior fixes it.** The final value head now has a direct path from the strength share that starts at V = 2·share − 1. Here share = sigmoid(z), and z = log(our health ÷ theirs) + log(our damage per round ÷ theirs). Killing enemy units lowers their health and their damage, so z rises and V rises; losing our units does the opposite.
- **By construction, not learned:** that's why the sign is right from epoch 1, in both runs and for the attack too (its kill credit went from −0.03 to +0.48).
- **Still trainable:** the weight and the exponents can change if the data disagrees, but training starts from the right direction instead of having to discover it.

**One thing to watch.** Losing our own units now costs −0.6 to −0.7 per army. For a side that will lose anyway, its own losses don't affect its reward directly, because it keeps nothing. They only matter through the kills those units would have made later. That penalty may be too strong and make the defence avoid even trades. I'll check this in the round-2 review: whether the defence trades badly or hides, and its kill/loss credits by epoch 400.

[2026-10-24 11:49] AI: ## 1. Least squares

The value heads aren't trained with least squares. They already use what you suggest: each head is tanh, the −1..1 label is mapped to 0..1, and the loss is cross-entropy (`losses.value_loss`). Least squares only appears in `credit_check`, a logging diagnostic that fits "change of V" against "army fraction killed / lost".

- **MAE would be wrong for training.** It fits the median, not the mean. Advantages need expected values, so cross-entropy or squared error is right.
- **For the diagnostic, the loss choice isn't the problem; the question it asks is.** Kills and losses happen together in exchanges, and good players kill more because they're winning. So the fit mixes "V responds to kills" with "kills come with situations V dislikes". The negative coefficient I reported was partly that confounding, not proof that V penalises killing.
- **The right test is an intervention.** Take real recorded states, remove X% of one enemy stack in the inputs, and measure how V changes. Same for our own stacks. That measures the sign directly, with nothing confounded, and needs no new battles. I'd replace the regression with this.

## 2. Zero-sum: yes, I think it's the better design, and it fixes real problems

**We're already close to constant-sum, but by accident.** In a fight to the death the two rewards add up to about 2: the winner's "kept" fraction mirrors what the loser killed. The search relies on that (`other_side_value = 2 − x`). But each side's fractions are measured against its own starting army, and the round and mana penalties, retreat and surrender all break the symmetry.

**What I'd use instead: each side's assets.**
- Assets = remaining army value + hero value + mana value.
- R_A = (assets B lost − assets A lost) / (both sides' starting assets), in −1..1. R_B = −R_A exactly.

**What it buys:**
1. **One value function for both sides,** V_B(s) = −V_A(s).
   - Every self-play battle teaches both seats the same thing.
   - The value-gradient conflict between sides measured earlier (cosine −0.6) loses its main cause: two differently normalised rewards.
   - The search no longer needs the approximate formula for the other side's value.
2. **Killing is always good and losing always bad,** structurally, for both sides. The trained V can still be badly fitted, but the target has no case where a kill is worth less.
3. **The natural form of V is the strength prior.** The expected asset trade is roughly our Lanchester share, so V ≈ 2·share − 1 is close to the right answer, not just a starting guess.
4. **Pyrrhic results are valued correctly.** Trading 10k of army for 14k of theirs and then losing is good in a campaign, and this reward says so. Today's +1/−1 outcome term says it's terrible. The outcome itself then needs no special term: losing simply means losing the hero and the army.

**Mana, your idea, which I'd adopt:**
- **Value per point:** each mana point is worth c × spell power, with c fixed for now and later supplied from outside (near a Magic Well it's worth less).
- **Spending mana:** casting costs us its value, which replaces the current mana penalty.
- **Losing:** the hero and its mana are gone. If the winner takes the artifacts (as in H3), they move from loser to winner, which is zero-sum too.

**Hero:** the asset is hero value − default hero value: what you lose beyond a fresh hire. Retreat keeps it but loses the army. Surrender keeps hero and army but pays gold.

**The genuinely non-zero-sum parts, and what to do with them:**

| Event | What it does | Why it's non-zero-sum | Treatment |
|---|---|---|---|
| Surrender | the loser pays gold and keeps its army | I checked the server: the gold disappears (`giveResource(player, GOLD, −cost)`), the winner gets nothing | a per-side term |
| Experience for the winner | value created | the loser doesn't pay for it | a per-side term |
| Round penalty | a speed tie-break | applies to both | drop it, or make it tiny and symmetric |
| Later: Necromancy, Eagle Eye | value created from the battle | not taken from the enemy | per-side terms |

Two structural parts handle all of these:
- **A zero-sum core** that the shared value head learns.
- **Small per-side terms,** added to each side's own reward and learned by a small separate head.

These are rare events, so the extra machinery stays small, and the core carries almost all the learning. Without the split, every rare non-zero-sum event warps the main value head. That's the complexity you predicted, and I agree with you.

**Costs and risks:**
- The win/loss flag disappears from the reward. That's intended, but it changes behaviour: a side that will lose anyway is rewarded purely for what it trades, which is exactly what we want from Inferno here.
- Value heads, rewards, the search's leaf values and the logs all change at once, and old runs stop being comparable on raw reward. Our measure, reward minus BattleAI's on the same seeds, still works if we compute both with the new reward.

**My recommendation:**
1. Make the zero-sum asset reward the main reward, with surrender gold and the round tie-break as small per-side terms.
2. Train one value head for both seats, with V_B = −V_A built in.
3. Replace the credit regression with the intervention test.

It would be the next round after round 3, compared on the same measure. Want me to build it then?

[2026-10-24 15:22] AI: Stopped `g16`. The seed-2 replicate keeps running, since it measures the noise we need for judging these changes.

Yes, there are real gaps. Most matter more once battles vary, but the first two already affect castle_vs_inferno. In priority order:

**1. Creature abilities are learned per creature, not shared.**
- The vocabulary has 72 ability types across 150 creatures. `rules.py` reads about 17 of them; everything else exists only in the per-creature embedding.
- Each creature has to learn its own abilities from its own games. Nothing learned about Cerberus' three-headed attack carries over to Hydras, or from one flyer to another.
- With a varied generator each creature appears far less often, so this gets much worse.
- **Fix:** add an "ability bag" to the unit token: the sum of one learned embedding per ability the creature has. That is 72×64 ≈ 4.6k parameters.

**2. The damage features ignore abilities that decide fights, including several in this scenario.**
- Cerberus (three-headed) and Magog (fireball shot): area attacks, scored as single-target.
- Efreet Sultan, Fire Shield: melee attackers take damage back, which the features don't see.
- Champions, jousting: damage grows with the distance charged.
- Others: breath attacks, death stare, double damage, life drain, rebirth, regeneration.
- The damage model also leaves out hero Offense, Archery and Armorer, and artifacts. Once heroes vary, that is a large error.
- This plausibly explains the attack finding. Our Champions and Griffins open by meleeing the Sultans, the features rate that as free, and we lose twice BattleAI's Champions.
- **Fix:** the encoder (our agent code, not a VCMI patch) sends the engine's damage estimate per target. That is the same kill-range number the game's UI shows players, so it is fair information, and it includes every bonus and hero skill. `rules.py` stays for the features computed from it. BattleAI's attack evaluation already computes area damage and retaliation, so the encoder can reuse that.

**3. Retreat and surrender can't be priced.**
- The inputs have no surrender cost, and the hero's value (experience, artifacts) is not recorded.
- In lopsided random battles these become the most valuable decisions.
- **Fix:** add the surrender cost as an input. Give the RETREAT and SURRENDER logits a feature: the exact zero-sum reward of taking that action now, from `reward.py`. That needs 2 inputs and ~0 parameters, and it is also the per-side correction the TODO asks for.

**4. Spells that don't change health ignore their target.**
- Slow, Haste, Bless, Blind and Berserk get one learned value per spell, the same whichever unit they hit.
- **Fix:** give each target its share of its side's damage per round and of its health. The Lanchester code already computes these, so it costs no parameters.

**5. Remove parameters that only memorise and generalise worse with variety.**

| Embedding | Parameters | Why it can go |
|---|---|---|
| `hexes.obstacle_id` | 16k | Obstacle type plus accessibility already say what matters. |
| `heroes.hero_type` | 16k | ~180 heroes, each rarely seen; a hero's specialty already shows in its stats and the engine's cast values. |

That is −32k parameters (−10%), more than paying for #1.

**6. Smaller points:**
- Hard-coded scales (attack/20, round/20, mana pool/50, hero skills/10) saturate softly for high-level heroes. That's acceptable.
- Walls aren't in the damage model, so the generator should leave sieges out at first.

I'd do #2 and #1 first, because they can be tested on the current scenario: does the Champions' loss drop and the attack beat BattleAI? Then #5, then #3 and #4 together with the generator.

**Attack outcomes (2), by playing each attack instead of modelling rules:**
- Each legal attack of the acting unit is played by VCMI's own rules on a copy of the battle. So everything counts, including abilities nobody listed (spells cast after an attack, acid breath):
  - retaliation;
  - three-headed and all-adjacent attacks, breath, Magog fireballs;
  - Fire Shield;
  - life drain, including permanent resurrection, priced as the reward prices it;
  - death stare, death blow, jousting, rebirth, regeneration;
  - obstacles on the way, hero skills and artifacts.
- The dice are at their expected value: damage rolls at their mean, and every luck or ability roll played both ways and weighted by its chance.
- **Cost:** about 5 ms per decision. Attacks from different hexes that hit the same units are played once; between tries only the battle is reset.
- **Checked:** Familiars come out at exactly their 900-gold value. The Archangel hitting Efreet Sultans nets +355 instead of about +1,100, because of Fire Shield. The Champions' value changes with the hex they charge from.
- **Model features from it:** the attack's value, its value against the best target, and each from-hex against the target's best hex.
- **Damage estimates:** every other damage feature (threats, strengths, summaries) now uses the engine's own estimate, which includes hero skills. On ~6,800 real pairs it equals the old Python formula exactly, since these heroes have no damage skills.
- **Engine change, not a bug fix:** the battle dice sit behind a small interface, and the existing battle-copy code got a battle-only reset (7 files, ~60 lines).
- **Limit:** threats and strengths use the estimates, not played attacks, so for them area attacks and death stare are still approximate. Only the acting unit's own attacks are exact.

**Abilities (1):** each of 68 creature abilities gets one embedding, shared by every creature that has it.

**Spells:** each target's value is now a feature. Which unit to Slow, and why, is left to the model to learn.

**Retreat and surrender:** the exact zero-sum reward of retreating or surrendering now, plus the score if the battle ended now, go into the action-type logits. New inputs: surrender cost, each side's starting army and mana, and kept creature counts.

**Scales (6):**
- Fixed scales are replaced by x/(x+c) at three scales, so 4 vs 10 mana and 400 vs 1,000 mana both stay distinct. That covers mana, mana pool, spell power and knowledge, unit attack, defence and buffs, shots, casts, round, effect turns and strength, and obstacle turns.
- Hero attack and defence: as you suggested, both differences against the other hero, as the damage formula's factor (+5% per point up to +400%, −2.5% per point down to −70%). No hero counts as 0 attack and 0 defence.
- Spell power was there before, but squashed by /10; it's now multi-scale.
- What's still fixed is game geometry, each with a comment: speed of 15 (the field width), the retaliation cap, 7 tactics columns.

**Removed (5):** the hero-type and obstacle-id embeddings. The model now has **286.6k parameters, down from 317.2k**.

Old checkpoints can't play on the new contract. Their recorded scores remain the baseline.

[2026-10-24 16:21] AI: **1. Speed: no slower.** Measured over epochs 2–41:

| Run | Epoch | Waiting for battles | Eval (32 battles) |
|---|---|---|---|
| old model (`up-zs-rs-lin`, 2 seeds) | 8.7–9.3 s | 0.2 s | 25–31 s |
| new model (`up-v11`, 2 seeds) | 8.3 s | 0.0 s | 20–21 s |

The 5 ms isn't large next to the network itself, which takes ~16 ms per decision in the C++ log. Battles also run while the previous epoch trains, so play time is hidden; training is the bottleneck. I can't fully explain why eval got faster; the old runs may have been slowed by the memory leak that was fixed in between. The cost would matter with search on, since the search encodes many states per decision. For that, `restoreBattle` could become a copy-on-write layer.

**2. x/(x+c): it can't divide by zero.** Each c is a fixed positive constant (5, 20, 80, …), and x is clamped to ≥ 0, so the denominator is at least c. The signed version uses |x| + c, which is the same. The inputs are integers, so infinities can't come in either. The bounds test checks that every feature is finite.

I think it's a good choice:
- **vs log1p(x):** that is unbounded and puts 1 vs 2 and 500 vs 1,000 at the same distance.
- **vs a single x/(x+c):** that saturates.
- **What a bank of a few fixed scales gives:** a bounded and smooth result, without extra parameters, and the next layer learns how to weight the scales.
- **Trainable c:** possible, but it adds parameters for little gain, which is what you asked to avoid.

**3. Issues found along the way.** Already fixed:
- The MPS memory leak that filled swap (fixed earlier).
- Fixed-seed training overfits: the attack's shooters stopped shooting. Random seeds only from now on.
- Changing the features broke the frozen `model_v2`; patched.

Still open:
- **Hero value is always 0.** The reward and all the inputs ignore hero experience and artifacts, so a hero worth 30k counts the same as a fresh one. That makes retreat and surrender wrong once the generator varies heroes. It needs inputs (level/experience, artifact values) and `summarize` to count them. **This is the most important one.**
- **Effects with no health change count 0:** spells cast after an attack that blind, petrify or slow, and Hypnotize. That's the same as non-health hero spells: the model has to learn their value from the ability bag and the target value.
- **Exact only for the acting unit's attacks:** threats, strengths and unit summaries use pair estimates, so they still miss area attacks, Fire Shield, death stare and double damage.
- **Dice approximations:**
  - Each attack is cut off at its 8 most likely dice branches. That only matters with many chance rolls at once (luck on both sides plus abilities); none come up in this scenario.
  - For random picks other than damage (rebirth's extra creatures, random spell choice), the simulation takes the median outcome rather than the mean.
- **Spell effect strength:** `u_effect_value` keeps only the first bonus of a multi-bonus effect.
- **Eval is narrow:** one scenario, against BattleAI only, with ±0.01 noise between seeds. Small differences between runs aren't meaningful until the varied generator exists.

I'd fix the hero value before building the generator. Shall I?

[2026-10-24 16:30] TOOLS: Bash

[2026-10-24 16:30] AI: **My recommendation for hero value:**

```
hero_asset = 2000 × levels_above_1 + Σ prices of its artifacts (war machines included)
levels_above_1 = (level − 1) + the fraction of the way to the next level (from experience)
```

**Why 2000 per level, not 1 gold per XP** (what `reward.py` sketches now):
- **The XP curve explodes; a level's worth doesn't.** Past level 12, each level needs 20% more XP than the last, so per-XP pricing would value a level-30 hero at hundreds of thousands of gold. Yet each level always gives the same thing: one primary point and one secondary-skill level. Value should be linear in level.
- **2000 is the game's own price for a level.** The Tree of Knowledge sells one for 2000 gold (or free, or 10 gems).
- **The fraction keeps it smooth:** a hero halfway to the next level is worth half a level more.
- **It's on a sensible scale:** a level-10 hero comes to about 18k plus artifacts, comparable to one of these 15k armies. Losing your main hero should hurt about that much.

**Artifacts at their configured prices.** VCMI gives a dead hero's removable artifacts to the winner (verified in `BattleResultProcessor`: worn and backpack items, not the Grail). Destroyed war machines are removed and spent charges discharge.

**What each outcome leaves:**
- **WIN:** army, hero and mana, plus the loser's artifacts.
- **LOSS:** nothing. The levels are gone, since another player can rehire the hero, and the artifacts go to the winner.
- **RETREAT:** hero (levels and artifacts) and mana, minus the 2500 rehire.
- **SURRENDER:** everything, minus the cost and the rehire.

An artifact changing hands moves the zero-sum score by twice its price: once as our loss, once as their gain. Correct for a relative score; a dead creature moves it once. The score stays exactly zero-sum.

**Deliberately left out:**
- **Experience the winner earns** (1 XP per hit point killed, +500 for a hero) creates value nobody pays for. It stays on the non-zero-sum TODO.
- **Primary points from shrines and the Arena:** probably ~1000 each, but separating them from level and artifact bonuses needs more engine plumbing. I'd add it with the generator, not now.

**Inputs:** a hero's level is visible, its artifacts are not. So the reward uses true values, and the inputs give the model:
- **our hero:** level, experience fraction, artifact value;
- **the enemy hero:** level only (it learns to expect their artifacts).

**Area attacks, so you can correct it before I build it:**
- **Exact, from the engine:**
  - For our attacks: the number of enemy and own units each attack hits, from each hex. The engine already computes which units it hits, so the counts are free, and the attack's value is already played.
  - For spells: the same two counts per target, from the spell's affected units.
- **Slow path, not deceptive, for enemy area attacks next turn:**
  - Per unit: the value of its allies and of its enemies standing next to it.
  - Per move hex: the value of our units next to that hex.
  - Together with the Hydra's abilities in the ability bag, the model can learn "Hydras like grouped targets, so don't group next to one".
- **What I'd skip:** playing every enemy's attack on every target, which costs a lot.

Shall I go ahead with 2000 per level?

I read "creature cost is like 1 level at least" as: a fresh hero's hire price, with the creatures it comes with (2500), covers the first levels, and one level is worth at least that much. So:

```
hero_asset = 2500 × max(0, L − 3)^1.3  +  artifacts at market price
L = level + the fraction of the way to the next level
```

- **Below level 3:** 0, as you asked.

| Level | Value (gold) |
|---|---|
| 4 | 2,500 |
| 10 | ~31,600 |
| 20 | ~100,000 |
| 30 | ~181,000 |

- **Configurable:** both 2500 and 1.3 go in `RewardConfig`, so they're one-line changes.

[2026-10-24 17:26] AI: I found the cause of the slower learning; it wasn't the fewer parameters. The fixed model (v12) is retraining on seeds 1 and 2 and finishes in about 70 minutes; I'll report total_score against the old model's +0.118 and +0.104 then.

**The regression.** v11 finished at total_score **+0.052 and +0.027**, against +0.118 and +0.104 for the old model, and nearly all of the loss was on defence. The defence killed 41–56% of the attacker instead of 69–73%, and in the eval games it scattered and ran from fights. I switched off one group of changes at a time on seed 2 and checked the defence at epoch 80:

| Switched off | Defence kills (epoch 80) |
|---|---|
| nothing (v11) | ~21% |
| engine attack values | 18% |
| ability bag | 23% |
| **retreat/surrender/score-now features** | **73%** (old model: 57%) |

The culprit is `score_now`, the score if the battle ended now, which I had added to the battle-wide input. The value heads simply read it off. Running from a fight delays losses in that score, so retreating looked good, even though the defence loses everything at the end either way. It's removed from the model's general input. The exact retreat and surrender rewards now feed only the RETREAT and SURRENDER choices. I added a comment in the code so it doesn't come back.

**Retreat/surrender prior.** The fixed −6 bias now lifts only when we're much weaker. "Weaker" is measured by the health × damage ratio the strength features already compute, so the enemy hero's attack and defence count through the units' stats; its spells don't count.

| Enemy army vs ours | Escape bias |
|---|---|
| even, or we're stronger | about −6, as before |
| 3× | about −5.3 |
| 5× | −3 |
| 10× | about 0, as likely as waiting |

My first threshold (half the bias lifted at 3×) let a third of the lost battles in the first epochs end in an escape. A losing side gets weaker every turn and gets many chances to escape, so a per-decision bias adds up. With the lower threshold, early epochs average about 0.5 escapes per 8 defence battles.

**Hero value**, as you specified: 2500 × max(0, L − 3)^1.3, plus artifacts at market price. L includes the fraction of the way to the next level. Details:
- **Artifacts:** the winner takes the loser's artifacts, using VCMI's own transfer rule.
- **War machines:** I left them out. One destroyed in battle is removed before the winner takes the rest, and the army value doesn't count them either.
- **Enemy hero:** VCMI doesn't show a player an enemy hero's level, so in the inputs it's 0 alongside its artifacts. The reward uses the true values.
- **Checks:** the C++ and Python rewards are tested to agree exactly, levels and captures included.

**Area attacks**, as you asked:
- **Exact path:**
  - every legal attack counts the enemy and allied units it hits from each hex;
  - every legal spell target does the same for the units it affects.
- **Slow path:**
  - per unit: how much of its own side and of the enemy stands next to it;
  - per hex: how much of our army the moving unit would end up next to.

  With the ability bag, that's enough to learn that Hydras like grouped targets and not to group next to one.
- **A bug in my own first version:** I counted hits with BattleAI's helper, which also counts allies for Cerberus. The real attack uses a different engine function that only hits enemies there; I switched to that one.

All 113 tests pass, and everything is committed and pushed. Model size is 288.8k parameters.

**Bugs found, all from playing generated battles:**
- **Sieges were played by StupidAI (fixed, committed).** Arrow towers stand on off-field hexes, and our pair-damage encoding made every siege decision throw. Bug in our AI code.

- **The model didn't see tome or scroll spells, and read spell mastery from skills (contract v13, built and tested on the dev AI).** It now gets the spells the hero can actually cast, and the engine's own mastery, which also covers magic plains and the elemental fields. The v12 weights load unchanged.
- **Checked and fine:** ONNX vs torch agree to 1e-5 on generated battles, the training forward equals the played forward (now a test), and no NaN anywhere.

- terrains and phases;
- force ratios;
- armies by faction, with counts from weekly growth (the first version gave 10,000 peasants);
- heroes with real levels, class level-ups, guild spells limited by Wisdom, and artifacts in free slots;
- war machines;
- sieges with fortification level and garrison-only defences;
- neutrals, which wait on a small engine change (not a bug fix).

40 generated battles ran with 0 errors at 0.1–1 MB each.

**Bugs found by playing generated battles**
1. **Sieges were played by StupidAI, not the model.** Arrow towers sit off the battlefield, and our damage encoding threw an error on every siege decision. Fixed; this was our AI code.
2. **Every experiment so far had hidden random artifacts.** VCMI's battle-only mode applied the player's random starting bonus. In about a third of games a hero silently got a random treasure artifact, otherwise gold or resources. That is why adding the hero value changed 18 of 32 eval results. Fixed in the engine; this is a bug fix. Old scores were measured with this noise, so they are slightly off.
3. **The model didn't see spells from tomes or scrolls.** It also read spell mastery from school skills, which is wrong on magic plains and the elemental fields. New input version (v13) takes both from the engine; the v12 weights load unchanged.
4. **Our earlier battle-only engine patch ended neutral battles before they began.** Splitting the monsters into stacks triggered its end-of-game check. Fixed.

One false alarm: an ONNX-vs-torch mismatch was my own file mix-up. With matching weights they agree to 1e-5 on generated battles, and no NaN appears anywhere. The training forward also matches the played forward exactly; I added a test for that.

**Generator (`battle_model/generator.py`, sampling your `generator.toml`)**
- **What it covers:**
  - battle types: hero vs hero, sieges (fortification level, with or without a defending hero), and neutrals (always fight, split into stacks the way the game does);
  - all land terrains, the game phases and force ratios;
  - armies built by faction;
  - heroes with real levels, level-ups by class odds, mage-guild spells limited by Wisdom, artifacts in free slots, and war machines.
- **One rule changed from your file:** the first version gave armies of 10,000 peasants. Stack counts now follow weekly creature growth, and the first stack is always the army's top level. Your level weights now only choose which levels appear.
- **Smoke tests:** 80 generated battles of every type ran with 0 errors, at 0.1–1 MB per battle (0.5–9 MB/min).
- **Not supported yet:** creature banks, boat battles, special battlefields, partial mana and player gold (which decides whether surrender is affordable).
- **Model gaps:**
  - war machines and the tactics phase are played by StupidAI, not the model;
  - a big siege can have more than 10 units per side, so the lowest-value ones are dropped from the model's view.
- **Not wired into training yet,** since you wanted to discuss the design first.

**OOD baseline (40 generated battles, vs BattleAI)**

| Battle type | Epoch 400 | Epoch 1000 |
|---|---|---|
| hero vs hero, attack | −0.134 | −0.091 |
| hero vs hero, defence | −0.044 | −0.054 |
| vs neutrals | −0.163 | −0.139 |
| siege | about even | −0.002 / −0.070 |
| **total_score** | **−0.091** | **−0.081** (noise about ±0.04) |

Most of the gap comes from close fights that BattleAI wins and we lose outright, not from retreats or surrenders. That's expected after training on a single matchup, and it's what training on the generator should address.

[2026-10-25 00:00] AI: The retreat feature is computed correctly. The enemy hero's hidden value only changes the denominator, and it does so the same way at the start and the end. So this is learned behaviour, not a bug, and the cause is plausible:
- **In self-play the opponent is the model itself,** and it also retreats when losing. Winning therefore pays little (the loser escapes with its hero), while losing a fight to the end costs the army, the hero and the artifacts the winner takes. Both sides learn to retreat early.
- **BattleAI never retreats,** so against it fighting on to a win is worth much more than the model expects.

That's a self-play equilibrium effect. You asked for self-play only, so I won't mix BattleAI into training. I'll track the retreat count per eval: 7 at 250, 7 at 500, 10 at 750. If it keeps rising while the score stalls, I'll flag it.

**What went wrong:** our AI hands every unit to StupidAI after round 30. In one month-3 castle siege, StupidAI on both sides stalemated: the walls stood and neither side could reach the other. VCMI has no round limit, so the battle never ended.

**Three bugs stopped training:**
1. **The game hung when the winning hero levelled up.** Its level-up dialog waits for a player turn that battle-only games never have. Fixed in the engine (bug fix): the game now ends as soon as the battle result is accepted.
2. **The game hung when a battle ended before anyone moved,** for example arrow towers killing a tiny attacking army. My earlier neutral-battle change set its "battle started" flag too late. Fixed in the engine (bug fix): the flag is now set when the battle starts.
3. **A siege stalemated forever after round 30.** StupidAI takes over every unit at that point, and VCMI has no round limit. Fixed in our AI: from round 50 on it retreats whenever it can.

Each fix was tested on the battle that hung plus 40–80 mixed battles. Everything is committed and pushed.

**Two things I didn't change:**
- **Retreats dominate defence.** About 40% of self-play battles end in a retreat. With your hero value, a mid-game hero is worth about as much as its army, so retreating from a losing fight is cheap and reward-correct; I checked the reward, the retreat input and the advantages. Against BattleAI, which never retreats, the model also gives up some even defences that BattleAI goes on to win. Those cost about −1.5 of reward over the 20 eval battles.
- **The eval set is too small.** 10 scenarios can't confirm improvement eval by eval. Something like 40 scenarios would cost about 1 minute per eval instead of 15 s.

If you want to change either, the options are the hero value, some non-self-play opponents, or a bigger eval set; your call.

Nothing is left running, and generated files are cleaned up after every epoch. The run folder is 80 MB, with checkpoints every 250 epochs.

[2026-10-25 08:14] AI: Yes, a lot has improved. The final model is the best we've had, though only partly on the eval's own numbers:
- **On 40 fresh generated battles it improved steadily:** +0.064 at epoch 1000, +0.081 at 2000, +0.098 at 4000.
- **The 10-scenario eval plateaued after epoch 1000:** it moved between +0.044 and +0.095 and ended at +0.089. That's within its noise, so it neither confirms nor contradicts the steady gain.

**On the stalemate rule:** you're right that it needs the catapult destroyed and no towers left. My rule is broader than the one you'd accept, though. From round 50, **either side** retreats if it can, whether or not damage is being done. I can narrow it to "the attacker retreats after N rounds in which nothing was killed" if you want.

**On retreats:** I agree the problem is retreating when the enemy can't actually finish us before our next turn. A side-level feature for "the most we can lose before our next turn" can be computed from what we already have: each enemy stack's best attack on us (the engine's damage estimates), plus the enemy hero's strongest damage spell. A second feature for the Blind-plus-shooting lock on our best stack is harder, since we can't see the enemy's spellbook. Expected Blind effect from the hero's power is the realistic version. I haven't built either.

## Results

| Measure | Start | Now |
|---|---|---|
| castle_vs_inferno vs BattleAI (evals 200–400) | +0.074 (up-ppo) | +0.118 (old model); **+0.135** (v12, evals 800–1000) |
| Generated battles vs BattleAI (40 fresh battles) | −0.048 (castle-trained model) | **+0.098** (generator-trained) |

## Improvements

**Reward and training**
1. Zero-sum reward from each side's assets (army, hero, mana). It became the single measure, total_score.
2. Hero value in the reward: 2500 × (level − 3)^1.3 plus artifacts; the winner takes the loser's artifacts; war machines excluded.
3. Linear learning-rate decay.
4. Random battle seeds instead of fixed ones (fixed seeds overfit and collapsed).
5. Retreat/surrender prior that depends on strength: about −6 normally, lifted only when we're about 5–10× weaker.

**Model inputs and architecture**

6. Lanchester strength features for each side.
7. Attack values played by the engine: every legal attack is simulated on a copy of the battle with expected dice. That covers retaliation, area attacks, Fire Shield, life drain, death stare, jousting and obstacles.
8. The engine's own damage estimates replace the Python damage formula.
9. Ability inputs: 68 creature abilities, shared across creatures.
10. Value of a spell's target, so buffs and debuffs can be learned.
11. Multi-scale saturating features instead of fixed divisors, and hero attack/defence as the damage formula's factor.
12. How many enemy and allied units each attack and spell hits, plus "next to" features, so the model can learn about area attacks.
13. Retreat and surrender rewards feed only the retreat/surrender choices.
14. Spell mastery from the engine (covers magic plains, elemental fields, specialties), and the spell list now includes tome and scroll spells.
15. Hero-type and obstacle embeddings removed, giving fewer free parameters.

**Bugs found and fixed**

16. **v11 regression:** I had fed the current score into the battle input; the defence learned to run from fights.
17. **GPU memory leak:** MPS compiled a new graph for every batch size, grew to 9+ GB and filled swap. Fixed with fixed batch shapes.
18. **Sieges:** arrow towers made every siege decision throw, so StupidAI played all sieges.
19. **Random starting bonus (engine bug):** a third of all games gave a hero a random artifact, so every experiment so far had hidden noise.
20. **Three hangs:** the winner's level-up dialog, the "battle started" flag set too late (neutral and tower battles), and siege stalemates after round 30.
21. **Area-attack counts** used a BattleAI helper that wrongly counted allies for Cerberi; they now use the engine's own function.
22. **Armies of 10,000 peasants** from the generator: counts now follow weekly growth, and the top-tier stack is always included.

**Infrastructure**

23. The battle generator, from your `generator.toml`. It covers all land terrains, the game phases, force ratios and faction armies. Heroes get levels, class level-ups, guild spells limited by Wisdom, and artifacts. It also does war machines, sieges (fortification level, garrison-only) and neutrals.
24. The map tool can now set experience, artifacts by slot, towns and monsters.
25. Engine support for battle-only sieges with chosen fortifications, and for neutral battles.
26. Training on generated battles, with an eval played from both sides against BattleAI's own result.
27. Checks: ONNX and torch agree to 1e-5, no NaN in any module, the training forward equals the played forward (now a test), and the C++ and Python rewards agree exactly.

[2026-10-25 08:58] TOOLS: Bash×3, Edit

[2026-10-25 08:58] AI: Short answers first:
- **Neutral monsters:** yes, about 40% of generated battles are a hero against neutral monsters. BattleAI plays the monsters, which also gives us a non-self opponent.
- **Staggered data:** yes. Run many games at once as separate processes that all feed one replay buffer, and draw every minibatch across games. The design is below.

## Next experiment: design

### Goal and pass criteria
"No exploitable failure modes" can't be proven; it can only be tested for. So the run counts as a success when all of these hold:
1. Positive total_score, with a confidence interval above 0, against **BattleAI, MMAI and StupidAI** on every eval set (section 4).
2. No category significantly negative. Categories are battle type × phase × force-ratio bucket × side, so a failure in, say, defending sieges can't hide in the average.
3. The failure-mode metrics (section 4) stay under their thresholds.
4. An **exploiter**, a fresh model trained for a few hours only against the frozen final model, can't reach a clearly positive score. If it can, its battles show us the exploit.

### 1. Data
**Sources, 50/50 by decisions:**
- **Generated battles:** the generator as today, plus the gaps fixed in section 2.
- **Real games:** headless random-map games with 2–4 Nullkiller2 players, a mix of templates, sizes and factions.
  - Every player's battle AI is ours, sampling at temperature 1, and each side's record is kept.
  - Neutral monsters are played by BattleAI.
  - A game is cut at about 3 months of game days or when one player dominates, then a new one starts.

**Actors (14 cores):**
- About 5 real-game processes and 6 generated-battle loops run continuously.
- The learner trains on the GPU.
- All records go to the learner over the existing record socket.
- Models are published by atomic rename of `current.onnx`. Each battle picks up the newest model, because the AI reloads it at battle start.

**Replay buffer (this answers your concern):**
- One FIFO per source.
- **Batches are stratified:** half from each source, and **no single game may supply more than 1/8 of a batch**. Within a source, battles are sampled uniformly.
- **Staleness cap:** records more than 2–3 model versions old are dropped. PPO's ratio already uses the probabilities the recording model had, so slightly stale data is handled. Each battle is used about 4 times, as now, then dropped.
- So consecutive battles of one game never fill a batch. They arrive interleaved with ~5 other games and the generator, and a game's battles are spread over many updates.

**Trivial battles:** real games are mostly a big hero crushing small neutral stacks. Those would flood the data with easy wins. Battles where the enemy holds under about 5% of the assets get a lower weight or are subsampled, and we log how many per source.

**Opponents (league), so self-play can't settle into a shared blind spot:**
- Generated battles: 70% current self-play, 15% past checkpoints, 15% fixed AIs (BattleAI, MMAI).
- This is the main defence against a self-play equilibrium like the retreat one.
- Real games stay self-play, apart from neutrals played by BattleAI.

### 2. Must fix before the run (exploitable or wrong today)
1. **The tactics phase.** Our AI skips it, but real games are full of Tactics heroes, which is a free advantage to any opponent. The model needs to place units in the tactics zone; the action types are already reserved in the contract.
2. **War machines.** Ballista targeting (it matters with Artillery), catapult targeting (with Ballistics) and the First Aid Tent are played by StupidAI today. Move them to the model; CATAPULT and HEAL are reserved too.
3. **Remove the round-30 handover to StupidAI.** The model should play every round. The stalemate rule becomes yours: the attacker retreats after N rounds in which no unit died.
4. **Units per side:** 10 can drop arrow towers, war machines or summoned units in big sieges. Raise it to about 14, or never drop towers, war machines or anything that can act this round.
5. **Retreat features, as you suggested:**
   - **Worst-case loss before our next turn**, as a share of our assets. For every enemy unit that acts before our next turn, its best attack on us (the engine's damage estimates), plus the enemy hero's best damage spell at its power and mana.
   - **What we can kill before they act**, the mirror of the first.
   - **Blind-lock risk:** the enemy hero's power and mana against Blind's cost and duration, as a proxy for losing our best stack to Blind plus shooting. It can only be a proxy, since we can't see the enemy's spellbook.
   - These go into the battle input and onto the retreat and surrender choices.
6. **Our own hero's artifacts as an input:** a bag, like the ability bag. Artifacts with no visible stat (Orb of Vulnerability, Recanter's Cloak, …) are partly visible today only through the engine-played attack and spell values. The enemy's artifacts stay hidden.
7. **Generator gaps that real games will hit:**
   - special battlefields (the map tool places the terrain object);
   - partial mana and player gold, which decide whether surrender is affordable (needs a small battle-only engine change);
   - creature banks and boats can wait.
8. **Battle → scenario exporter:** at each real battle's start, write it out as a scenario file. That gives:
   - a realistic eval set (section 4);
   - real statistics to replace the generator's CALIBRATE guesses.

### 3. Reward decisions (yours)
- **Retreat is too cheap.** It keeps the hero for free, but in the game you pay 2,500 gold to rehire it and lose tempo. I'd charge the rehire, as surrender already does, plus a small fixed tempo cost.
- **The winner's experience isn't counted.** With your level formula a big win can be worth a level, and leaving that out makes winning look worse than it is. The exact gain is known from the battle result. This is the TODO already in `reward.py`.
- Together these make fighting on in a close battle worth more, which addresses the retreat behaviour without hard-coding anything.

### 4. Evaluation, every ~30 min, all fixed sets
1. **40 generated two-sided battles:** our model and BattleAI each play both sides; our result minus BattleAI's.
2. **20 generated neutral battles:** ours vs BattleAI's result as the hero.
3. **40 real-game battles** from the exporter, both sides.
4. **castle_vs_inferno, 16 seeds:** a regression check on the old scenario.

All against BattleAI; sets 1 and 3 also against MMAI and StupidAI. MMAI is built, but I still need to check that its model loads in our setup. That's about 2–3 minutes per eval instead of 15 s, and the noise drops from about ±0.05 to about ±0.02.

**Failure-mode metrics, logged every eval:**
- retreat or surrender while V > 0, or while the worst-case loss before our next turn is under 30% of our assets;
- shooters moving or waiting when they could shoot unblocked;
- spells cast at zero predicted value when a positive one was available, and casts on war machines or towers;
- dying with most of our mana unspent;
- rounds with no damage, i.e. stalling;
- per-category scores (section 0's grid).

### 5. Run plan
1. Smoke stage, about 1 hour:
   - every fix above runs 100 mixed battles with no errors or fallbacks, apart from those labelled;
   - real games run for 1 hour with no hangs;
   - throughput is measured for each source.
2. Main run: about 24 hours, linear LR, a checkpoint at every eval, the same bug-stop-fix-resume routine as tonight.
3. Exploiter test on the final checkpoint: about 3 hours.

**Model size:** keep 289k parameters unless value explained variance stalls while the data clearly isn't learned. Widen only on that evidence.

### Order of work
- **First:** the real-game runner, the buffer and actors, and the scenario exporter. Without them the experiment doesn't exist.
- **Then:** tactics, war machines, removing the round cap, and units per side. These are a contract change.
- **Then:** the retreat features and the artifact bag (contract change), the reward decisions above, and the generator gaps.
- **Last:** the eval suite with the MMAI check.

The contract changes (items 1–6 in section 2) fit one version, v14. It changes the inputs and enables the reserved actions, so the current weights don't carry over as they did for v13. The run starts from scratch, which is cleaner anyway with the new data mix.

I added the hero-embedding TODO to `HeroTokens` in `model.py` and pushed it (29622f1). It says why a hero-identity input would let the model memorise repeated campaign heroes, and when to reconsider.

[2026-10-25 11:58] AI: The four neutral dragons are levels 8 and 10 in VCMI's data, and the generator only accepts levels 1–7, so they could never appear. I'll fold them into the level-7 pool, which gives them the very small chance you want.

## How I'd address your points

**Neutral creatures in hero armies.**
- They're already in the generator: 5.9% of stacks, so 28% of armies have one (sharpshooters, nomads, mummies, trolls, …). That's more than "very small".
- I'd lower it to about 2% per stack, so about 10% of armies have one.
- The level 8/10 dragons go into the level-7 pool, as above.

**Exploits found by playing against the model.** A TODO: a "hard cases" pool. A battle where you beat the model is exported as a scenario (the exporter below), kept in the pool, used as a small share of training battles, and replayed in every eval. Not built now.

**The model plays neutral monsters too, in real and generated battles.**
- Our AI becomes the neutral battle AI, and the monsters' side is recorded like any other.
- **Two code gaps:**
  - The record and seed settings are per player colour, so a "neutral" colour has to be added.
  - VCMI reuses one battle-AI instance for every neutral battle in a game, so the model would never reload. Fix: reload at battle start whenever the model file has changed.
- **Learning neutral battles:** the monsters can't retreat or surrender (the engine masks it). Otherwise they learn the same way.
- BattleAI stays only as a league opponent.

**Example weights.** Every example gets a weight in the policy and value losses.
- **Initial guess:** `w = clamp(smaller side's share of both sides' assets / 0.1, 0.05, 1)`. An even fight gets 1. A 100k hero against 2k of neutrals gets 0.2.
- **TODO** in the code: replace it with "value of the forces fighting / the player's whole empire" once the campaign AI computes empire value.
- **Note:** advantages are already normalized per side over each epoch, so trivial battles' tiny advantages are diluted today, but only implicitly. The weight makes it explicit and also covers the value loss.

**Tactics, a proposal.** The tactics phase is one placement decision per unit, which fits the existing model with no new head:
- The engine gives the side with tactics a turn per unit, and that unit is the "acting unit" as usual.
- **The two choices** (both already reserved in the contract):
  - TACTICS_MOVE: place the unit on a hex, using the existing move-hex head masked to the engine's legal tactics hexes;
  - TACTICS_END: leave the rest of the army as it is.
- The existing `tactics` battle feature tells the model it's in the tactics phase.
- Credit comes from the battle's result like any other decision.

**War machines:**
- **Ballista:** SHOOT with the existing target head.
- **First Aid Tent:** HEAL with the unit head, masked to our wounded units.
- **Catapult:** CATAPULT with the hex head, masked to wall parts that can be attacked.
- When the hero lacks the skill (Artillery, First Aid, Ballistics), the engine decides whether we're even asked, so our masks just follow it.

**No StupidAI anywhere.**
- The model plays every round, war machines and tactics included.
- A model error becomes a counted error that plays a legal Defend. Errors must stay at 0, and the smoke tests fail if they don't.
- A missing model is a hard failure, not a fallback.
- **Stalemate:** the attacker retreats after 30 rounds in a row with no unit killed or damaged. In our setups the attacker is always a hero, so it can always flee.
- The BattleAI delegate stays, for the baseline evals only.

**Units per side:** 14.

**Retreat features, all learned; the model decides how much to trust them:**
- **Worst case before our next turn:** the sum, over enemy units that act before our next turn (from the turn queue already in the inputs), of each one's best attack on any of our units, using the engine's damage estimates, as a share of our assets.
- **The mirror:** what we can kill before they act.
- **Enemy hero's spell threat** (the enemy's spellbook is hidden):
  - whether it can cast;
  - its mana against common spell costs;
  - its power at several scales.
- **"Lose one full stack" risk:** the value of our best stack as a share of our assets, so it can be crossed with the spell threat.

All of these are computed in Python from inputs we already have; none needs a contract change. They feed the battle input and the retreat/surrender choices.

**Artifacts, your design:**
- A fixed list of rule-changing artifacts only. I'll build the list from the bonus types that aren't plain stats, about 20 artifacts.
- They come in as a multi-hot vector for our hero, mapped through a Linear down to about 4 dims before joining the hero input. That's about 80 parameters.
- The enemy's artifacts stay hidden.
- Multi-hot rather than "top 3": it's simpler and the bottleneck already limits it.

**Generator, as varied as possible:**
- **Cheap now:**
  - special battlefields (the map tool places the terrain object);
  - the Tactics skill (allowed again once tactics is modelled);
  - neutral dragons;
  - a wider tail of extremes: a few % of very high levels and huge armies;
  - partial mana and player gold: one small battle-only engine change. Mana doesn't survive hero setup, and gold can't be set in maps.
- **Later:**
  - creature banks: their visit dialog has the same "UI not ready" problem as level-ups, so they need an engine path;
  - boat battles.

**Reward:**
- **Retreat pays the hero's rehire (2,500),** as surrender does.
- **Experience:** I agree with you. Leave it out, because the reward should stay battle-local and zero-sum, and campaign-map gains aren't counted either. I'll note it in `reward.py`'s TODO list.

## Revised plan

**Contract v14:**
- 14 units per side;
- tactics, war-machine and heal actions enabled, with engine masks;
- a neutral colour for records;
- the rule-artifact input;
- a stalemate counter (rounds with no damage).

Starts from scratch.

**Python:**
- the retreat features;
- the artifact bottleneck;
- example weights;
- the retreat rehire cost;
- the replay buffer with stratified sampling.

**Generator:** the changes above, plus a battle → scenario exporter at each real battle's start (for the eval set, the CALIBRATE numbers and the future hard-cases pool).

**Real-game runner:**
- random-map games with 2–4 Nullkiller2 players;
- our AI for every battle side, neutrals included;
- cut at about 3 months of game days;
- the model reloads whenever its file changes.

**Actors and learner:**
- About 5 real-game processes and 6 generated-battle loops.
- Records go into per-source buffers, sampled half real and half generated by decisions.
- At most 1/8 of a batch from one game; records more than 3 model versions old are dropped; each battle is used about 4 times.

**League** (generated battles only): 70% self, 15% past checkpoints, 15% BattleAI or MMAI.

**Eval every ~30 min:**
- 40 two-sided generated battles;
- 20 neutral battles (ours as the hero, and also as the monsters);
- 40 exported real-game battles;
- castle_vs_inferno.

All vs BattleAI; the generated and real sets also vs MMAI. Plus the failure-mode metrics and the per-category grid.

**Run:**
1. Smoke stage: 100+ mixed battles and 1 hour of real games with 0 errors, plus throughput measured.
2. Then about 24 h.
3. Then the exploiter test.

## Double-check: what I found
1. **Neutral dragons** never appear (above).
2. **The learner may become the bottleneck.**
   - It handles about 280 decision-passes per second, i.e. about 70 new decisions per second at 4 passes.
   - 11 actors may produce about 60 per second.
   - That's tight. If the learner falls behind, I'll drop to 3 passes rather than let the data go stale.
3. **Buffer memory:** a decision is about 0.3 MB in memory, mostly the attack-value tables, so a buffer of about 3,000 decisions is about 1 GB. That's fine with 24 GB, but it needs a hard cap.
4. **The neutral AI instance is reused** across a game's battles (above). Without the reload, neutrals would play an old model forever.
5. **Real-game dialogs:** in real games, level-ups and other dialogs are answered by Nullkiller on its own turns, so the battle-only hangs don't apply. Creature-bank and joining dialogs come from the adventure AI, not us.
6. **Surrender in generated battles depends on gold,** which is fixed today. Without the gold change, the generator teaches the wrong price of surrender.
7. **MMAI** is built (`libMMAI.dylib`), but I haven't verified it runs as an opponent in our harness. It's the first smoke check.

## Ready?
I'm ready to start coding with the defaults above. Before I start, please confirm or change these four:
1. the tactics design (one placement per unit, plus "leave the rest");
2. stalemate after 30 rounds in a row with no damage;
3. the example-weight formula as the initial guess;
4. the league mix of 70/15/15.

The order of work:
1. the real-game runner and the buffer/actors (the riskiest part);
2. v14 (actions, units, neutral colour, artifacts, stalemate);
3. features, weights and reward;
4. the generator additions and the exporter;
5. the eval suite.

I'll report each piece as it passes its tests, and I won't start the long run until you say so.

[2026-10-25 18:49] USER: "Optimisation: KL is 0.014" => what is this? a new loss somehting you monitor?

[2026-10-25 18:49] AI: It's a diagnostic, not a loss, and it isn't new: `approx_kl` has been in `progress.csv` (and TensorBoard under `stability/`) since the first PPO runs.

**What it measures:** how far one update moved the policy away from the one that played the battles. For each chosen action in the update, take its log-probability under the old model minus its log-probability under the updated model, then average (computed in `losses.ppo_loss`). That's an estimate of the KL divergence between the old and new policy.

**How to read it:**
- **About 0:** the policy isn't changing, so nothing is being learned (dead gradients, LR too low).
- **About 0.005–0.02:** normal PPO steps. The current 0.014 is here.
- **Above about 0.05:** steps too big. PPO's clipping is fighting the optimiser, and training tends to become unstable or collapse.

It isn't used in training at all, only watched. Its companion `clip_fraction` (the share of decisions whose probability ratio hit PPO's ±20% clip) tells the same story.

[2026-10-25 18:55] USER: is the thing that is running now the "20261025-1656-smoke-long/tensorboard/20261025-165650"? does it represent a real training run with all the processes into the dataloader and data coming from both real games and our scenario sampler? are they staggered? does the "eval/total_score" there mean our new eval (on sampled games) and not the Castle VS inferno one?

[2026-10-25 18:56] AI: Mostly yes, with two caveats about what TensorBoard shows.

**Which run:** yes. `20261025-1656-smoke-long` is the run folder, and `tensorboard/20261025-165650` is the TensorBoard folder that run created at start. It's a smoke run, capped at 300 updates (about 1 h). Otherwise it uses the settings I'd use for the long run.

**Real data from both sources:** yes.
- **Actors:** 6 generated-battle actors and 5 real games run at once. They're threads that launch VCMI processes, not torch DataLoader workers; a DataLoader is built from the chosen battles at each update.
- **Records:** every battle record goes into one buffer. Real games send theirs as each battle ends, while the game is still going.
- **Measured so far:** about 51% of each update's decisions come from real games, from about 10 different games per update. Records are about 1.1 model versions old on average (at most 3), and each battle is used by one update only.

**Staggered:** yes.
- No real game may supply more than 1/8 of an update, so one game's consecutive battles are spread over many updates, interleaved with 4 other games and the generator.
- Within an update, decisions are shuffled before the PPO passes.

**`eval/total_score`:** it's the new eval, not castle_vs_inferno alone. Against BattleAI, each eval plays 208 battles, every battle from both sides:

| Set | Battles | Share |
|---|---|---|
| Generated, two-sided | 40 × 2 | 38% |
| Generated, vs neutrals | 20 × 2 | 19% |
| From real games | 40 × 2 | 38% |
| castle_vs_inferno (regression check) | 4 × 2 | 4% |

Two gaps in what TensorBoard shows:
1. Only the BattleAI eval is logged there. The MMAI eval goes to `eval_MMAI.csv` and the console.
2. The score isn't split by set, so a gain on real battles could hide a loss on castle_vs_inferno. I'll log a score per set (generated, neutrals, real, castle) and per opponent before the long run.

[2026-10-25 21:52] AI: The first long smoke run finished its 300 updates. Learning has started, but the run hit two problems at the end, both fixed now, and a validation run is going.

**Results (score = our reward minus the opponent's own on the same 208 battles):**

| Update | vs BattleAI | vs MMAI | Retreats while ahead | Worthless casts |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | −0.031 | −0.037 | 0 | 0.64 |
| 100 | −0.040 | −0.049 | 0.125 | 0.56 |
| 200 | −0.017 | −0.031 | 0.052 | 0.38 |
| 300 | −0.029 | −0.028 | 0.051 | 0.37 |

300 updates is too short to say more. The previous run needed about 80k decisions to move, and this one saw 120k across a much more varied mix.

**Issues found in the smoke runs, all fixed:**
1. **Real games were stuck on one model.** They resolved the model link once at game start, so they never got newer models. The file was also deleted under them after 6 updates, and every later battle in that game failed to load a model.
2. **Updates came up short.** The cap of 1/8 of an update per game also hit the generated battles, so the second side of each self-play battle never fitted. The cap now applies only to real games, and an update waits until it can be filled.
3. **Exported scenarios broke the record format.** VCMI's "compact" JSON still contains line breaks.
4. **The worthless-cast metric ignored the side.** Cast values are attacker-minus-defender, so the defender's sign was wrong.
5. **Nullkiller2 crashed (SIGSEGV) after one of our retreats.** It compared a removed hero through a dangling pointer. It's a VCMI bug fix: compare by the hero's id. Our model retreats far more often than BattleAI, which is why this rare path came up.
6. **CPU starvation late in the run.** Nullkiller2 plans its turns on every core, and in the middle game its 5 real games starved the battles. Battles were killed as stalled, and the last eval took 2 h instead of 7 min. Real games now run at low priority, so battles always get the CPU first.

**Working as designed:**
- 0 model errors and 0 server rejections;
- 51% of each update from real games, from about 10 games, with records about 1.1 versions old;
- learner memory flat at about 3 GB (5.7 GB during evals);
- tactics, catapult, tent, neutrals on both sides, gold/mana and special battlefields all exercised.

**Still open:**
- **The learner is the bottleneck.** It takes about 8.6 s per 400 decisions and never waits for data, so the actors' surplus is dropped. I'd use 2 PPO passes instead of 4.
- **An eval against both opponents takes about 7.5 min**, so run it every 30–45 minutes.

The validation run (450 updates, about 1.5 h) checks the priority fix in the real games' middle game, the crash fix and the new 50/50 score. I'll report when its evals come in.

The first stack came through `getHeroRoleOrDefaultInefficient(path.targetHero)`, which builds a `HeroPtr` from a hero pointer stored in a path. [2026-10-26 00:26] AI: The MMAI result at eval 545 was fake: MMAI was off and BattleAI played in its place. It's fixed and the 11-hour run keeps going.

**What happened**
- MMAI only works when its mod is listed in the shared VCMI mod settings file.
- The generated-battle actors build their battle maps in parallel, and every build rewrites that file: VCMI empties it, then writes it again.
- Now and then one build read the file while it was empty, fell back to the default mod list (no MMAI) and saved that.
- From then on every game started without the mod, and MMAI quietly played as BattleAI, which is its built-in fallback.

**Which results are affected**
- Eval 545: only the MMAI half is wrong. Its rows match the BattleAI rows exactly.
- Evals 189 and 377: real MMAI games, because their MMAI results differ from the BattleAI ones.
- Score so far: TOTAL went from −0.038 at update 0 to +0.012 at update 377. Eval 545 shows +0.018, but that number counts BattleAI twice.

**Fixes**
- **For the running process (it still has the old code):** I put MMAI back in the shared file and made the file read-only, so nothing can empty it. New games have MMAI loaded again. The file needs to be made writable again after the run, which also matters if you use the VCMI launcher. This is noted in memory.
- **Permanent fix (26b150c):** each thread builds its maps in its own small user folder, so the shared file is never written. The folders of exited processes are deleted, and the build logs stay small.
- **Guard (4e22867):** an eval now refuses to run against MMAI if the mod isn't active, instead of quietly scoring against BattleAI.
- Both commits are pushed and the 122 tests pass.

I didn't restart the run: the read-only file already protects it, and the new "worthless cast" definition isn't worth the risk of resuming mid-run.

The next eval, around 23:45, will be the first real MMAI result since 377. Run time so far: update ~585 at ~10 s per update, and a real game is still crashing about 4 times in total. The crash catcher hasn't caught one yet; a game it ran just hit its 900 s limit with no crash.

[2026-10-26 01:32] AI: The crash catcher found a second kind of crash, and it's fixed and pushed (vcmi `d8c48ba91`, labelled bug fix). It isn't the segfault (exit 139) we were hunting.

- **The bug:** in Hoard, a siege that had just started, the game-server code inside the client aborted (exit 134) with "Movement terminated abnormally". When a unit is sent to a hex it can't reach, the path lookup returns an empty path at distance 0. That passes the range check, and the move code then throws an error nothing catches, which kills the whole game.
- **The fix:** an empty path is now rejected like any too-distant move ("Given destination is not reachable!"). The battle carries on, and the unit just loses that move.
- **Still open:** our AI only offers moves to hexes the engine reports as reachable, so why it sent this one is unknown. The log has no detail on the action. The crash is rare (about 1 in 40 games), so I'm not chasing it further.
- **Rebuild:** the rebuild redid all 832 targets, including the shared libraries running games load. Training wasn't affected: no new actor failures, and it's at update 904, ~11 s per update.
- **Segfault:** the Nullkiller2 segfault after "Recruit X" is still not caught after ~40 debug games. It costs about one real game an hour and the battles it already sent are kept. I'm not restarting the catcher, since its two extra games take CPU from training.

The next eval is due around 00:45.

[2026-10-26 02:06] AI: The eval and two real-game actors had been stuck because macOS's `sample` tool hung. I unblocked them and fixed `headless.sh` (`66e61a7`, pushed).

- **What was stuck:** when a game goes quiet, `headless.sh` runs `sample` to save a stack trace before killing it. `sample` itself hung, and three scripts waited on it:
  - the current eval: about 6 minutes on one battle, with training paused because actors stop during evals;
  - two real-game actors: about 2 hours each. That's why real-game data fell from 0.50 to ~0.33: only 3 of the 5 real-game actors were producing.
  - A fourth hung script was an orphan left from an earlier run around 22:02; I killed it too.
- **Unblocking:** I killed the hung `sample` processes, and the scripts finished. The eval retried the failed group of battles, and the retry passed.
- **Fix 1:** the stack sample now has a 20 s deadline.
- **Fix 2:** a silent game that is still using CPU no longer counts as stalled. The stuck eval battle wasn't frozen: MMAI had handed over to BattleAI, which can think for over a minute in a big battle. Under the old rule that would kill an eval battle every time it happened, and three failed attempts in a row end the whole training run. Busy loops are still capped by the time limit (300 s for evals, 900 s for real games).
- **Applies now:** every new game reads `headless.sh` fresh, so both fixes are already in effect without a restart.

The eval result for update 1055 should come in shortly.

[2026-10-26 02:32] AI: The real-game segfaults (exit 139) are fixed: it was a data race in Nullkiller2, the AI that plays the map side of real games (vcmi `400b76421`, labelled bug fix, pushed).

- **Cause:** a hero levels up after a battle, and Nullkiller2 handles that on a separate thread that rebuilds its hero-role table. At the same time its turn planner was reading that table from parallel threads without taking the lock, and the reads hit freed memory.
- **How I found it:** macOS had saved a crash report for every one of these crashes. All four point at the same lookup, `HeroManager::getHeroRoleOrDefault`. The debug client wasn't needed, so I removed it and reverted its uncommitted engine change.
- **Fix:** the three planning steps now hold the same lock as the rest of Nullkiller2's state. None of them waits on anything else, so it can't deadlock. A 200 s test game reached day 60 with 187 battles, so no hang.
- **Takes effect:** real games load Nullkiller2 when they start, so new games already run the fix.
- **One lost battle:** a generated battle that started during the rebuild failed, because the build briefly removes the client's config folder.

Tonight's fixes so far: MMAI silently off, two kinds of real-game crash, and the stack-sample hang that froze an eval and two real-game actors. All are in memory. The next eval is due around 01:35.

[2026-10-26 02:34] AI: A fourth cut-off record, and two in a row came from the same actor's games, both neutral battles. That fits a different explanation from bad timing. When a game is killed at its time limit, the shutdown may end any battle still in progress, and our AI sends a record for it. The 1 s SIGKILL then cuts that record off. If so, some records of unfinished battles might arrive complete, with an outcome that never happened. Checking when our AI sends a record:

[2026-10-26 02:34] TOOLS: Bash×2

[2026-10-26 02:34] AI: That's not it. Our AI sends a record only at `battleEnd` with a real battle result, and a killed game ends no battles. So a cut-off record can't be an unfinished battle, and no fake outcomes get in.

The likelier cause: the receiving thread in the trainer competes with training for Python's interpreter lock and sometimes reads slowly. The game's send then blocks once the socket buffer is full, and a 900 s time-limit kill that lands during that wait cuts the record. Neutral battles are the most common kind, which is why they show up most. It costs a few battles out of hundreds and is safe to leave alone.

[2026-10-26 03:00] TOOLS: Monitor, Bash

[2026-10-26 03:00] AI: Eval 1242 came in at a TOTAL of −0.009 (vs BattleAI +0.013, vs MMAI −0.031). Behind the drop is a design mistake in the training, now fixed (`0fc65e0`). The run has resumed with the fix at update 1346, with 4.34 of the 11 hours used.

**What went wrong:** shooters stopped shooting. In the eval, the share of decisions where a unit could shoot but moved instead went from 0.03 at update 877 to 0.53 at 1242. Win, loss and retreat counts barely changed, but battles got longer: castle battles took 44 decisions instead of 24.

**It was learned, not a bug:** I replayed the full eval set vs BattleAI on today's binaries with two saved models:

| Model | Shooter idle | Mean reward |
|---|---|---|
| model 850 | 0.10 | 0.025 |
| model 1200 | 0.42 | 0.023 |

So the binaries are fine and the policy changed.

**Cause:** the entropy bonus, which is meant only to keep the policy from settling too early, was biased toward moving.
- It was computed over the whole action, so an action type with many follow-up options earned more bonus. MOVE has about 100 hexes to pick from, SHOOT a few targets, WAIT none.
- That paid MOVE roughly 0.05 per decision. That's small against the advantage signal, but it pushes the same way every update, while the advantage noise averages out.
- Once the real gains flattened out, that steady push won.

**Fix:** each sub-choice still has its own entropy bonus, but the bonus no longer pushes the choice of action type toward types with more options. The entropy value logged is unchanged. A new test checks this; it fails on the old code and passes on the new.

**Restart:**
- I stopped the trainer cleanly and resumed from the latest checkpoint (saved every update), so nothing was lost but the record buffer.
- I also fixed resume so the actors keep numbering models from 1346 on. Before, numbering restarted at 0 and would have overwritten the saved past models that self-play also plays against.
- The resume also picks up the earlier Python fixes: the new worthless-cast definition, per-thread map-build folders, and the MMAI-mod guard.

I didn't roll back to model 877: it would cost about 1.3 hours of the 11, and its score wasn't better overall. The open question is whether shooters go back to shooting on their own now that the push is gone. The next eval is at about 02:30 and will tell.

**The weak spot is neutral battles.** That set is negative against both opponents in every eval: BattleAI and MMAI do better in our seat than our model does. I compared it battle by battle against BattleAI playing the same seat:
- **Worst case:** our hero attacks 635 skeletons with Archangels, Swordsmen, Griffins, Behemoths and Cavaliers. Our model made 67 moves and 2 melee attacks, then retreated (−0.34), while BattleAI wins the same battle (+0.11).
- **Not the entropy drift:** model 850, from before the drift, does the same thing (132 decisions, then a retreat).
- **Likely gap:** beating one huge stack needs coordination. One unit takes the stack's single retaliation for the round, then the others hit it freely, and the model hasn't learned that. In self-play the monsters are also our model and may defend better than BattleAI's, so attacking looks worse in training than it really is.
- **Ruled out:** dropped battles. No generated battle was killed at its time limit, so long battles aren't silently vanishing from training.

This is a gap in what the policy has learned, not a bug, and more training is the remedy for now. It's in memory as a candidate for the hard-cases pool or for search. The run continues, with about 5 hours left.

Against BattleAI it's at +0.058, its best. Against MMAI it's +0.016 to +0.039, noisier. These are still small margins, not the large lead you want.

**What I changed tonight**

Each item is committed and pushed, and the 123 tests pass.

1. **Entropy bonus pushed the model toward moving (design mistake, `0fc65e0`).** The bonus was computed over the whole action. So action types with many follow-up options, like MOVE with ~100 hexes, earned more of it than SHOOT with a few targets, or WAIT with none. Over time shooters drifted from shooting in 90% of the turns they could to 55%. I proved it was learned, not a bug, by replaying the same eval battles with an older and a newer model. The fix keeps each sub-choice's own entropy but stops it from favouring types with more options. I resumed from the latest checkpoint at 02:00, and scores have improved since.
2. **MMAI was silently off.** Battle maps built in parallel wrote over a shared VCMI settings file. A half-written copy could drop the MMAI mod, and MMAI then plays as BattleAI without any error. Maps are now built in folders of their own, and an eval refuses to run against MMAI if the mod isn't active. Only the MMAI half of eval 545 was affected.
3. **Real-game crashes, both engine bug fixes:**
   - The segfault (exit 139): two threads in Nullkiller2 used its hero list at once, one updating it after a level-up while its planner read it. The planner now takes the lock. The macOS crash reports had the stack all along.
   - The abort (exit 134): sending a unit to a hex it can't reach aborted the whole game. The move is now rejected instead.
4. **Stuck games (`headless.sh`).** macOS's `sample` tool hung forever, which froze an eval and two real-game actors for about two hours. That's why real-game data had dropped to a third of each update instead of half. It now has a 20 s deadline. Also, a silent game that's still computing (BattleAI thinking in a big battle) is no longer killed as stalled; the time limit still applies.
5. **Resume renumbered the models from 0**, which would have overwritten the saved past models that self-play also plays against. It now continues from the newest.
6. **Smaller:** worthless casts now count only casts that lose us value, so buffs no longer count; the MMAI mod-settings file is read-only for now (make it writable again after the run); I removed the debug client and its uncommitted engine change.

**Known issues I left alone**
- **Neutral battles:** the model is weakest against one huge monster stack. It circles and retreats where BattleAI wins, so it hasn't learned to absorb the stack's single retaliation with one unit and then pile on.
- **Mana unused when losing:** in lost battles the hero leaves about 60% of its mana unspent.
- **Freezes on two maps:** real games occasionally freeze on Freedom and The Challenge, about one game an hour.
- **Diagnosing those freezes:** that needs stack traces. Every debugger attach hangs because macOS developer mode is off. Enabling it is your call: `sudo DevToolsSecurity -enable`.
