[2026-10-26 09:15] AI: Point by point.

**Debuffs and Force Field.** Those aren't counted as worthless. The metric only flags casts with a negative immediate value, like a Frost Ring that hits our own stacks. Debuffs, buffs, Force Field and summons score 0 immediate value, so they never count. The metric can't tell whether a Force Field in front of shooters was a good idea; only the battle outcome can. So it catches the clearly dumb casts and stays silent on the clever ones, which is what we want.

**Hard scenarios.** Agreed. After the run ends (about 15 minutes), I'll add a hard-scenario share to the generator. To start it will hold one huge neutral stack against a hero whose spellbook draws from the spells that matter there: Blind, Bless, Slow, Haste, Shield, Force Field, Berserk, Forgetfulness and similar. It's a generator category with its own probability, so we can add more kinds as we find them.

**Mana when losing.** Agreed, the metric is crude: a hero that dies early leaves mana through no fault of its own. A better one: in lost battles, the share of rounds where the hero could cast and didn't. I'll switch to that.

**Freezes.** Agreed, I'll find the cause. With developer mode on, the watchdog's stack sample should now work. After the run ends I'll play several Freedom games in parallel until one freezes, and read where every thread is blocked.

**Is the noise in the eval or in training? Both, and the numbers point to three things:**

1. **The MMAI half of the eval is randomised.** MMAI samples its moves at temperature 0.5 with a random seed (`mmai-settings.json`), so the same model on the same battles scores differently each eval. That matches what we see: BattleAI results move smoothly (+0.034 → +0.058), MMAI results swing ±0.03. The fix is a fixed MMAI seed: same strength, but repeatable.
2. **Each eval set is small.** About 128 greedy battles per opponent, and one flipped battle moves the score by ~0.005–0.01. I'll measure the real error bar by playing the same model twice. My guess is about ±0.015, which would make most of the eval-to-eval swings tonight noise.
3. **The training signal is thin, and the learner is the bottleneck, not the data.**
   - Each update is 800 decisions, only about 15 battles, so the gradient is driven by ~15–30 battle outcomes. With value explained variance at ~0.35, the advantages are still mostly outcome noise. On top of that they're normalised per side over those ~15 battles.
   - The learner waits 0.03 s per update for data and trains for 10.9 s. The record buffer often fills to its 8000-decision cap and drops the oldest, so the actors' battles are being thrown away.
   - More data per update is therefore nearly free. Next round I'd try 3200 decisions per update with minibatches of 256 instead of 64: about 4× the battles per gradient for a similar number of steps, smoother advantage normalisation, and no wasted battles. First I'll profile the 11 s: if most of it is turning records into examples on the CPU rather than the gradient steps, that part should run in parallel.
   - The update steps themselves are healthy and small: KL 0.0065, clip fraction 0.07.

**Other lessons for the next round:**
- **A bias in the objective beats noise.** The entropy bias was only about 5% of a standard deviation per decision, but it was systematic, and over ~1000 updates it moved behaviour a lot. Any term that isn't zero-mean — the action-type prior, the per-battle weights, the retreat reward — deserves the same check.
- **The critic is the lever.** Value explained variance is ~0.35–0.45. A better value head directly cuts advantage noise, more than a bigger batch would.
- **The time-based schedule needs care across resumes.** The learning rate follows elapsed hours across a resume. That's fine, but a long run should save `run_hours` with the checkpoint so it isn't lost.
- **The eval cost fits the 10% budget,** about 3 minutes every 30. If the error bar turns out to be ±0.015, I'd make the set bigger and eval less often, say 2× the battles every 60 minutes, rather than act on noise.

Once the run finishes, I'll do these in order:
1. Measure the eval error bar.
2. Profile the learner.
3. Hunt the freezes.
4. Add the hard scenarios and the new mana metric.
5. Fix the MMAI seed.

[2026-10-26 09:21] AI: **1. Is the mana measure a loss?** No, it's only a metric. It's logged for the evals and TensorBoard and never feeds the loss.

**2. The bottleneck.** I overstated it earlier. Data production and training are roughly balanced, and both are slow:

| Stage | What it does | Rate |
|---|---|---|
| Actors (11 game processes on 14 cores) | Our AI spends ~70 ms of CPU per decision: 38 ms model inference + 32 ms attack-value simulation | ~75–90 decisions/s, all actors together (real games also burn CPU on Nullkiller2's map turns) |
| Learner | 800 decisions per update, 2 passes in minibatches of 64 on the Apple GPU | 10.9 s per update ≈ 73 decisions/s |

The learner never waits (0.03 s per update), and only a modest surplus of battles is dropped. So the whole system trains on about **75 decisions/s, ~1.5 battles/s**, and each gradient sees only ~15 battle outcomes. That's the thin signal.

Making it much faster with a stronger signal takes both ends:
- **Learner:** a minibatch of 64 is far too small for the GPU; per-step overhead dominates. Minibatches of 512 should cost about the same per step and handle ~8× the data. Updates of ~6400 decisions (~120 battles per gradient instead of 15) also fix the noisy per-update advantage normalisation. The ONNX export after every update costs time too; with bigger updates, publishing every update is fine. First I'll profile the 11 s (CPU data preparation vs GPU steps vs export).
- **Actors:** at ~70 ms per decision the CPU caps us at ~150 decisions/s even if nothing else ran. Two targets:
  - The model at 38 ms on one thread: profile the ONNX graph. The raw-input transforms inside the graph, such as the exposure features over hex pairs, may be the heavy part.
  - Attack values at 32 ms: they re-simulate 5–15 attacks per decision, and could be cached or simplified.
- **Plan:** profile both, then size the update to what the actors produce. Halving the per-decision cost and batching the learner properly should give several times today's throughput, with ~10× more battles behind each gradient.

**3. A better value head, when the trunk already does most of the work.** The trunk is shared, so the value head is only as good as what the trunk learns. Several things limit it beyond capacity:
- **Irreducible noise.** Explained variance can't reach 1. Dice and the temperature-1 sampling in self-play make the outcome random even from a known position. I'd measure the ceiling by replaying the same positions many times and taking the spread of outcomes. If the ceiling is ~0.5, we're near it and the fix is variance reduction, not a better network. If it's ~0.9, the network is the problem.
- **We don't bootstrap from the critic.** GAE λ = 0.95 over ~25 decisions per battle is nearly pure Monte Carlo: each decision's advantage is mostly the final outcome's luck. With explained variance now ~0.4, λ ≈ 0.8 would lean on V and cut the variance a lot. The cost is some bias while V is wrong; it's cheap to try.
- **Too few outcomes per gradient.** Value learning from ~15 outcomes per update is slow. The larger updates above help the critic most.
- **The policy and value share the trunk and compete.** The value loss weight is 0.5 with a shared trunk. Common fixes are a value-only trunk, or the phasic approach: extra value-only passes on the same data, plus distilling the value into the policy network.
- **Find where it's wrong.** Split the value error by situation (sieges, spells, neutrals, lopsided battles) to see where it fails. A targeted feature, like the value ratio and exposure features, usually helps more than size.

The run ends in about 10 minutes. Then I'll do, in order:
1. Measure the eval error bar.
2. Profile the learner, the ONNX model and the attack values.
3. Measure the value ceiling.
4. Catch a Freedom freeze with a working stack sample.
5. Add the hard scenarios and the new mana metric.
6. Fix the MMAI seed.

[2026-10-26 09:30] AI: Agreed on both: bigger minibatches and no per-update export. Here's how I'd lay out the training so it uses the hardware properly, both here and on the RTX 5090 machine.

**Central inference instead of ONNX inside each game**

Today every game process runs its own copy of the model on one CPU thread, at 38 ms per decision. The trainer exports a new ONNX file every update, and each game reloads it. Both are wasteful: the CPU does work the GPU is built for, and the export is a serial step that more hardware doesn't speed up.

The better design is one inference server inside the trainer, working from the live PyTorch weights in GPU memory:
- Our AI in each game sends its encoded inputs over a local socket, as it already does for records, and gets the model outputs back.
- The server gathers the requests from all running games and evaluates them as one batch on the GPU. One forward pass for 64 decisions costs about the same as for 1.
- **No export:** after each update the server just switches to the new weights in memory. Records still say which weights version played, so stale records can still be dropped.
- **The CPU only runs VCMI:** today that's ~70 ms per decision, about half of it model inference. The other half is attack-value simulation, which is next to optimise.
- **On the 5090:** one GPU easily runs both inference for ~100 parallel games and the learner. The CPU then sets how many games run, which is what we want, since games are the part that has to be on the CPU.
- **ONNX remains** only for checkpoints and the finished product, which must run without Python.

This is the standard actor/learner layout of large RL systems: SEED RL, and AlphaStar-style setups. It's the biggest single gain available. It means C++ work in AlphaBattleAI (a socket client in place of the ONNX session) plus a batching server in Python.

**Learner, a config change only**

Minibatches of 512 and updates of ~6400 decisions, about 120 battles per gradient instead of 15. That gives 12 steps per pass, about what we have now. The learning rate may need to go up somewhat with the bigger batch; I'll check KL and clip fraction. First I'll profile where today's 11 s per update goes, so we know whether CPU-side data preparation also needs moving off the main thread.

**Persistent battle processes, to check**

Every generated battle starts a whole VCMI client and server, loads the mods and the map, and plays about 25 decisions. If start-up is a large share of a battle's time, one long-lived process playing battle after battle would be a big gain. I'll measure that during the profiling.

**"Bootstrapping from the critic"**

That's about how each decision is judged, the advantage:
- **Monte Carlo:** advantage = final outcome − V(before the decision). Every decision carries the luck of everything that happened after it.
- **Bootstrapped (TD):** advantage = V(after the decision) − V(before it). Each decision is judged by how much the critic thinks it improved the position. That's much less noisy, but it's only as good as V.

GAE λ blends the two: the final outcome's weight is λ to the power of the number of steps left. I also need to correct myself: with λ = 0.95 a decision 12 steps from the end gets 0.95^12 ≈ 54% of its signal from the final outcome, and one 25 steps away 28%. So today's training is about half Monte Carlo, not nearly pure as I said. With V's explained variance at ~0.4, λ = 0.8 would put ~7% on the outcome for a decision 12 steps out. It's a cheap experiment with a real chance of cutting noise.

**Separate value network, quickly**

Agreed: sharing isn't bad in itself, and the test should be fast. Two policy-training runs side by side would take hours and be noisy. Instead:
1. Collect a fixed dataset once from the current model, e.g. ~200k decisions.
2. Train the value head offline both ways, shared trunk and separate, on the GPU in minutes.
3. Compare their explained variance on held-out battles.

The same dataset also gives the value ceiling: replay the same positions many times and measure the spread of outcomes. If separate doesn't predict clearly better offline, it won't help online either. If it does, it's worth a full run.

**Order**

1. Profile the learner and the start-up cost of a battle.
2. Switch to minibatch 512 and 6400-decision updates.
3. The offline value benchmark: shared vs separate, plus the ceiling.
4. The central inference server.

[2026-10-26 12:23] AI: The 11-hour run finished at **+0.044** in the final eval (+0.055 vs BattleAI, +0.033 vs MMAI), up from −0.038 at the start. Since then I've done the engineering pass. Training throughput on this Mac went from **73 to ~194 decisions/s**, and each update now covers ~40–60 battles instead of ~15. Everything is committed and pushed, and the 128 tests pass.

**Speed:**
- **Central inference server.** Games now ask one server for the model's outputs over a local socket. That removed two big costs: creating an ONNX session in every battle (1.1 s of CPU each) and exporting the model after every update (1.3 s). Actors now always play the newest weights. In a 5-battle test, game CPU dropped from 15.6 s to 4.7 s.
  - It works for more than battles: a model family is a network plus its input/output contract, so the campaign AI can reuse the same server and client.
  - On this Mac it runs as 2 CPU processes. Apple's GPU was slower for this model and crashes when two threads use it. On the 5090 it will run as one CUDA process.
  - The ONNX path stays, for evals and the finished product.
- **Distance maps** rewritten with the same results (exact match on 2145 real positions): all the in-graph features are 2× faster. They were half the model's compute.
- **Attention bias:** a CPU training step is 5× faster and a GPU step ~20% faster, with identical outputs.
- **Bigger updates by default:** 3200 decisions, minibatches of 256, 2 passes. I left the learning rate at 1e-4. One correction to what I told you earlier: on this Mac's GPU, 512-minibatches are only ~1.5× more efficient per example, not 8×, because the model is many tiny operations rather than heavy compute.

**Bugs found:**
- **Real games played a stale model.** The model cache was keyed by the `current.onnx` link, so each real game kept its first model for its whole 15 minutes, while its records named the newest version. About half of every update was ~80 updates old and still passed the freshness filter. Fixed and checked with a model switch mid-game.
- **The "freezes" on Freedom and The Challenge were games that had ended.** The headless client only quits when the red player wins or loses, and those maps have no red player. It now also quits when anyone wins (engine bug fix, labelled). All three seeds that froze now finish cleanly.

**Your other points:**
- **Hard scenarios:** 10% of generated battles. The first kind is one big stack against a hero worth 0.6–1.4× its army, with a spellbook, full mana, and a few of Blind, Bless, Slow, Force Field, Berserk and similar, as far as the hero's Wisdom allows. There's also a new "hard" eval set; the existing eval battles are unchanged, so scores stay comparable with v14-11h.
- **Mana metric:** replaced by "rounds the hero could cast but didn't, in lost battles". It's only a metric, not a loss.
- **MMAI eval:** now repeatable. I fixed its seed (strength unchanged), and the same battles now give identical results.

**Decision needed: a persistent battle process.** VCMI start-up is now ~0.6 s of the ~1.1 s of CPU per battle. On the 5090 machine, where the GPU will be fast, that makes start-up the largest actor cost, and one long-lived VCMI process playing battle after battle would roughly double the battles per core. That needs a real engine change, a mode that loads the next battle instead of exiting, and it isn't a bug fix, so it goes against the minimal-engine-changes rule. Do you want it?

**Next, if you agree:**
1. The bootstrapping test (GAE λ ≈ 0.8 vs today's 0.95).
2. The offline value test: shared vs separate value network, plus how much of the outcome is irreducible noise.
3. A learning-rate check for the bigger batches.

On this Mac the learner is now the bottleneck. About 60% of its slowdown comes from competing for the CPU with the games, the server and its own actor threads, so moving the actor threads into a separate process is a smaller follow-up.

Training throughput on this Mac is now **238 decisions/s**, up from 73 this morning, with ~40–60 battles behind every gradient instead of ~15. Everything is committed and pushed, and the 128 tests pass.

**The persistent battle process.** The engine change is labelled as not a bug fix. With `VCMI_TEST_QUEUE` set, a headless VCMI client plays game after game in one process. Each game gets its own environment and settings, and newly written maps are found by rescanning the maps folder. On the Python side each generated actor keeps one such client; a failed or stalled battle restarts it, and it also restarts every 300 battles. In a test, one process played 10 different battles at **~0.28 s of client CPU per battle, against ~1.1 s before**.

**Tuning:**
- Generated battles now run at low priority (`nice 10`), like real games. The learner was starved for CPU: 15 s per update in a run against 9.4 s alone.
- That's not Python's interpreter lock: a learner in a separate process slowed down even more. So splitting the trainer into processes wouldn't help.
- The default is now 10 generated actors. On this Mac 16 starved the learner and 6 left it waiting.

**On the 5090 machine** the learner and the inference server run on the GPU, so the CPU only plays VCMI, at ~0.3 s per battle. I expect several times this Mac's throughput there, limited by its core count. I can't measure that from here.

**Next: the bootstrapping experiment (GAE λ 0.8 vs today's 0.95).** First I need a baseline on the new setup: the bigger updates change how much it learns per hour, so v14-11h isn't a fair comparison. I'm starting that baseline now, 3 hours from scratch at λ = 0.95, with an eval every 30 minutes. It uses 10 generated actors, 5 real games, a real-game share of 0.4 and learning rate 1e-4. Then the same 3 hours at λ = 0.8, compared eval by eval; the eval is now repeatable against both opponents. After that, the offline shared-vs-separate value-network test. It gets the useful part of AlphaGo's trick for very little cost, and it doesn't break anything we've built.

**What we lose today**

The current state inputs are already rich: retaliations left, waited, defending, spell effects with their turns, turn order. What the model forgets each decision is what it has **watched happen**:
- **The enemy hero's spells.** They're hidden from us (`h_spells` is all 0 for the enemy, as it should be), but every cast the enemy makes reveals one, and we drop it immediately. "They have Implosion" or "Armageddon" should change how we spread and commit our stacks.
- **Silence is information too.** Rounds where the enemy hero could cast and didn't suggest no useful offensive spell, or it's saving mana.
- **Intent.** Who has been attacking whom, which enemy units go for our shooters, who keeps waiting. Within a battle that's an opponent model, and it matters most against humans, who have habits.
- **Realised damage and losses per stack.** How much each unit has dealt, taken and lost, including creatures that resurrection could bring back. The engine knows a stack's starting count; the model only sees the current one.

**Why AlphaGo's history helped, and why not copy it literally**

Its last-8-positions input mostly gave the network "what just changed", plus repetition rules. Stacking 8 full copies of our ~94k-element input would multiply the input cost 8×, almost all of it repeated board. The deltas are what carry the information, and those are small.

**The design: three small additions to the input contract, filled in by the C++ AI from engine events it already receives**

1. **Seen knowledge, per side:**
   - which spells that hero has been seen casting, with counts;
   - how many hero casts so far;
   - how many rounds it could have cast and didn't.

   That's about 2 × 72 numbers.
2. **Per-unit history:** creatures at the start (so losses and resurrection potential), damage dealt, damage taken, kills, the unit it last attacked, and when it last acted. That's 28 × 6 numbers.
3. **An event log:** the last 16 actions of both sides, each one compact token: round, side, the acting unit, action type, target unit or hex, spell, damage, kills. In the model these become **extra tokens in the trunk's attention**, each linked to the actor's and target's unit tokens. That's how the model learns "this enemy unit is heading for my Marksmen".

**What it costs and how it fits**
- **Compute:** the inputs grow by under 1%. The trunk grows from ~218 to ~234 tokens, about +7% model compute.
- **Fair:** only what a human player sees during the battle. Hidden enemy spells stay hidden until they're cast.
- **Fits what we built:** each decision stays self-contained, so PPO batching, the stateless inference server and the records are unchanged. A recurrent memory would break all three: sequence training, hidden state carried through the server, harder PPO.
- **Search:** the simulator appends its simulated actions to the log, so search sees the same kind of inputs.
- **Engine side:** our AI already gets every needed event from the engine: each action of either side, attacks, damage, spell casts with caster and spell, moves, new rounds.
- **Later, for real games and the campaign AI:** keep each enemy hero's seen spells across battles in the same game, as a human remembers the hero who cast Armageddon on them last week. The same mechanism serves campaign play.

**How to know it helps**
- **Normal evals:** compared with the baseline.
- **A targeted eval set:** enemy heroes with mass-damage spells like Armageddon, Implosion or Chain Lightning, or Berserk. The check is whether our units change behaviour after the first cast is seen. A memoryless model can't.
- **Ablations:** check which part matters by masking it.

**Order**

It's a contract change (v15: new inputs, C++ encoder, model tokens, codegen), so I'd build and test it while the current runs finish. Then it gets its own run, compared with the baseline, after the λ run, so the two changes don't blur each other's results. The baseline is at ~1.5 of its 3 hours.

Should I build it?

[2026-10-26 15:57] AI: Past value estimates help less than they seem, and I wouldn't add them as plain inputs. There's one cheap form worth keeping as an option.

**Why not as plain inputs**
- **No new information.** A past estimate V(s₍t−1₎) is computed from the earlier position. With the seen-knowledge features and the event log in the inputs, the network can already work out everything V summarised. It's at most a convenient summary.
- **A feedback-loop risk.** If the value head gets its own previous estimate as input, the easiest thing to learn is "copy the last estimate, plus a little". That's a lazier critic that judges the position less on its own. It's worst exactly where it matters: the first decisions of a battle have no history, and late-game mistakes carry forward. AlphaGo-style systems feed back positions, not their own predictions, for this reason.
- **"We're losing, take risks" is already covered.** The policy and value share a trunk, so the current position's value-like features are already available to the policy in the same pass.

**Where past estimates are useful**
1. **As the training signal.** That's what bootstrapping is: judging each decision by how V changed after it, instead of only by the final outcome. It's exactly the λ experiment.
2. **As a per-event value change in the event log, as an option.** Each logged event can carry how much our estimate moved across it. That marks which events mattered, so attention can find "the enemy's last move cost us 30%" quickly. It's also an indirect hint about hidden strength: a hit that hurt more than expected suggests unseen skills or artifacts. It's one number per event, a small feedback risk, and easy to switch off for an ablation. I'd build it in, switched off by default, and test it with the history run.
3. **As diagnostics,** which we already do: retreats while the value head thinks we're winning use V, and large value drops after our own moves point straight at blunders.

**Run status**

The KL controller needed one more fix, committed: it now adapts on the last minibatch's KL, so an early-stopped update lowers the rate. The baseline `v15-base-kl` has restarted with it. | Decisions left | λ = 0.95 | λ = 0.8 |
|---|---|---|
| 5 | 77% | 33% |
| 12 | 54% | 7% |
| 25 | 28% | 0.4% |

So **λ = 0.95** means mostly the final outcome. **λ = 0.8** means mostly the value head's estimates, which is the bootstrapping you said sounds better. The two runs differ in nothing but this number.

[2026-10-26 16:22] USER: explain more about what λ is and who it works

[2026-10-26 16:22] AI: Let me check exactly how our code applies λ, so the explanation matches what training actually does:

[2026-10-26 16:23] TOOLS: Bash

[2026-10-26 16:23] AI: λ decides **how each decision gets credit**, when the only reward comes at the end of the battle.

**The problem it solves**

In a battle our side makes maybe 20–60 decisions, and the reward R arrives once, at the end. PPO needs a number for each decision saying "better or worse than expected": its advantage A. Two ways to get it:
- **Final outcome:** A = R − V(before the decision). That's honest, but it carries all the luck that came after the decision: dice, the opponent's choices, and our own later good or bad moves. In a battle we won, every decision looks good, including the blunders.
- **One step:** A = V(after) − V(before), how much the value head's estimate rose across this decision. It's low noise and it isolates the decision's own effect. But it's only as right as V: if V misjudges something, the policy learns V's mistake.

Between two of our decisions the opponent also acts and dice are rolled. To our side that's just the environment, and its effect shows up in V(after).

**How λ mixes them**

Each step's change is δₜ = V(sₜ₊₁) − V(sₜ), and for the last decision δ = R − V. The advantage is computed backwards from the end:

  **Aₜ = δₜ + λ · Aₜ₊₁**

So each decision gets its own one-step change, plus λ times the credit of the decision after it, which in turn includes λ times the next, and so on. A change k steps later counts with weight λᵏ:
- **λ = 1:** the weights never decay, and Aₜ is exactly R − V(sₜ), the final outcome.
- **λ = 0:** only the decision's own step.

**A worked example**

Our side makes 4 decisions; the value head says 0.10, 0.30, 0.20, 0.50 before each; we win with R = 1.0. The steps are δ = +0.2, −0.1, +0.3, +0.5. The second decision looks like a mistake: the estimate dropped from 0.30 to 0.20.

| | Decision 1 | **Decision 2** (estimate dropped) | Decision 3 | Decision 4 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| λ = 1 (final outcome) | +0.90 | **+0.70** | +0.80 | +0.50 |
| λ = 0.95 (old setting) | +0.80 | **+0.64** | +0.78 | +0.50 |
| λ = 0.8 (now) | +0.57 | **+0.46** | +0.70 | +0.50 |
| λ = 0 (one step) | +0.20 | **−0.10** | +0.30 | +0.50 |

At λ = 1 the apparent mistake gets a big reward (+0.70), only because we won anyway. At λ = 0 it's punished (−0.10). This toy battle has only 4 decisions, so the weights barely decay. Real battles have 20–60, and there it matters much more. For a decision 12 steps before the end, the final outcome's weight is 0.95¹² ≈ 54% but 0.8¹² ≈ 7%. Most of the later luck that used to drown the signal is gone.

**What it does not change**
- **The value head still learns from real outcomes.** Its target for V stays the final result, and the shorter-horizon heads have their round scores, so its errors keep getting corrected. λ only shapes the credit the policy gets.
- **The cost of lower λ is bias.** If V is systematically wrong in some situation, say it undervalues a spell, a low λ makes the policy follow that error until V learns better. That's why λ = 0 is rarely used, and why 0.8 rather than lower.

**Why it should help here**

Our value head explains ~0.3–0.45 of the outcome variance, so it's informative but imperfect. At 0.95 most of the credit was still dice and later moves. That noise is a large part of why learning was slow and evals jumped around. 0.8 trades a little bias for much less noise. Whether that's a net win is exactly what the deferred 0.95-vs-0.8 comparison will measure.

[2026-10-26 16:29] AI: On your first point, let me check exactly how the second term enters the advantage:

[2026-10-26 16:29] TOOLS: Bash

[2026-10-26 16:29] AI: You're right on the first point; I simplified. The policy's credit is the sum of two terms, and **λ only affects one of them**:

- **Final-reward term:** the GAE term on V. This is where λ applies.
- **Round term:** the score at the end of round r+2, minus V2's own prediction of it. That's real outcome, but only two rounds ahead, so it's already a short-horizon, lower-noise signal, and it's untouched by λ.

So we already get part of what a low λ gives, through the V2 term. Lowering λ makes the final-reward term rely on V as well.

**Why not one step, if V can improve on its own?**

V does learn from real outcomes whatever λ is. Its targets are actual results, and a separate value network would keep the policy's gradients from disturbing it. But with one step (λ = 0), **the policy can only learn what V already understands**:
- **Delayed payoffs:** a move whose payoff comes 3–4 decisions later, like a setup, a wait, positioning or a spell's effect, gets no credit until V has learned to value the in-between position.
- **Early training:** at the start V is poor (its explained variance is negative in the first updates). With pure one step, the policy would follow V's errors and likely sit flat, the case you called bad.

Higher λ lets real outcomes reach the policy directly while V is weak.

**Why 0.95 and 0.8**

0.95 is the standard default from the paper that introduced GAE, tuned on control tasks that discount future rewards by 1% per step. That's a different setting from ours, with no discount, short battles and one reward at the end, so it isn't sacred. 0.8 was a moderate first step, not a tuned value.

**Would 0.5 be stable, and better?**
- **Stable:** yes, in your sense. λ doesn't change how big the training steps are; the KL control and the 1e-4 cap handle that. λ changes the quality of the credit, not the step size.
- **Better:** unknown. At 0.5 the final-reward term looks only ~2 decisions ahead (25% weight two steps on, 6% four on). Its quality rides almost entirely on V:
  - **Now:** V explains ~0.3–0.45 of outcomes once trained, and less early on. I'd expect 0.5 to learn slower at the start, as V's errors are copied into the policy.
  - **Later:** possibly better, once V is good. The V2 term keeps some real outcome in either case.

**What I'd do instead of guessing**

Make the deferred comparison three-way: 0.95, 0.8 and 0.5, with equal runs and the same evals, judged by which keeps improving and doesn't go flat. A natural follow-up is a **schedule**: start high while V is weak, then lower λ as V's explained variance rises. That gets the low-noise credit only once V has earned it.

The current 11-hour run uses 0.8. Switching to 0.5 now means a restart, losing ~30 minutes. I'd rather leave it running and include 0.5 in the comparison. Your call.

[2026-10-26 16:33] USER: what is the standard for games like Go? which directions would you expect us to shift given we have more noise and more moves?

[2026-10-26 16:35] USER: what direction should we move this 0.95 given that we have more moves and more noise?

[2026-10-26 16:35] AI: **Down.** And there's a structural point about what λ should be counted in.

**Why down**
- **More noise means lower λ.** λ sets how far ahead the real outcome feeds a decision's credit. The further ahead, the more dice, opponent choices and later moves of our own are mixed in. Lowering λ cuts that variance, at the cost of trusting V more.
- **0.95 is effectively the whole battle for us.** Its horizon is roughly 1/(1−λ) = 20 decisions, and a battle is 20–60 of ours. So it's close to plain final-outcome credit, the noisiest option.
- **The right horizon is how long a move takes to show its effect.** In Heroes that's mostly within the current round and the enemy's reply, sometimes two rounds. A setup, a wait or a slow spell pays off within a round or two. One round is our ~5–8 unit turns plus the hero's cast. A horizon of ~5–10 decisions gives λ ≈ 0.8–0.9.
- **The V2 term already covers two rounds of real outcome.** So the final-reward term can afford to sit at the low end of that range. **~0.8 is a sound choice, maybe 0.7.** 0.5 has a horizon of about 2 decisions, shorter than a round: credit would stop before the effect of most moves shows, unless V already captures it.

**The structural problem: λ counts decisions, but the game moves in rounds**
- **With 7 stacks** we make ~7 decisions per round, so λ = 0.8 reaches about 1 round ahead.
- **With 1 stack** we make 1 decision per round, so the same λ reaches about 5 rounds ahead.

So today the effective horizon depends on army size, which is arbitrary. The better form is to **discount by rounds elapsed, not decisions**: each step's weight is λ_round raised to the rounds between the decision and that step. The horizon is then the same game time for every army, for example λ_round ≈ 0.5, which reaches roughly 1–2 rounds. It's a small change in the advantage code (the decisions' rounds are already in the records), and it fits the noise argument better than any single per-decision number.

My recommendation: keep 0.8 for the current run. In the comparison, replace the 0.5 arm with **per-round discounting**, which is the principled version of "lower λ for more noise".

[2026-10-26 16:36] USER: why do we even want exponential decay in our case? wouldn't a linear 50% loss from bootstraping and 50% loss from the end result be better?

[2026-10-26 16:37] AI: Exponential decay isn't required. Any weighted mix of "how did things look n steps later" is a valid credit signal, and exponential is just one choice. But a 50/50 mix of one step and final outcome is probably the worst of the reasonable options, for two reasons.

**1. It keeps half of the noise we're trying to remove.**
- **The mix:** the final-outcome part still carries all the luck of the rest of the battle, at weight 0.5. The noise is only halved.
- **λ = 0.8, 12 decisions from the end:** the final outcome gets 7% weight. That's ~7× less of that noise.

Most of the noise is in the distant part of the battle, and the mix doesn't reduce it with distance.

**2. It skips exactly the horizon where the signal is.**

A move's effect usually shows within the current round and the next: the enemy's reply, our follow-up, the spell taking hold. The 50/50 mix gives that range no direct weight:
- **One step** catches it only if V already understands it.
- **The final outcome** catches it buried under everything else.

So each part is weak exactly where the information is.

**Why something like exponential makes sense**

Look at what "n steps later" tells us about a decision as n grows:
- **The signal**, the decision's real effect, builds up over the first several steps, then stops growing.
- **The noise**, every later die roll and choice, keeps growing with n.

So the best weights put most weight on short-to-middle horizons and decrease steadily beyond. A decaying curve does that; exponential is the simplest one. The exact shape matters much less than **where the weight sits**.

**What fits your intuition better than 50/50**

Keep your idea of an explicit, readable mix, but aim the bootstrap part at the right horizon:

  **credit = 80–90% × [ V(the position ~1–2 rounds later) − V(now) ] + 10–20% × [ final outcome − V(now) ]**

- **The first part** covers the window where a move's effect shows, measured in game time, so the same for every army size.
- **The small final-outcome share** keeps real outcomes in the signal. That corrects V's blind spots over time and stops the policy from just copying V's mistakes.
- **It sits beside our existing V2 term,** which already adds two rounds of real score.

That's the same idea as per-round discounting, written as an explicit mix you can read and tune. It's a small change in the advantage code: the rounds of each decision are already in the records.

For the comparison I'd run: 0.95 (old), 0.8 (current), and this windowed mix. If you want your 50/50 version in as well, it's one more run; I expect it between 0.95 and 0.8.

- **Kept:** the V2 round term, as today.

**Verdict at ~2 hours: the window credit has stalled.** The evals have been flat since the 30-minute mark (−0.017, −0.020, −0.018), and the hero is unlearning spells. A likely mechanism:
- **The reward counts mana as an asset,** so a cast's cost is booked in full, at once.
- **Much of a spell's benefit comes later or indirectly:** a buff, a slow, a stack saved from dying three rounds on.
- **With 85% of the credit coming from V's view of the position just 2 rounds on,** that benefit only counts as far as V understands it.
- **V is still weak:** explained variance ≈ 0.0–0.2 at 2 hours. So "mana spent, benefit not visible" wins, and casting looks bad.

That's the bias risk of trusting V that I raised earlier. It's also a reason short windows suit Heroes worse than chess or Go, as you said: effects take a long time to show.

Options:
1. **Raise the outcome share** to about 50/50 until V's explained variance passes ~0.4, then lower it. Same linear form, more real outcome while V is weak.
2. **Lengthen the window** to 3–4 rounds, keeping 85/15.
3. **Go back to GAE λ = 0.8,** which never showed this, and compare later.

I'd do option 1, with the share tied to how good V is. It keeps your design and fixes the specific failure. The run is still going in the meantime. Which do you want?

[2026-10-27 00:39] AI: The idle-shooter metric counted all three: wait, defend and move, whenever shooting was legal at that decision. That was too broad, and I've fixed it:

- **Defend while blocked was never counted.** With an enemy adjacent, shooting isn't a legal action at all, so those decisions never entered the metric.
- **Waiting was over-counted.** A shooter waiting for the enemy to come within full range counted as a failure even when every target was beyond 10 hexes, where shots do half damage.

The metric now counts a wait, defend or move only when **a full-damage shot was available**: a legal target within 10 hexes, or a shooter with no distance penalty, such as Sharpshooters, for whom waiting gains nothing.

On the saved real battle records it gives 0.019 instead of 0.024 under the old definition, so some of the "idle" decisions were legitimate waits. There's a new test for the range rule, and all 132 tests pass. It's committed and pushed.

The running training keeps the old definition until its next start, so tonight's 0.04 → 0.09 rise in idle shooters partly includes legitimate waits. As agreed, I'm not restarting the run for a metric. The run is unaffected: it's training normally, with the next eval due shortly.

[2026-10-27 00:40] USER: what do you think is the best training schedule given what you know now? Is it likely to be the current one or are there clear improvements?

[2026-10-27 00:41] AI: The current setup is reasonable, but there are three clear improvements, each backed by tonight's data, plus several things that need a test before I'd call them.

**Clear improvements**

1. **Outcome share tied to how good V is.** The window credit trusts V for 85% of the signal.
   - **Evidence:** in the first ~2 hours V was poor, with explained variance below 0. The run went flat and the hero half-unlearned casting (casts 0.073 → 0.056, skipped casts when losing 0.23 → 0.49). Both recovered exactly as V's explained variance rose to ~0.2–0.4.
   - **Fix:** outcome share = 1 − explained variance, kept between 15% and 50%. Mostly real outcome while V is weak, sliding to your 85/15 once V is good. It removes the wasted first two hours without changing the design you chose.

2. **Drop the linear learning-rate decay and let the KL control do the lowering.** Right now the rate decays twice:
   - **The schedule** takes it toward 0.2× over the run.
   - **The KL control** trims it on top: it's at 4.0e-5 at 4.7 h, ~60% of where the schedule alone would be. By the end of the run it'll be ~1e-5 or lower, so learning will slow to a crawl in the last hours, the flat late curve you don't want.
   - **Fix:** a constant 1e-4 cap, which keeps your rule, with the KL control cutting it whenever updates get too big. The KL control guards stability, so the decay only adds slowdown. If anything, decay just in the last ~10% to settle the final model.

3. **Start future runs from this run's model.** Every fresh run spends its first ~2 hours re-learning basics with a useless V. The battle model's contract is fixed now, so new runs can start from the latest v15 checkpoint, with the same contract. Only a contract change forces a fresh start. This and #1 together remove the early dead zone.

**Likely helpful, but I'd test first**
- **More value learning.** V's explained variance is only ~0.35 at almost 5 hours, and with window credit V's quality limits everything. The offline shared-vs-separate value test, written and waiting, answers whether a separate value network helps. It takes minutes when the machine is free.
- **The window length:** 2 rounds versus 3–4, given your point that effects in Heroes come late.
- **The comparison you deferred:** λ / exponential decay against the window.

**Leave as is**
- **Update size:** 3200 decisions, minibatches of 256, 2 passes, KL target 0.015. KL has stayed at 0.006–0.008 all night and there have been no instabilities.
- **The rest:** the self-play league (85% current, 15% past versions), hard scenarios at 10%, and evals every 30 minutes.

My recommendation for the next run: #1, #2 and #3, run long, with no restarts unless it crashes. Tonight's run finishes as is, and its final checkpoint becomes the starting point. Should I build #1 and #2 now, so they're ready when it finishes? That's code only; the live run isn't touched.

[2026-10-27 00:45] USER: do you think the exponential decay would be better than the linear one? the difference between round and turn is very large and something that is 10 turns away can still be a part of this round, this is why I wanted the linear version, to not kill the signal by the time the post wait of a creature comes back

[2026-10-27 00:45] AI: No. I think your linear, round-based version is the better structure, for exactly the reason you give.

**Per-decision exponential is the wrong unit for Heroes.** GAE's λ decays per decision, but a round can hold anything from 1 to 10+ of our decisions: army size, waits that give a unit a second turn later in the round, hero casts. At λ = 0.8:
- **10 decisions later, often still the same round,** a consequence keeps only 0.8¹⁰ ≈ 11% of its weight.
- **A unit that waits and comes back** at the end of the round, the case you describe, is mostly cut off, while for a 1-stack army the same λ reaches several rounds.

So the signal depends on army size and waiting, not on game time. That's a flaw, not a tuning problem.

**What the window actually does.** It credits every event inside the window at full weight, with no decay at all, and only then hands over to V's judgement of the position at the window's end, plus the 15% real outcome. A wait and the unit's return, the enemy's reply and our follow-up all count fully, as long as they fall within the window. The window runs from our decision to our first decision two rounds later, so it always covers at least one full round, more for decisions early in a round.

**An exponential version could only be fair if it decayed per round,** not per decision. Then it's the same idea as the window with a softer edge: weights fading over rounds instead of a hard cut at round 2. I don't expect that to beat the window clearly, and the window is easier to read and reason about. I wouldn't spend a run on it.

**What I would test is the window's parameters, not its shape:**
1. **Length:** 2 rounds versus 3–4. That's the real open question, given that effects in Heroes often show late.
2. **Outcome share tied to V's quality,** from my last message, to remove the early flat phase.

So for the deferred comparison I'd drop exponential decay and compare window lengths instead. Your call.

[2026-10-27 02:41] AI: I went through the review, fixed what was real, pushed it (3263898), and resumed v15-window at update 860 (log `runtime/train/v15-window-r7.log`). It's training now, set to stop at 19 h of run time, around 14:00 today. Tests pass (132), a short smoke run was clean, and two evals since the resume worked.

**The review: mostly right**
- **#1 is the big one.** Since the resumes, league opponents played every unit defending, because their models were registered under the wrong names, and we trained on those battles. Fixed. A side whose model fails to load now aborts the battle instead of playing on.
- **#2:** the KL control now measures how far the update itself moved the policy, not the distance from the stale policy that played the battles.
- **#3:** generated actors wait when the buffer is full, the oldest battles are taken first, and dropped battles are now counted.
- **#4:** the idle-shooter metric is scored per unit and round, so waiting and then shooting no longer counts as a failure.
- **#5:** the wipe chance now models VCMI averaging up to 10 damage rolls, instead of one uniform roll.
- **#9/#12:** evals run on 12 persistent battle processes and take ~26 s vs BattleAI and ~65 s vs MMAI (it was ~90–108 s each). The eval model is checked against the trained one each time, each set's score has a standard error, castle went from 4 to 16 seeds and hard from 8 to 16.

**Where the other AI was wrong**
- It said the v14-11h run also had the league bug. It didn't: that run loaded models from files, not the server.
- It suggested judging the worst set by a lower confidence bound. That would single out the noisiest set even more. Bigger sets are the fix, so I grew them instead.

**Two bugs I found myself, both fixed**
- After a resume, league snapshot numbers fell behind the update count. "v750" was really update ~850. Takes effect at the next resume.
- On resume, `eval_MMAI.csv` never got its header upgraded, so newer rows didn't match it. I repaired the file; TensorBoard wasn't affected.

**Evals aren't fully repeatable, even on fresh processes.** BattleAI seeds its random choices from the clock. Fixing that means changing the engine, so I didn't.

**Run state**

| Update | TOTAL | vs BattleAI | vs MMAI | Worst set vs MMAI |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 853 (before the fixes) | +0.025 | +0.028 | +0.023 | |
| 955 | +0.034 | +0.041 | +0.028 | hard −0.050 |
| 1051 | +0.030 | +0.049 | +0.012 | hard −0.057 |

From 955 on, castle and hard are bigger sets, so they don't compare exactly with earlier rows. The weak spots vs MMAI are the hard set and castle (−0.025 at 1051).

- **Learning rate:** stays between 4.4e-5 and 1e-4, never above. About 13% of updates still stop early, because one 256-decision minibatch's estimate spikes even though the update's average (~0.006) is far below the stop level. That errs on the safe side, so I left it.
- **Waste:** ~17 battle records per update are thrown away. They're mostly surplus from the real games, which produce more than their 40% share. Wasted compute, not a bias.

**Deferred:** #6 memory, #7/#8 (bugs in the two uncommitted tools, never run), #10 siege moat and gate, #11 summoned units, #13 real-battle mana and gold, and weighting league opponents toward recent versions. The review doc and those two tools are still uncommitted.

I'm watching each eval and the training process until you're back.

**Per set at update 2106**

|---|---|---|

Against MMAI we're clearly ahead on three sets. Neutrals and hard are within noise of zero. Generated is still the strongest set by far, which fits your concern that it carries too much of the training.

**Training health:** the LR stays at 1e-4 (never above), the update's KL is ~0.005 against the 0.015 target, and ~19% of updates stop early on a noisy single-minibatch reading. That errs on the safe side.

**What happened overnight**
- **Review fixes:** committed and pushed in 3263898. Since then the league opponents actually play, and evals run faster and report each set's noise.
- **One crash, at 03:00:** an eval battle never ended. Our Sprites flew away from BattleAI's last two Gorgons every round, and BattleAI never retreats. Evals now skip a battle that fails twice (b3de8c8), and the run resumed from update 1240.

**Decisions for you**
1. **Endless battles:** when we defend and the attacker can't catch us, nothing ends the battle. Training just discards these battles, so the model is never penalized for stalling. The options are a stalemate rule for the defender too, or scoring timed-out battles instead of dropping them.
2. **Neutrals and hard vs MMAI:** these are the sets keeping us from winning everywhere. My suggestion is to shift training weight from generated toward neutral and hard battles.
3. **Untracked files:** `docs/adversarial_review_2026-10-27.md` and the two tools (#7/#8 bugs, never run). There's also a new `docs/campaign_ai.md` that I didn't create.

[2026-10-28 16:19] AI: # Review: campaign AI and battle AI changes

I read the whole uncommitted working tree and changed nothing. That covers `agents/AlphaMapAI/` (C++), `campaign_model/`, `game_values/`, the `battle_model` diff since b3de8c8, the new `battle_model/audit.py` and `gradient_study.py`, the `AlphaBattleAI.cpp` diff and the VCMI patches. All 211 tests pass.

## The five that matter most

1. **The campaign fight prior is far too pessimistic and blocks early fights entirely.** I computed it with `features.py`'s own functions, for a hero of level ≤3 with an army worth 10,000 gold and nothing behind the monster:

   | Our strength vs theirs | P(lose) in the prior | Expected return (gold) | Prior logit |
   |---|---|---|---|
   | 1.5× | 0.31 | −6,923 | −3.7 |
   | 2× | 0.20 | −4,500 | −3.35 |
   | 3× | 0.10 | −2,250 | −2.9 |
   | 10× | 0.01 | −223 | −1.4 |

   - P(lose) = 1 − σ(2·ln r) is far above what H3 battles show at these ratios.
   - It stacks on top of the separate expected-loss term, so danger is counted twice.
   - Wandering monsters are "nobody's", so destroying them gains nothing.
   - Φ prices levels 1–3 at 0, so early experience is worth 0 as well.
   - Result: every fight against neutrals is negative, even at 10:1. That fits joint1's finding that self-play never attacks and NK2 knocks us out.
   - Fix: make P(lose) ≈ 0 inside NK2's safe ratio (a sharpness of 6–8, or NK2's threshold), and drop the double count.

2. **Towns are under-valued and their defence under-estimated.**
   - `model.py:229`: town strength = log1p(garrison power). It ignores the fort level (walls, towers, moat), even though `t_fort` is an input, and it ignores the defending hero's skills.
   - `priors/object_values.csv:48`: the town prior is 4,000 and annotated "Φ: none". That is wrong: a captured town adds its buildings at cost (2,500 for the village hall alone, 10–30k for a developed town) plus its creatures for hire, and it takes the town from a rival.
   - The value audit never checks the town row, because it has no "gold" column.

3. **TRAVEL ignores danger, and doesn't use the exact travel days the encoder already computes.**
   - `Encoder.cpp:613–640`: each area's target is its nearest tile by days, with no check for a guard, monster, enemy hero or town. TRAVEL can walk into a fight that the MOVE prior would refuse.
   - `model.py:247` `travel_prior`:
     - it gives MONSTER areas 0;
     - BANK areas get the mean bank worth (~3.5k, Dragon Utopia included) with no guards counted;
     - an enemy town counts 4,000 whatever its garrison.
   - `features.py:291` estimates days as Chebyshev distance / (move_max/100). The encoder already holds the pathfinder's turns for every area's target; it could send them as an input of shape [HO, L, TG, TG].

4. **The gradient study doesn't show what the entropy-floor change (B1) claims, and the tool can no longer re-test it.**
   - A deterministic regularizer's minibatch gradients have coherence ≈ 1 by construction; the policy gradient's never do. Comparing coherences says nothing about which term is bigger.
   - |t| = 9–28 measures how consistent the push is, not how large. The mean effects (`shoot_adam_mean`) aren't reported.
   - v15-window trained 2,290 updates with the same 0.01 bonus and didn't drift.
   - `gradient_study.py:180` uses `PPOConfig().entropy_weight`, which is now 0.0. A rerun gives zero entropy gradients (NaN coherence) and never includes the floor term.
   - The floor may still be right. Proving it needs an ablation (bonus vs floor) from checkpoint 2840 on the same data, plus the mean effects.

5. **Two battle changes point the wrong way.**
   - **Defender stalemate rule** (`AlphaBattleAI.cpp:516`): a defending hero retreats after 35 quiet rounds even when it is far ahead. In last night's endless battle our side had killed everything but 2 Gorgons; under this rule that defender throws away a won army plus the hire cost.
   - **`hard_hopeless`** (`generator.py:429`): it claims the battle is won by retreating early. But a retreat scores max(0, hero + mana − 2,500) (`reward.py`), which is 0 for heroes worth under 2,500, while fighting on still kills enemy value. For week-1 heroes (levels 1–4) and much of week 2, fighting beats retreating, so ~20–30% of this kind teaches the opposite of its trick.

## A. Campaign model design (`campaign_model/`)

| # | Sev | Where | Problem | Consequence and fix |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A4 | Med | `Encoder.cpp:675` | RECRUIT always goes into the garrison first, the visiting hero only if the garrison has no slot. | Arming a hero takes RECRUIT + ARMY: two decisions, two prices, a credit chain to learn. Let the model choose the destination. |
| A5 | Med | `model.py:601` | Value heads start at a share of 0.5 in 2–4 player games, though the mean share is 1/n. | While trust = 0 the credit is pure Monte Carlo, so 4-player decisions get advantages shifted by ~−0.25. This is variance, not bias, but large early on. Start the bias at logit(1/g_players). |
| A6 | Med | labels | Labels end at d+7 and buying at list price is neutral. | Anything paying back after a week (high-level dwellings, mage guild, hero development) is invisible to the labels. The doc says "only the value head can carry that", but the heads are trained on d+2/5/7 shares only, so nothing carries it. Expect a bias toward income buildings over army infrastructure. This follows from your decision; I'm flagging the consequence. |
| A7 | Low-Med | `model.py:242` | The map-object gate checks only player visits, not per-hero visits (`m_visitors`). | Shrines, learning stones, trees of knowledge and arenas this hero already used still count in TRAVEL and map values. |
| A8 | Low | features | The exploration prior is 600 gold per sight of new land; Φ pays 1 gold a tile (~10–40 per sight). | The policy gradient keeps pushing against the fixed prior. Deliberate option value; worth watching. |
| A9 | Low | `model.py:668` | The question's object is hashed with `bucket(d_object, d_subtype, d_subtype)`; every other call uses `bucket(obj, obj, subtype)`. | Harmless today (separate tables). More important: 1,021 buckets for all (object, subtype) pairs means `object_subtype_delta` corrections are shared across unrelated objects. |
| A10 | Low | `model.py:151–154` | `subtype_rows` and `subtype_score` are persistent buffers whose shapes depend on the CSV files. | Any edit to `object_values.csv` or `bank_guards.csv` breaks loading every earlier campaign checkpoint, `init_from` included. |
| A11 | Low | `model.py` `object_gold` | A Python loop over the subtype rows. | Graph size and compute grow with the CSV. Use a gather from a lookup table. |
| A12 | Low | `features.empire_value` | In-graph Φ sees only the first 8 backpack artifacts and the tiles revealed now; the ledger counts every artifact and the union of ever-revealed tiles. | Small mismatch in the growth targets. |

## B. Campaign AI at runtime (`agents/AlphaMapAI/`)

| # | Sev | Where | Problem |
|---|---|---|---|
| B1 | Med | `AlphaMapAI.cpp:525` | `noEffect` flags `records.back()`. If the action raised a dialog during `execute()` (Town Portal's town choice, a yes/no, a level-up), the dialog's record is last: the wrong decision is flagged and the actual no-op is not. Keep the turn decision's record index and flag that. |
| B2 | Low | `AlphaMapAI.cpp:784–801` | The exchange runs in an async task and clears the turn's `futile` set (an unsynchronized `std::set`); the turn loses its no-op list. |
| B3 | Low | `AlphaMapAI.cpp` | No-op memory is keyed by (type, index) without the target tile: one failed Dimension Door blocks every Dimension Door of that hero until the state changes. |
| B4 | Low | `AlphaMapAI.cpp` | `activeHero` / `activeTarget` (a dialog's context) are written by the turn thread and read by dialog tasks with no lock. Dialogs our move didn't cause (start-of-turn events, a level-up after defending) get a stale hero and object. |
| B5 | Low | `showBlockingDialog` | A dialog with both a selection and a cancel offers no "cancel" option. |
| B6 | Low | `AlphaMapAI.cpp` `ledgerDay`, `Ledger.cpp` `explored` | Both are process-wide. They're correct only because every campaign game is a fresh client; in a persistent test-queue process they'd carry the previous game's explored tiles and skip its ledgers. |
| B7 | Low | encoder | Every decision redoes two full-map visibility scans, a new pathfinder, a full-map TRAVEL scan per hero and a full object sort, after every action and for every dialog (measured 35–105 ms a decision). Cache within a turn while nothing changed. |
| B8 | Low | tests | No C++ tests of masks against what `execute()` does; only the generated header is checked. Legality bugs surface only as no-effect rates at runtime. |

In v15-window r7/r8, ~19% of updates stopped early while the mean update KL was ~0.005 against a stop line of 0.0225. Use a running mean of the last few minibatches (both learners). |
| C2 | Low | `labels.py:65` | Any final snapshot dated after the last daily ledger marks the game as ended. An eliminated player's AI sends one; if the game is then killed by the time limit before the next daily ledger, every later label is frozen at that day's shares. |

[2026-10-28 17:44] AI: Only a change of model size needs a from-scratch retrain; everything else can keep checkpoint 2840. I changed no code, because the other agent is editing these same files.

## What forces from scratch
- **A schema version bump alone doesn't.** It only means re-exporting. The other agent's v16 change (`u_magic_resistance` and `u_magic_mirror`, used inside `spell_features`) adds no parameters, so 2840 loads as is.
- **A new or reshaped parameter breaks loading.** Both `init_from` (`train.py:805`) and the league snapshots (`inference.py:139`) load weights strictly. The way through is a short migration:
  - copy the old tensors;
  - set the new input columns or rows to zero;
  - start fresh Adam moments for the reshaped tensors;
  - apply it to `models/snapshot_*.pt` too, or league games can't load their opponents.

  Starting from scratch instead throws away 2,840 updates learned from a very weak training signal.
- **Model size is the only true from-scratch change.** The model has 324k parameters, of which the attention trunk is 67k (width 64, 2 layers). I wouldn't change it now: the gradient study showed the policy signal is at noise level, so capacity isn't the bottleneck.

## War machines: decision
**Ballista, ammo cart and first aid tent are worth half their price (1,250 / 500 / 375). The catapult and the arrow towers are worth 0.** This should be one constant in `game_values`, used by both the campaign's empire value (Φ, which has them at 0 today) and the battle model, with this comment.

Why half:
- Full price brings back joint1's hero spam. A hire was measured at ~3,600 of value with the catapult priced at 1,000. At half price a hire is worth less than its 2,500 cost.
- Zero makes a destroyed ballista free, and makes buying one a pure loss that only pays back weeks later, past the 7-day labels.
- War machines can't be sold, but replacing a destroyed one costs full price.

**Counting them doesn't break zero-sum.** The reward is what the enemy lost minus what we lost. A defeated side loses its war machines whether or not they were shot, exactly like its hero. So the final reward pays nothing for shooting the machines of an enemy you then defeat. It pays only when their owner would have escaped with them. XP stays out of the reward, per your 10-25 decision.

Engine rules to encode, all checked in `vcmi/`:
- A destroyed ballista, cart or tent is removed after the battle, whatever the outcome; the catapult never is (`BattleResultProcessor.cpp:72-87`).
- The winner never takes the loser's war machines: `commonWornSlots` has no war-machine slots.
- A hero that retreats or surrenders keeps the surviving ones.
- The battle ends without them: `battleIsFinished` skips arrow towers and war machines.
- They can't melee, don't retaliate and can't be healed, but they can be blocked.

## Checklist for the war-machine change (none of these needs a retrain)
1. **`rules.py:73`**: the features price the arrow tower at 1,000 and the catapult at 1; both should be 0, and the three machines get the half value. Today the features count them while the reward and the engine's predictions (`keptAfterBattle`) count 0.
2. **C++ `keptAfterBattle` / `remainingAfterBattle`** should return a worth instead of a yes/no. They feed `u_kept`, the attack predictions (`keptValue`, `AttackValues.cpp:28`), the cast predictions (`castValue`) and the per-round army lists. Generate the numbers from Python rather than copying them into C++.
3. **Start and end assets:**
   - `g_start_army` counts army slots only (`BattleSearch.cpp:83`); add the hero's war machines.
   - In `reward.assets_after`, RETREAT and SURRENDER keep the surviving machines, and the winner's captured artifacts never include them.
   - The start and end events read by `data.summarize` must list the machines.
   - `Reward.h` needs the same change for the search's end-of-battle values (covered by `test_search`).
4. **`features.side_strengths`** counts war machines' and towers' hit points as army health. The catapult alone has 1,000 HP, often more than a week-1 army. That skews the strength share, the value prior and the retreat prior in every early siege. Count their damage, not their health.
5. **`features.melee_reach`** treats war machines as melee threats to the hexes next to them, but they can't melee. The catapult (0 damage, 24 shots) counts as a shooter that can reach any hex.
6. **Eval baselines.** `train.py:576` reuses `eval_baseline*.csv` whenever the (side, seed) keys match, and `init_from` copies them (`train.py:814`). After any reward change, `vs_baseline` would compare new rewards with baselines scored under the old reward. Delete the files or key them by a hash of the reward rules.

## Changes that add parameters: do them together in one migration
1. **Mana price input**, per side (you planned this). The battle prices every mana point at 2 × spell power, while Φ only counts what 3 casts can spend (`capped_mana_value`). So for a hero with plenty of mana, the campaign treats casting as free and the battle model as expensive, and it casts too little in campaign battles. Randomise the price in generated battles; the campaign fills it in later.
2. **Each spell's mana cost.** Once mana has a price, a cast costs price × mana cost. The model sees its mana and each cast's predicted gain, but not what each spell costs.
3. **A stake input — your call.** This is what the winner gains besides the armies, such as the town in a siege. Your 10-25 rule keeps the reward local to the battle, so a besieged defender decides whether to fight on without seeing a 10-50k town. A stake of 0 reproduces today's reward, so the input costs nothing until the campaign fills it.
4. **Summoned units and clones** (review #11) are worth 0 in every gold feature: a clone of 50 Archangels shows 0 value destroyed. Add a feature that prices their threat.
5. **Spell-created obstacle kind.** No layer reads `x_obstacle_id`, so quicksand (stops movement), fire wall and land mines (damage) look alike. A 4-row embedding fixes it.
6. **Separate HEAL and CATAPULT heads.** HEAL reuses the shooting head, whose features are all zero for our own units. CATAPULT reuses the movement head, with movement features for a unit that can't move. Both are rare, so do these last.
7. **Tactics placement order** is chosen in C++ (most valuable unit first, `AlphaBattleAI.cpp:419`), which is a behaviour rule in C++. Also rare.

## No retrain needed, do any time
- **Siege reach** (review #10): `features.standable` treats only fully open hexes as walkable, so defenders can't path through their own gate. `walk_distances` doesn't stop at the moat or quicksand.
- **The other agent's resistance factor:**
  - Spells aimed at a hex still ignore resistance (their own note). The fix belongs in C++ `castValue`, because torch only sees the hex cast's total.
  - A chain spell's whole value is scaled by its first target's resistance.
  - `u_magic_resistance` on enemy units includes the enemy hero's Resistance skill, which the contract otherwise hides from us. Check whether a player can see it.

[2026-10-28 22:57] AI: You're right: the battle model has no per-turn exponential decay, and the λ = 0.8 claim was wrong for every run that matters.

- **What the code does:** since commit ca3ee7b (2026-10-26 15:43) the default credit is the round window. Each decision's credit is 85% of the change in the value head's prediction over the next 2 rounds (or up to the result, if the battle ends first) and 15% of (actual result − prediction now). The 15% rises to 50% while the value head predicts poorly. There's no discount (γ = 1). λ is read only by the unused `credit="gae"` option (`losses.py:178`).
- **What each run used**, from their `config.txt`:

  | Run | Credit |
  |---|---|
  | v15-base-lr2 | GAE, λ 0.95 |
  | v15-history | GAE, λ 0.8 (the only run that used it; 0.8 was the default for just 40 minutes) |
  | v15-window | round window |
  | joint1 | round window |

  So the checkpoints you're building on (2106 → 2840) never used λ.
- **"The campaign's trust mixing was copied from this"** is half right. The campaign's credit copies the window's structure: a short-term value-head estimate mixed with the real outcome, with the outcome's share falling as the value head gets better (battle 50% → 15%, campaign 100% → 25%). It copied nothing from λ.

The quoted sentence isn't in any repo file. It most likely came from my memory note `v15-history.md`, which still said "GAE lambda default 0.8, the 0.95-vs-0.8 comparison is still to be run", although the window had replaced GAE 40 minutes later.

[2026-10-29 07:47] AI: I think so, at least in part. The flat score and the drift have different causes, and the drift is the real problem. Everything below comes from joint2's logs, TensorBoard and checkpoints. I ran no battles and didn't touch the other thread's processes.

## Why the score stayed flat: those shots were worth almost nothing under our reward

- **The reward only counts what's left at the end.**
  - When you win, the enemy's whole army counts as lost whether you shot it or not. A shot only pays through the losses it prevents, and waiting, kiting and defending prevent losses too.
  - When you lose, only the damage you dealt counts.
- **The value head has learned exactly that.** The training log fits how V moves against our losses and our kills:
  - For the attacker (wins 70% of evals), V moves −0.43 per share of our army lost, but only +0.10 to +0.24 per share of the enemy's killed.
  - For the defender, who usually loses, it's the reverse.
  - Cyclops kiting and Archers defending are what "don't get hit" looks like.
- **The evals agree.**
  - 2840 (4% idle) and 3201 (31% idle) score the same: +0.077 and +0.078.
  - Army kept and army killed are unchanged on both sides.
  - On the same battles, two models' scores differ with an SE of ~0.005, so the skipped shots cost less than ~0.01.
- **I don't think something else got really good.** Nothing improved by more than the normal jitter between evals.
- **It has happened before.** In v14-11h, shooters went from 90% to 55% shooting "while the outcomes stayed the same" (the comment in `distributions.py:entropy`).

## Why it drifts: nothing holds shooting up, so any steady push wins

- **The training signal for shooting is noise.** The 2026-10-28 gradient study measured it at noise level, for the reason above. This is the third drift: v14-11h, joint1 (the entropy bonus), and now joint2.
- **The entropy floor (B1) may have moved the push rather than removed it.** Not verified yet.
  - The floor's weight is 10x the old bonus (0.1 vs 0.01), but it applies only to decisions sharper than 0.3 nats.
  - The sharpest decisions that aren't forced are probably confident single-target shots. So the floor's whole push would land on p(SHOOT): it pushes them down to about 0.95, and through shared weights on every shooting state.
  - The share of decisions below the floor rose from 4.9% to 8.5% during the run.
- **The other candidate is the value loss**, which pushes through the shared trunk. It was measured at t ≈ −5 on p(SHOOT) in joint1, and B1 didn't touch it.
  - A possible route, also unverified: SHOOT's type logit includes the best target's score (`best_of`). The target softmax never pins that score's level. So a trunk change can move p(SHOOT), and only the noisy type gradient pushes back.
- **The other thread's study will tell which.** Its gradient study already has both the `floor` and `value` terms, so the run on 3078 will settle it.

## The weight average (B3) makes the drift look worse than it is

- In all 5 evals, the averaged weights scored below the live ones on the same battles. Pooled: −0.0075 ± 0.0025.
- At 3315 the average idles 0.25 against BattleAI and 0.49 against MMAI; the live weights idle 0.15.
- So the real drift is 0.03 → 0.15, not → 0.49. B3 didn't work. I'd evaluate the live weights until we know why.

## What I'd check, then do

1. In the paired 3078/3201 run, split the idle turns by whether that side won. I expect them to cost about nothing where it won and something where it lost.
2. Count the decisions below the floor by action type. If most are SHOOT, set `entropy_floor_weight=0`; PPO's 20% clip already limits how fast a choice can sharpen.
3. If the value loss turns out to be the push, cut its gradient into the inputs the action heads read.
4. The root problem stays either way: the reward says nothing about these shots. If you want 0.99 shooting, something has to make them count:
   - a fixed prior on SHOOT when a full-damage shot is legal, using the same fixed-prior-plus-learned-correction pattern as the other priors;
   - or a lower-variance target, i.e. search.

   Otherwise the next steady push will move it again, in places where we have no metric to notice.

I added these findings to the plan memory, with the floor explanation marked as unverified.
